GUIDE PRATIOUE DU JARDINIER FRANCAIS OU TRAITÉ COMPLET D'HORTICULTURE contenant 1° TEUS LES DÉTAILS RELATIFS AU JARDIN POTAGER, AU VERGER, A LA PÉPINIÈRE, A LA GREFFE ET A LA TAILLE DES ARBRES FRUITIERS, ETC.; THE MAIN CEREAL, FORAGE, ECONOMIC AND MEDICINAL PLANTS; AZX PLANT DISEASES, HARMFUL INSECTS; 9° ALL DETAILS RELATING TO THE PARFERRE, THE VEGETABLE GARDEN; THE CULTIVATION OF ORNAMENTAL TREES, SHRUBS AND FLOWERS, ARRANGED IN === ALPHABETICAL ORDER; 3R A VOCABULARY OF GARDENING TERMS, BOTANICAL NOTIONS, ETC., ETC. BY H. DESMOULINS Vice-president of the Societés d'agriculture et d'horticulture du canton de l'Isle, Membre de l'Académie nationale, des Socie Tepifales d'horticulture de France, de Pontoise, de l'isle-Adam. Prix d'hanneur, ancdaille d'or, diplòme d'honneur aux Exposilions 1869-18 0 - 1873-1875 Medailles d'or et d'argent aux Expositions de Versailles, - Saint-Germaïn en Laye, Pontoise, Corbeil, Sceaux, Étampes, Isle-Adam, Montmerency. ... - TROISIÈME ÉDITION Revue, corrigée et augmentée PARIS LAPLACE, SANCHEZ ET Cie, ÉDITEURS RUE (CEGUIER, 3 Gathering together in one inexpensive, portable volume, neither too long nor too short, of easy reading for people who do not know even the first elements of horticulture; indicating the discoveries of our predecessors, simplifying them, making them intelligible to all, such is the twofold aim we set ourselves in publishing this Practical Guide to the French Gardener. The work is divided into two parts, each of which can be consulted separately. The first part deals with soil selection, fertilizers, plant propagation, plant diseases, insect pests, the cultivation of perennial vegetables, root vegetables, edible bulb vegetables, etc., etc., etc.; in short, everything that the market gardener and nursery gardener needs to know, and we have never demanded so much of them as we do today. For the sake of completeness, we've included a few more chapters on medicinal plants, economic plants and field plants, a subject rarely covered even in the largest and most expensive gardening treatises. The second part is devoted to flowers, ornamental trees and shrubs, greenhouses, landscape gardens and so on. The second part is devoted to ornamental trees and shrubs, greenhouses, landscape gardens, etc. It contains succinct descriptions and detailed cultivation procedures for around a thousand species of ornamental plants in the open ground, etc., etc.; very simple, very abbreviated notions of botany, which are nonetheless sufficient to explain the terms most commonly used in this science, which is graceful in itself, but so complicated and off-putting when bristling with barbaric or pedantic expressions. Drawing largely on the studies and discoveries of our predecessors, and, if we may say so, on the personal results of long experience, we have endeavored to make this second part the vade mecum, the indispensable guide for the French gardener. Numerous life-size figures are interspersed throughout the text. Tables by subject and alphabetical order help the reader to find the object of his research with as much certainty as promptitude; the man of the field as well as the city dweller, the novice gardener boy, the young child, will use this book without any difficulty, so simple is it in its general plan, clear in each of its chapters and paragraphs; we wanted to put it within everyone's reach, to make it, in a word, truly popular. May we succeed in inspiring others with our long-standing, but still passionate, love of beautiful and rare flowers, and our taste for the less noble, but more useful and positive products of the vegetable garden and orchard! FRENCH GARDENER PART ONE VEGETABLE GARDENER'S CALENDAR (1) January. - This month is devoted to clearing the ground, already begun in November and December; opening the pits, where Asparagus will be planted in March and April; transporting manure and fertilizers. If the weather is mild, you can remove the litter that covers Celery, Artichokes, etc.; sow small quantities of Marsh Beans and early Peas on sheltered, south-facing soil; towards the end of the month, sow Onions in light soil, but protect them from frost with litter. Renew the manures in your beds every two weeks, where you have already sown Cucumbers in December; renew the beds for Radishes, Raves and Salads; sow Gotte, Crêpe and other spring lettuces(1) See in the second part: Flower gardener's calendar, and the note at the bottom of the first page of this calendar. Romaine, Cresson alénois, Chicorée d'été; small white Radish from Holland, early purple and red ones, Carrots, Celery, Cauliflower; Melons, Cucumbers in small pots, from which you will remove the plant a fortnight later, to put it on a new bed. Sow tightly on a warm bed: Early beans, peas, broad beans; take from the ground and replant tightly, on a bed with panels: Tarragon, Sorrel, Parsley; sow York cabbage; continue to bed mushrooms in warm, barely lit greenhouses. Clean Cardoon, Celery, Cauliflower, Chicory, Carrots, Turnips and other roots still in greenhouses. Plant all tree species, except conifers, in dry soils, but postpone this operation until March or April, if your soils are cold and damp. If it doesn't freeze, start pruning the less vigorous apple and pear trees; prepare your stiles, trellis slats, etc.; prepare the branches and twigs for cuttings, which you will keep half-buried, by the large end, in fresh sand or light soil, sheltered from heavy frosts and the heat of the sun, until April, when they will be planted. You'll look after your pineapple greenhouse as you did the previous month, and if you have room for potted strawberries, they'll bear fruit from February onwards. The vegetable greenhouse offers: Cardoons, Cauliflower, Celery, Capuchin Beard, Curly Chicory, Carrots, Turnips, Beetroot, Potatoes, etc. Open ground provides : Leeks, Chives, Savoy cabbage, Brussels sprouts, Head cabbage, etc., Salsify, Scorzonera, Lamb's lettuce, Rapunzel, Parsley, etc. Layers yield : Cutting lettuce, Watercress, Tarragon, Parsley, Radish, Purslane, etc.; some small Asparagus. February. - Plough actively when the weather permits; sow in the first fortnight: Marsh beans, Early peas, Onions; in the second fortnight, on coasts: Carrots, Leeks, Spinach, Chervil, Sorrel, Parsnips, Burnet, Garlic, Shallots, etc., etc., etc. Reheat your layers; make new ones on which to transplant : Melons, Cucumbers, Lettuce, Romaine, Early cauliflower, Dwarf peas, Broad beans, Aubergines. Continue pruning apple, pear and grapevine trees; stock up on branches suitable for grafting; sow apple and pear seedlings, chestnut and horse chestnut seeds, etc. Clean your greenhouses and give them air whenever the weather permits. Vegetable greenhouses and open ground provide the same things as in January; Strawberries of the four seasons, forced under frames, give more abundant fruit. March. - Complete your ploughing; bury manure and fertilizer; sow Lettuce, Peas, Broad Beans and the other seeds indicated in the previous month; in addition: Bonne-Dame; plant Asparagus in strong, cold soil; sow in place or in nurseries. Bulbs and roots collected the previous year and which will bear seeds should be planted by hand: Beets, Turnips, Carrots, Onions, etc. Seedlings and plantings should be covered with a light mulch or a small layer of potting soil to protect them from sunburn and frost. On beds and in trays, plant the following plants: melons and cucumbers, fine Italian chicory, lettuces, cauliflowers, eggplants; sow lettuces and supplies, turnips, beans; plant asparagus stalks. Prune your espaliered fruit trees, except those with a lot of vigor and Peach trees; immediately tie up the branches; plow the base of the trees and spread a thick mulch. Sow pear and apple seedlings and tree and shrub seeds in the ground or in a terrine; you can also lay or butter Cognassier mothers and cuttings prepared in the previous month. Cuttings under cloches; tree stakes. The vegetable greenhouse now yields little more than: Beets, Carrots, Turnips, Potatoes; the open ground provides a little more abundantly: Wild Chicory, Chard, Chervil, Parsley, Sorrel, Spinach; the beds offer: Asparagus, Raves, Radishes, Cauliflower, Lettuce, etc. April. - As we no longer have to fear heavy frosts, sow abundantly in soft, light soil: Early turnips, Savoy and Brussels sprouts, Cardoon, Celery, Summer chicory, Beetroot, Cucumbers, Gherkins, etc. Ordinary layers are used only for Aubergines, Tomatoes, Melons and Cucumbers; muted layers for late-season Potatoes, Peppers and Melons. Nevertheless, for fear of the cold, water only in the morning and during the day. Prune vigorous fruit trees reserved until then, and Peach trees; remove useless buds. In the event of frost, cover espaliers exposed to direct sunlight with canvas or straw mats. Weed; destroy any caterpillar nests that may have escaped winter weeding. All grafting had to be done when the buds of the trees were elongating; some plants are cut under a bell and grafted on approach or otherwise. Boldly renew the air in your greenhouses, most of which can do without fire; take out the least delicate plants. Open ground and beds yield the same products as in the previous month, but in greater abundance: white onions, crambe, broccoli, asparagus, etc. May. - Redouble your efforts. In the first fortnight, plant large quantities of beans that you want to harvest dry, and sow from fortnight to fortnight those that will be eaten green; sow in the same way, but a little at a time: Broad Beans, Peas, Lettuce, Romaine, Chervil, Spinach, etc.; continue sowing from fortnight to fortnight.Continue sowing beets, carrots, cardoons, gherkins; large-ribbed cabbages, Brussels sprouts, turnips, early turnips, broccoli; put in bed-raised cardoons and celery, cabbages, gherkins, cucumbers, tomatoes, eggplants, etc. Mushroom stacks outdoors. Keep an eye on the branches of your fruit trees; immediately cut back fruit-bearing branches of Peach trees that have remained sterile onto replacement branches, to fortify the latter; destroy worms, bud cutters, etc. Stakes, ties; first hoeing. There is now an abundance of Asparagus, Broad Beans, Raves, Radishes, Celery for cutting, Chard, Pear, York Cabbage, etc., etc.; Artichokes, Strawberries, etc. The first flute and escutcheon grafts with growing eyes are made. June. - Same work as in May; make sure the garden is always stocked with in-season vegetables, and don't forget those that will produce later; sow for autumn: Cauliflower, Swede and Turnip cabbage, Carrots, Turnips, Broccoli, Celery, Scarole, Clamart peas, Beans, Black radish, etc. Weeding, hoeing, pinching and removing unnecessary buds. Grafting is carried out on all kinds of fruit trees. Great abundance of almost all the vegetables mentioned in the previous two months, plus: white celery, beef cabbage, white cabbage, tomatoes, etc.; strawberry, raspberry, some cherry and currant bushes; Myrobolan plum, petit muscatel and Amiré-Joannet pears. Frequent watering. July. - Continue with seedlings and plantings that will produce fruit in less than four months, such as: Beans to eat green, Peas and Broad beans, Black radishes, Autumn cauliflowers, Broccoli, Turnips, Salads, etc.; Chives for winter. Scarole and Chicory are blanched; Celery is milled every two weeks; onions intended for preservation have their stalks twisted; Garlic and Shallots are plucked as soon as the tops are dry. Trim; pinch; uncover fruit over-shadowed by leaves; disbud to form cattails; rake. Graft into buds with growing or dormant eyes. Destroy snails and earwigs. Abundance of all kinds of vegetables; Tetragonia replaces Spinach; there are already early potatoes. - Strawberries, melons, figs, apricots, blackcurrants, redcurrants, gooseberries; raspberries, cherries, guignes; early peaches; Tours plums, early Monsieur, early Royale, etc.; pears: Madeleine, Épargne, Muscat Robert, early Rousselet; apples: Calville. Frequent watering. August. - Harvest seeds for sowing; continue planting everything that will be eaten in the current year, or will overwinter, to give its produce the following year; sow Beans to eat green; Scarole, Chicory, Curly Lamb's lettuce, Tetragona, Spinach, Salsify, Scorsonera, Raves, Radishes, Turnips, Carrots, York cabbage, Beefheart cabbage. Cabbage, etc. New Strawberry plants need to be planted, and borders of Sorrel, Hyssop, Tarragon, Lavender, etc. need to be replanted. A little manure or guano put at the foot of the cauliflowers, which have already been stubbed out, activates their vegetation. Cut off the onion tops and trim the artichoke stems that no longer produce. Mushrooms in the open air. In August, grafting is carried out on well-arched wood. Fruit trees need to be trellised, ripe fruit carefully uncovered, disbudded and staked. Artichokes planted in spring are in full report. We have : Four-season strawberries, cherries, sour cherries, apricots, peaches, almonds, figs, green walnuts, Reine-Claude; Astracan apples, Passe-Pomme apples, summer Rambour apples; melons, etc., etc... Frequent watering. September. - Sow and plant everything that will be consumed before the frosts: Raves, Radishes, Salads; sow: 1° for autumn and winter: Chervil, Spinach, Turnips, Lamb's lettuce; 2° for the following year: Passion lettuce, York sprouts, cabbage, etc., which you'll take care to transplant on the coast or in a nursery and on the first layers of December. Stuff the Cardes-Poirées, to blanch them, and hoe the Celery. There is little to do in the orchard; nevertheless, the most vigorous branches of the Peach trees need to be pinched and trellised, fruits too well hidden by leaves need to be uncovered, the nursery needs a final weeding, subjects whose sap was too strong in the previous month need to be grafted, and the most beautiful bunches of Chasselas need to be protected with horsehair or paper bags against birds, flies and early frosts. We have lots of vegetables, lots of fruit: Strawberries of the four seasons, Northern cherries, Autumn figs; excellent Peaches: Madeleine de Courson, Bourdine, Brugnon, Admirable, Royale, Grosse violette; Grapes: Chasselas, Muscat; Plums: Reine-Claude violette, Prune de Jérusalem, Damas de septembre, Surpasse-Monsieur, Sainte-Catherine, Dame Aubert, Couetsche; Pears: Bon-Chrétien d'été, Bergamote d'été, Gros Rousselet, Doyenné, Beurré gris, Beurré d'Angleterre; Apples: Belle d'août, Reinette jaune hâtive. Watering should be moderate. October. - In the first fortnight, you will renew the Artichoke plant, which has already given you two harvests; you will sow Pancake Lettuce and Romaine, which will have to be replanted on beds in November and December; on the spot you should only sow, and only if the weather is favorable, Spinach, Chervil and Lamb's lettuce, the produce of which you will have in March. Transplant white onions, York cabbage and other cabbages sown in August; Passion lettuce. At the end of the month, cut the Asparagus stalks, smoke and plough around these plants; ridge them a little later and give them a cover against frosts; cut the late Artichoke uprights, and do a little ploughing around the stalks to wait for the November ridging; harvest potatoes, leaving their roots to dry out in the sun around mid-day; then put them in a healthy place, away from cold and damp; pull potatoes when their leaves wilt; tend and cover beans, and butter the last celery plants. Don't forget, either, to prepare your manure supplies; destroy the old beds, whose unconsumed manure will provide mulch or be buried as fertilizer; sow the lettuces you intend to use for your February-March coasts on 1. old beds or under cloches. Pick winter fruit on a dry day. Keep the greenhouse temperature at a suitable level. November. - In this month and the next two, transplant York sprouts, Beef hearts, Brussels sprouts and Cauliflowers (frame or straw mats for the latter). If frost threatens, pull up carrots, turnips, beet, black radish, chicory, scarole, cardoon, celery, etc., and bring them into your vegetable greenhouse; cover artichokes and other vegetables left in place with leaves or litter. Sow on the old beds, under cloches or frames: Lettuce, Romaine, Early Radishes, Cauliflowers, Cucumbers, etc., etc.; Four Seasons Strawberries will continue to bear fruit if you protect them with frames. You can already prune weak or old seed trees, and remove dead trees. Fig trees and other trees and shrubs that are too sensitive to the cold of our northern climate should be wrapped in straw. Vegetables from the previous month are still plentiful, as are fruits such as Strawberries of the Four Seasons, Chasselas, Crassane Pears, Martin-Sec, Duchesse d'Angoulême, Chaumontel, Sylvange, Beurré d'Aremberg, etc.; Reinette apples from Canada, etc. Watch greenhouse temperature. December. - This month, the gardener is mainly occupied with breaking up the soil, ploughing with a spade, and transporting manure. He should not forget to repair his tools, buy boxes and frames, clean the seeds, etc., etc. He will still be sowing Michaux Peas in the open ground; in the greenhouse, he will be transplanting: Lettuces gotte, Romaines; he will sow : Cucumbers, Melons; he will plant Asparagus on beds every fortnight. If the frosts are not too severe, the gardener will prune the less vigorous apple and pear trees. The pineapple greenhouse and the warm greenhouse should be kept at -10 and 20 degrees Celsius, with fresh air and careful watering. For the temperate greenhouse and orangery, it's enough if the thermometer doesn't fall below zero, and if the sun wants to produce - 5 to 10, it's welcome. Cover greenhouses at night. WEATHER PRONOSTICS Old proverbs, the fruit of experience, should not be disdained. When swallows frequently skim the surface of land and water, when crows caw from tree tops, when lamp oil fizzles and the wick foams, it's often a sign of rain. If the stars shine brightly in the depths of a clear sky, if the fog falls and promptly settles to the ground, the weather is likely to be fine. If the sun sets behind a fiery cloud, it means wind for the night; if it hides in a blue or red mass, fear rain or wind; if it rises or sets radiant and cloudless, rejoice: it's almost certain that you'll have fine days. Yellowish-white clouds bring hail with them. Meteorology (the part of general physics dealing with atmospheric phenomena, their causes and effects) is more precise than popular tradition; it tells us, for the climate of Paris, that: 1° Rain and fine weather follow each other at short, frequent intervals in spring; in summer, the atmosphere is less changeable; showers are very common in autumn, but long-lasting rain is less frequent than in spring. 2° The wind that prevails in the first month of each equinox generally prevails for six months of the year; this is why, in some countries, it is said that, if it rains on Palm Sunday, . = 2 "1" 2 -4. . - E. 7- WEATHER PREDICTIONS. it will rain at harvest time. In Paris, if the wind begins to blow from the south, it will generally turn in the same direction around the compass rose, i.e. it will blow west, then north, then east, then back south and so on. Southwest winds are generally warm, rainy and humid; northwest winds are generally cold, bringing clouds but rarely rain. The wet autumn and temperate winter herald a cold, dry spring. If summer is rainy, winter will be harsh. If the barometer falls, you can expect rain; if it rises slowly and steadily, it generally indicates fine weather; if the mercury falls very quickly, it heralds a violent wind and squalls whose end you will learn from the rain; hence the saying: "Little rain brings big wind". Amendments. - Irrigation. Even slightly mediocre soil should always be preferred, but it should be deep and therefore easily amenable to gardening improvements, rather than soil that is better on the surface, but shallow. If you have a choice between several sloping plots, take the one facing east or south, even if it's inferior in quality to the one facing north or west; first make sure you'll be able to get water by digging wells of ordinary depth. If the soil is calcareous, it will yield in the wild: several species of Speedwell, Gallium, Sainfoin, Barberry, Viorne, Clematis, Pulsatilla Anemone; - if it is clayey, it will yield: Rush, Argentine, Coltsfoot, Saponaria; - if it is siliceous: Arearia, Viper's Bugloss, Spergula, etc. Strong or clayey earth is always soft to the touch; fired, it produces a tight, consistent brick. Iron oxide gives the soil a yellow-red color; if there are too many metal oxides, vegetation will be thin. Taste indicates the presence of salt. If the soil contains a lot of sulfur, it will release its vapors when burned on a red iron. Fire can also be used to determine the quantity of decomposing plant or animal substances contained in the earth, which essentially constitute its richness. Does a lump of this soil, when subjected to red heat, give off a gravelly smell as it burns? You can conclude from this that it contained a lot of animal matter. Fertilizers are divided into plant fertilizers and animal fertilizers. To make the former, fresh plants of all kinds must be collected and buried before the open air has robbed them of their natural moisture. Of all the fertilizers based on animal substances, the strongest and most energetic is animal black, which is wrongly thought to be good only for large-scale cultivation; use it with care in cold, clayey soils, and it will give great vigor to crucifers and root vegetables without imparting a bad taste. Stable manure can replace all other manures; it is very good for building layers; it will ferment more or less depending on whether you keep it wet or dry. Less esteemed than the previous one, as it is less active, stable manure is suitable for gypsum, sandy and calcareous soils, which are too light and very easy to heat up. Sheepfold manure mixed with stable manure is very hot. Pigeon droppings, pig manure and liquid fertilizers should also be used, although the latter should only be used to hasten the decomposition of plant debris because of their stench. The best potting soil is obtained by fermenting horned beast excrement under the soil for a whole year; it will be black, even in all its parts, soft to the touch and odorless. Common loam is the product of the complete decomposition of manure. There is also simple vegetable loam, usually made from the leaves used to build the layers. Compost is the mixture of various soils and fertilizers in proportions most suited to the cultivation of a given species of plant. For example, orange-tree soil is free-range soil with the addition of a quarter or a third of leaf compost and fatty manure or good heather soil. Dummy heather soil is made up of a mixture of half silica sand and vegetable loam, or three parts black loam from decomposed gorse and one part pure sand. Manure water, or bouillon, is obtained as follows. In a pit a few meters wide, Om.80 to one meter deep, walled in at the sides and perforated at the bottom, pour rainwater through a bung onto a compact mass of cow and horse manure from the stable; after a fortnight, drain off the liquid part. This broth restores vigor and beauty to ailing plants, and greenness to orange trees; use sparingly. Mineral substances that do not nourish plants, but modify the soil to advantage, are called soil improvers: sand, lime, marl, plaster, added earth; add ashes and soot. For gardening, marl should be mixed with equal parts of good soil; it's a good idea to spread a little every four years in the vegetable garden, if the soil is not too chalky or too light. In the coldest soil, no more than 25 to 30 liters of lime per acre should be used for initial liming, and as a maintenance dose, no more than 12 to 15 liters for the same surface area. Soot destroys insects; it should be diluted in water and sprinkled with great care. Ashes and charrée (ashes that have already been used to pour lye) are suitable for plants that don't like greasy manures; deposit ashes in small quantities at the same time as the grains. Trrigation. - Rainwater, especially when it has been laden with a greater or lesser quantity of organic matter as it flows over the soil, is rightly considered the best water for irrigation. Natural irrigation consists in dividing the vegetable garden into narrow planks, with the soil raised on both edges and a little dug in the middle, forming a gutter to receive the water. Hand-watering tools include: ordinary watering cans, the flat-sided watering can, the English watering can; continuous-spray syringes and the ordinary hand pump, the Dietz pump, and so on. Plants with soft or hairy leaves, strawberries, lettuces, melons, etc., like to be watered in the rain; in general, plants need more water in the first period of their vegetation than when the seeds and fruit begin to form; you should water more frequently plants grown for their stems and leaves (vegetables, forage), than those grown for their flowers or fruit. In summer, water your plants in the morning and evening; in spring and autumn, in the morning only; if you water in full sun, make sure your water temperature is as high as that of the plants. Well water, which is generally very bad for watering, loses some of its crudeness if it is agitated a lot and for a long time in the air before use. Spring water is often too cold and needs to be exposed to the air before use; stagnant water too often breeds moss and turns lawns yellow. The best running waters are river waters. Gardening tools and utensils. - Shelters for conserving plants: Frames, Layers, Greenhouses, Orangeries, Planters, Apartment greenhouses, etc. The spade is the gardener's main, indispensable tool. Its weight and handle length must be appropriate for the person using it. It must also be proportionate to the thickness of the plant layer. For good ordinary soil, neither too strong nor too light, we recommend the common flat-iron spade, slightly narrower at the bottom edge than at the top. For hard, stony subsoil, we recommend the curved-edge spade; instead of a straight edge, the cutting edge describes a curve with two points at the ends. The Flemish spade, slightly curved towards the middle of its length, holds back light soil that would otherwise fall back crumbled and badly turned. The pickaxe is used to make holes, to uproot trees, and to break up compacted soil that is difficult to break up with the spade. If this is not enough, a pick is used, which differs from the pickaxe in the strength and length of its thick, narrow blade, and in its very penetrating point. The hoe works more superficially, but also more quickly than the spade; it is used for ploughing at the foot of trees or in nurseries, for mounding potatoes, etc. The ordinary hoe has a carved blade. The ordinary hoe has a square blade, slightly curved on the inside, with a short handle, which forces the worker to work bent over, moving straight ahead; when working with a spade, he walks backwards. To break up hard, dry soil, we use a hoe with a 41 Fig. 1 - Hoe. triangular blade. Potatoes and other root vegetables are dug up using a hoe with two flat teeth of varying length. After the spade, the hoe is the most widely used gardening tool. Its blade, much lighter and narrower than that of the hoe, is used to make holes for sowing beans, buttering certain plants, planting potatoes and so on. The second method is called hoeing. The serfouette differs from the hoe in that it has a narrower blade; the forked serfouette has two tines forming a fork on the opposite side to the blade; it is used to bury soil around small flowerbeds, to pull up bindweed, quackgrass roots, etc., etc., etc. The disbudder, either hand-held or attached to a stick of varying length, is used to cut buds and twigs from tree trunks; the Fig. 2. - Disbudders. three forms indicated above are the most advantageous. The weeder, whose use is indicated by its very name, is used to break the hardened crusts that form around plants during drought, and to remove weeds. The blunter is a kind of scraper used to clear the trunks and branches of fruit trees of parasitic plants such as mosses and lichens. The rake is also used to clear paths of weeds, by cutting the roots below the collar. The tracing tool is a wooden instrument used to mark out radii on the ground for planting and sowing. Imagine a kind of compass with a stationary branch in the middle, and two outer branches that can be moved on hinges, but whose spacing can be controlled at will by means of pegs and corresponding holes drilled in a curved piece of wood. Forks and rakes are well known to everyone; suffice it to say, with regard to the latter, that they must be of different sizes, with C A 2 Fig. 3. - Planters. tines more or less spaced, well forged and pointed, so as to adapt to all the ways in which the garden is laid out, and to the spacing of the plants. The planter is made from a piece of hard wood with one end rounded and slightly pointed; the other end, curved and forming a more or less obtuse angle, provides a convenient grip for the hand. The simplest is a small, trowel-shaped spade, which is used to gently remove the plant, which is then placed in the hole prepared for it; other transplanters have blades bent into half and three-quarter circles, and others form a complete cylinder. Wooden shovels must have a strip of sheet metal lining the bottom of the flat part; iron shovels with curved, slightly concave blades are also used. Scissors of different sizes should be made of good steel, with a stag-horn handle that retains its roughness and is blackened, and with a wide, protruding edge, so that the instrument remains firm in the hand; Rouen scissors have a well-deserved reputation. Among the main grafting tools we'll mention the ordinary grafting tool, whose handle is fitted at its base with a spatula-shaped piece of ivory used to lift the bark; the blade folds back like that of a knife. The grafting tool has a retracting blade or a sliding blade with a spatula; the metro-grafting tool uses a pressure screw to measure the depth of the graft; the grafting tool is used for slit grafting, the hazelnut grafting tool is used for Pontoise grafting; the grafting tool-shears is used for ring grafting. Pruning shears are available in a range of sizes; when using this instrument, care must be taken to turn the sharp blade away from, and not towards, the branch to be cut; the pruning shear is preferred for large branches. The sickle should have a sharp cutting edge and a very thick back. Saws should always be able to work between the branches of bushy trees; the rough work done by the saw is completed with the pruning shears. Crescents, loppers and pruners are used for pruning and trimming large trees; they must be attached Fig. 4. - Loppers. firmly to their handles, which are several meters long. The pruning shear is a scissor-shaped instrument fitted at the end of a long handle to reach the highest branches easily; it is set in motion by pulling one of the blades with a string; a sort of net, attached below, catches the cut part. Towards the end of winter, before the eggs have hatched, the weeding must be done. Shears are large, strong scissors, armed with two sturdy handles, used for pruning young shrubs, palisades of greenery, hedges and so on. The cast-iron, wooden or stone roller consists of a hollow or solid cylinder, fitted with a handle, which the gardener pushes in front of him; the hollow cylinder is made more or less heavy by loading its inner part to a greater or lesser extent. The claie is used to sift the soil, compost and compost needed for cultivation; clâies with iron rods are more ~ ~ ~ Fig. 5. - Echenilloir. solid and provide better service than clâies with wooden rods. The trowel, fairly well defined by its name, is used to place the soil in the pots, and to open the holes where the pots will be placed. The cueille-fruit or cueilloir, carried on a long handle, is used to pick, without damaging them, fruit placed beyond the reach of the arm. Shelters are used to protect plants from the cold, strong winds and excessive sunlight. Let's list the main types of shelter. An ados is an elevation of earth in the shape of the back of a hut, wider at the bottom than at the top, generally leaning against a wall at the top, facing south, with a slope of Om, 32 or so; ados provides early crops, and makes them grow faster than on horizontal ground: lettuces, Raves, four-season French peas, etc. Stone walls are the best of all shelters for fruit trees; white walls reflect the sun's heat, black walls absorb it; on the south side, they hasten the ripening of fruit and vegetables and protect frigid trees from the cold; on the north side, they defend plants in cold countries from too much sun. In the absence of stones, plank walls of roughly the same height (2.50 m) are built and, to make them durable, coated with tar. Two rows of Jerusalem artichokes, with their rapid growth and abundant foliage, make excellent windbreaks for cold and temperate greenhouse plants that spend the summer outdoors; for seedlings that fear drought, and so on. Movable canopies, made of planks or straw, placed over espaliers, protect them from late spring frosts; for the same purpose, mulch or canvas is spread in front of flowering trees; greenhouses and frames are covered with it to break the sun's rays in summer and to cushion the impact of hailstones. Mulch is a layer of short litter or half-consumed manure, used from March onwards to retain water from rainfall and watering; to smother the seeds of certain weeds; to prevent the soil from cracking, drying out and hardening; and to protect delicate plants from spring frosts. Glass cloches are laid flat on the ground when the plants they are designed to shelter can do without air. If the heat is too great, or if the nights are cold, these cloches are covered with a bit of long straw or heavy canvas; if the temperature is mild, the cloches can be raised a little on the south side. Verrines are glass compartments joined together by lead strips; they have one or two small windows on their facets, which can be opened at will to give air to the plants thus enclosed; verrines are very useful, but expensive. Cages are closed or open cylinders made of wicker, with pointed feet that are sunk into the ground. This type of shelter provides certain plants with the amount of light they can withstand during their early development, and protects them from animals. Counter-sunshades (against the sun) are halves of flower pots, cut lengthwise, which are placed in front of a young plant to shelter it from heat and wind; they are mainly used for cucumbers, gherkins, pumpkins and other cucurbits. The box is usually made of wood and varies in length from 1.30 to 4 meters, with a width of only one meter when it is to contain a warm layer; you can make it 1.30 meters wide for warm or dull layers. The back of the box is always higher than the front, to give the panel a greater or lesser slope. Air is supplied from behind or in front by lifting the panels with a rack, sliding them gently backwards, forwards or sideways. Overlapping panes of glass must be both very transparent and fairly thick. The box rests on the ground, and even sinks a few centimetres into it, when the artificial heat is produced by fermenting manure; if a thermosiphon is used, the box rests on a 2 wooden floor, under which the pipes of the appliance pass; an outer covering of hot manure hastens or forces the plants contained in the box; a simple bedding of dry leaves protects them from the cold. Each mobile frame is fitted with two iron handles. The fixed frame, 2.10 to 2.80 m high at the back and Om.70 at the front, is a kind of small greenhouse, in which we place, planted in pots, full-ground shrubs, simply to preserve them from the cold, or to force them: in the first case, an outer cover of dry leaves or slats is sufficient; in the second, a manure stove renewed every 15 or 20 days is needed; a deaf layer is established inside, where the pipes of a thermosiphon are laid under the boards supporting the soil. For the construction of mobile or fixed frames, oak wood is rightly preferred, tarred on the inside and oil-painted on the outside. On economical white wooden frames, the glass panes are replaced by linseed oil-coated canvas or paper. The layers are made of manure, either on its own or mixed with dead leaves, moss, etc., which can heat up and carry the heat it gives off for varying lengths of time. The layers do not feed the plants directly, but must be covered with a more or less thick bed of potting soil or garden soil, combined in various proportions. They need to be protected by natural or artificial shelter from northerly winds, and placed on dry rather than damp soil. For warm layers, we use horse manure in all its fermentation stage, fresh from the stable, well mixed and well watered. This manure has the advantage over all others of interrupting fermentation when dry, and resuming it as soon as it is watered. Recent manure stoves or belts maintain or restore heat to these layers, whose length is indeterminate, whose width varies from Om, 80 to 1m, 30, and whose height from Om, 80 to one meter. The same dimensions, or more or less, are observed for warm layers built with a mixture of horse manure, cow manure and leaves; for deaf layers built, if you like, with the same materials as the previous ones, and having the distinctive character of being established in trenches of Om, 30, and of being domed on top. You will notice, moreover, that the muted layers retain their heat for a long time, but cannot be renewed with the help of stoves; it is nevertheless a good idea to line the paths separating these layers with a thick layer of dry, hot manure. As a general rule, to build up a layer properly, you need to mix the manures well, spread them out in equal bunches, tamp them down, strike the entire surface with the back of the fork, and clearly determine the vertical edges. Some gardeners build up their layers in large manure beds superimposed lengthwise and widthwise; others prefer, with good reason, to build up one end of the layer immediately to the desired height, and continue in this way to the other end, working backwards. It's better to wet manure before placement than afterwards. In tarpaulins and hot greenhouses, layers are made with tannin. Mushroom beds, finished in the shape of a roof, are made in dark, draught-free cellars or storerooms, with donkey, horse or mule dung well soaked in the urine of these animals; these beds produce on their own; otherwise, they must be lined with fragments of mushroom spawn arranged in a chessboard at Om, 16 from each other and flush with the surface, or with very small pieces of young fresh mushrooms picked and laid in fresh water. They are usually built of masonry, with the floor lower by Om, 50 to Om,60 than the outside floor; they are high enough inside for a man of average height to be able to stand on them everywhere; they are heated by means of furnaces, the openings of which are arranged in such a way that the smoke cannot harm the pineapples, Cape bulbous plants, vines, heathers, etc., which they usually contain. The glazing, hinged to the back wall, lifts from the front throughout the summer, night and day, except in rainy weather; tarpaulins and mats are used to protect against excessive heat and frost; a thick layer of river sand or even clinker protects the bottom from insects. The orangery differs from the greenhouse proper in that it has no glazed roof, is built entirely of masonry, and has large windows on the south side; the plants receive light from one side only, so this type of building should not be too deep; the temperature in the orangery must never fall below zero in winter, and to obtain this result, a permanent heating appliance is not necessary, so a stove is all that's needed. The cold greenhouse, with one or two slopes and a flat or domed surface, is used to house plants that naturally suspend their growth in winter without losing their leaves, or that continue to live as they would in their native country where winter is unknown: Camellias, Pelargoniums, Calceolaria, Indian Azaleas, Magnolias, etc. As in the orangery, it's enough that it doesn't freeze in the cold greenhouse; the Dutch greenhouse and the Flemish greenhouse differ little from the ordinary cold greenhouse: covers and straw mats protect against frost and the excessive action of the sun's rays. The winter garden, which has become very fashionable in recent years, is a two-sided greenhouse with walkways and a pond. The temperate greenhouse is the most useful of all greenhouses; if it weren't so expensive to build, it would have long since replaced all the orangeries built at such great expense over the last two centuries. It is made with one or two sides, and is preferably heated by a thermosiphon. This device consists of a hermetically sealed boiler under which fire is lit at will; cast-iron or copper pipes start from the boiler and, after describing more or less long convolutions in the greenhouse, return to the boiler at a point on its lateral surface lower than their point of departure; in this way, ascending currents of hot water and descending currents of cold water are established until the temperature of the entire contents of the boiler is perfectly balanced. The boiler and furnace are usually placed outside the greenhouse in an insulated compartment, such as a cellar. Large, temperate, double-sided greenhouses are topped by a gallery designed to facilitate the maneuvering of canvas and mats. Hinged windows that can be opened and closed at will allow hot air to escape from inside, but cold air from outside is introduced with extreme care, through pipes that are pressed against the thermosiphon pipes and receive some of its heat. The water used to water plants grown in temperate greenhouses must be maintained at the greenhouse temperature of -6 to 20. At night, the temperature should not exceed -6 to 10 degrees, to allow the vegetation to cool down a little. The construction, heating and ventilation of the hot greenhouse are the same as for the temperate greenhouse; the heat is brought up to - 30 and 40 degrees centigrade; the atmosphere should be as dry as possible and maintained at - 15 during the resting period; humidity is needed during the vegetation period, with frequent watering of the soil and foliage. Leorchids and aroids require a lot of humidity. In order 2. to provide each series of plants with the right temperature, the hot greenhouse is often divided by glass partitions, and the inside temperature of each compartment is varied as desired. The forcing greenhouse, designed to hasten the ripening of grapes, peaches and the flowering of ornamental plants, etc., is a temperate or hot greenhouse, more or less narrow and more or less long. The propagation greenhouse is a cold greenhouse, usually facing north or sunset, to protect greenhouse plants propagated by grafting or cuttings from the direct action of the sun's rays. We call mobile greenhouses long panels of glazed frames, leaning against an espalier wall, more or less inclined, heated by a stove with large pipes, or by a thermosiphon, finally well caulked. These types of greenhouse can be used to force Fig, Peach and other espaliered fruit trees to grow. The apartment greenhouse is a miniature greenhouse with a water tank underneath, heated by a spirit-of-wine or ordinary oil lamp. The aquarium is a kind of greenhouse, generally vaulted, and almost entirely occupied by a basin full of water maintained at the temperature required by beautiful tropical plants, such as Victoria regia, Nelumbo, etc., etc.; a flowerbed is laid out around it for foreign ornamental plants, eager for an atmosphere that is both warm and humid. The apartment aquarium is a device supported by copper columns, through one of which passes a lead pipe designed to carry the water, while another of these columns allows the overflow from the basin to escape through a small hole, creating a continuous flow of liquid. This kind of aquarium is a little expensive; here are two others, much simpler and moreover very sufficient for raising the plants in our streams and ponds -- Figs. 6 and 7. - Apartment aquariums. If the aquarium is supplied with fresh water, you can grow sticklebacks, very interesting fish to study, and pond shells, etc., in the midst of many aquatic plants; if it is supplied with seawater, with a bottom lined with pebbles, flat stones, rockeries, etc., you can have, in addition to the algae, a wide variety of other plants, such as the mollusks, the mosses and the mosses, In addition to seaweed, zostera, kelp, etc., you can also have seahorses (horsehead fish) and other small fish with curious habits, shellfish, etc.; we recommend placing these kinds of aquariums near well-lit windows. Floral hanging baskets, a kind of lamp-post suspended from the ceiling, are used to grow a number of hardy orchids in peat soil mixed with a little heather and covered with a bed of damp moss: Burlingtonia candida, with translucent white flowers and a yellow band; Zigopetalum, with green flowers marbled with brown; Stanthopea, with tiger flowers; Oncidum butterfly, so named because its shape is reminiscent of this insect; OErides; and still other plants: Achimenes, with long blue flowers and a white center; Flagelliform Cactus, with numerous superb red flowers; Serpentine Cactus, with pinkish-blue flowers and cylindrical stems reminiscent of a snake's coils; the hybrid Petunia with violet flowers; the Saxifrage sarmenteuse with pink flowers mixed with white and yellow; the Torénie asiatique and the Torénie magnifique; Capillaries, Sedums, Pelargonium with ivy leaves and pink flowers, white or scarlet Crassules, etc..., etc. In window boxes well filled with light but substantial soil, with a bed of fine pebbles, on a zinc base, you can easily grow the little daisy, the cute Saxifrage; even more often, you can place ready-made plants, bought in pots, such as; Camellias, Crocus, Bulbocode, Perce-Neige, Jacinthe, Hellébore (Christmas Rose), Helléborine, Violette: JANUARYFEBRUARY. - Dielytra remarquable, Adonide, Saxifrage de Sibérie, Hôteia du Japon, Tritélée uniflore, Ficoïde tricolore, Narcisse de Constantinople, Fritillaire méléagre: MARCH. - Auriculas, Scillas, Muscaris odorants; varieties of Narcissus; Silenus, Epimedium, Double daisies in APRIL. - Anemone varieties, Eucharidium, Gilia, Giroselle de Virginie, Oxalides, Némophiles, Adonide d'été, Lychnides, Œillet mignardise, Œillet d'Espagne, Saxifrage ombreuse, Renoncule des fleuristes, Glaïeuls hâtifs, etc., in MAY. - Cupidone bleue, Ephémère de Virginie, Amaranthe, numerous Pelargoniums, Érine des Alpes, Bermudienne, Calandrine, Chrysanthème caréné, Cinéraire maritime, Amaryllis, Lis, Campanulas, Lobélies, Œnothères, etc., etc., in JuiN. - Madagascar periwinkle, some small Phlox, Achimenes, Lupins, Crassule, Myrthe, Heather, Lantanas, dwarf orange trees, etc., etc., etc. But if you want your plants to remain vigorous, you need to expose them to as much light as possible, give them air, even in winter, as soon as the atmosphere outside is warm enough; remove dust from their leaves using a feather duster, a wet cloth or a watering can with very fine holes; for this watering, which should be done in the evening rather than the morning, you should use rainwater or river water in preference to any other water, and after cooling it down a little. Plants that are fat or have tough, glossy leaves can go a long time without water; plants with tender foliage suffer and die quickly from drought: 1° place them so that the base of the bulb rests on the rim of the decanter, and so that the tray and the roots emerging from it are completely immersed in the water; 2° give them air and light, without exposing them to too much heat; 3° change all or part of the water from time to time, but keep the decanter quite full; you can throw in a few grains of cooking salt to prevent the liquid from corrupting. When the roots are well developed and the buds are showing, the need for bright light diminishes and the onions can be placed back on the mantelpiece or on the furniture inside the apartment. This is how we care for Ornithogales, Amaryllis, Crocus, Hyacinths, Narcissus, Italian and Siberian Scillas, etc., etc., etc. Plants that require moderate heat and fear frost can be raised on double-framed windows; window greenhouses can be heated by hot-water basins with pipes, small thermosiphons, etc., etc. In good exposure, in the open air, often on balconies: Sweet peas, Nasturtiums, Ivy, Reseda, etc., etc. (1). (1) Flowers should never be kept in the bedroom at night. Means of plant propagation. - Sowing - Budding and Marcottage. - Cutting. - Grafting. - Crossbreeding. - Hybridization. The main aim of all horticulture is the multipli. cation of plants. Plants multiply by seed in their primitive, natural state; but we need to resort to artificial means in our cultivation. At the same time as sowing is the surest way of obtaining robust, fast-growing individuals, it also produces very curious varieties; roots, stems and branches perpetuate plants without alteration. Take well-ripened seeds, which you can tell by weight, color and inspection of the internal organs, which must be complete; not too old, with no wrinkles or alterations. The best way to preserve seeds is to enclose them in canvas bags of varying thickness, once they have been cleaned of all moisture. Large seeds with hard bark can be sown deeply; a thin layer of soil or pulverized compost is enough to cover the other fine seeds; the smallest seeds will even germinate without being buried; seeds with bumps, membranes or hairs should be rubbed between the hands for a few moments and mixed with ash or dust, to prevent them from attaching to each other. We hasten the germination of stone seeds (cherry, plum) and seeds with very hard skins by stratification, i.e. we hold these seeds for varying lengths of time in pots filled with soil or sand moist enough to ferment due to the action of the soil's heat, into which we push them at Om, 30 or Om, 40, at midday exposure, after which, the germ having developed, we sow in rows or broadcast. Seeds produce more hairs in soft, light soil than in heavy soil. Broadcast sowing. - This involves spreading the seeds as evenly as possible by throwing them with the hand from a distance. - Sowing in packets or pots. Holes are made at a greater or lesser distance depending on the species of plant, which is first surrounded with some of the soil removed, and then lightly tilled. - Sowing in terrines and pots. If the plants are very water-loving, there's no need to pierce the pots, which should be immersed up to a quarter of their height in the liquid, which gradually seeps through the walls, moistening the young roots sufficiently. Pierced pots or terrines should be lined with coarse sand to facilitate water drainage. - Ray sowing. Draw the rays with a chalk line, give them a depth of about Om, 03 to Om, 06; sow and cover the seed with the soil first raised. - Sowing in nurseries. Pips and seeds are broadcast and should be buried Om, 03 deep; pits are placed one by one, at a suitable distance, in Om, 06 holes. Mulch or leaves permanently against frost until spring. - Sowing on a bed. All seeds too delicate to be planted in the ground are planted in a bed or bell. Transplanting is any transplanting that is not permanent. Tubers are roots, underground stems or branches of underground stems of various shapes; separated and placed in the ground, they form new plants: Dahlia, Cyclamen, Potato, Jerusalem artichoke, etc. Each tuber eye produces true roots, stems, complete individuals in a word. If you cut large tubers into as many parts as there are eyes, each of these parts will produce a new plant. The stalks and bulblets should not be detached until they are ripe, which you can see by the complete desiccation of the leaves in whose axils these little bodies are born. Carnations are the rooted shoots that appear at the collar or on the roots of certain plants. Plants with perennial roots produce tufts of varying thickness, with seeds, buds or turions, which are broken off in autumn and towards the end of winter to produce new individuals. Strawberries and other plants have runners or stolons, which are spindly stems with buds at their joints; these buds, separated and placed in the ground, become new plants. Layering consists in burying a portion of a plant, one end of which protrudes outwards, while the other adheres to the mother plant, which is responsible for nourishing the cutting until it takes root and lives on of its own accord. A simple cutting is said to exist when a branch firmly fixed in this position by wooden hooks and covered with soil is placed in the ground at a depth of about Om, 08, 0, m10; marcotte 3 is said to be a serpentine marcotte when the top of the branch, instead of remaining freely erect out of the ground, is bent back in order to force it to give other marcottes; in marcotte by strangulation, the bark of the buried branch is tightened by means of an iron or linen wire, near and below an eye; in marcotte by circumcision, a circular strip of bark is removed below an eye, so as to maintain the descending sap at the base of the upper lip, and create a bead from which the roots grow; in marcotte by twisting, a branch with thin, fibrous bark is twisted at the point where it is to produce roots; in the simple incision method, the branch is split down the middle, and the separated parts are kept apart by placing a stone or other hard body between them; in the heel incision method, the branch is first split horizontally down the middle, then vertically upwards; in the complicated incision method, two perpendicular cuts are made on the horizontal slit made as above, and kept apart by placing small stones between them; in the amputation method, the cut piece is completely removed, as in the heel incision; finally, in the cepée method, after cutting a tree or shrub flush with the ground, the stumps are covered with soil; in this state, the stumps produce several shoots, which are removed once they have taken root. Annual shoots are often too high to be laid down or brought to ground level, or the wood is too brittle to lend itself to bending. In this case, after staking the branch, it is placed in a basket or pot with a deep indentation, in funnel-shaped glass vases, or in lead or zinc cups lined with soil; the soil is watered; the cutting is separated when it has taken root. Cuttings are young, fresh shoots that are placed in the ground at the cut end to take root. This operation is based on the inherent property of adventitious buds and all latent plant germs to convert into branches or roots, depending on the diversity of the environment in which they are found. In many cases, cuttings are an advantageous substitute for propagation by seed. Few plants are unwilling to undergo this operation. For cuttings to be successful, there must be an abundance of cellular tissue to prompt root development at the buried end of the shoot, and the soil must be as moist and warm as possible; it is important to prevent transpiration of the shoots and evaporation of moisture from the soil. Moist moss and heather soil encourage root development. Simple cuttings. - For very easy-to-grow trees: plane trees, poplars, willows and especially waterside plants, in December or March, cut off young branches two to six years old, prune them, leaving some of their shoots, and push them into deep holes, three to five decimeters square, filled with light soil and pressed in all directions. These branches will cover with leaves in the same season. For amenity trees that are difficult to regrow, in March, cut young shoots from the previous year below a node, divide them into sections of five or six buds, and bind them for a month up to a quarter of their length in fresh sand, sheltered from wind and frost; at the beginning of April, cut each section with a planting knife, in well-ploughed ground, facing north or east, leaving two or three eyes above the ground; water with gentle rain; mulch and keep cool at all times. Species which do not lend themselves to the two above-mentioned procedures can be cut with a bead or heel. As early as June of MULTIPLICATION OF PLANTS. the previous year, a circular ring is made on the young cutting, or a strong ligature is made with wire, so as to create a bead of abundant cellular tissue on this point; or, finally, a branch is broken off from the trunk, pulling up and down so as to remove with it the impasto naturally formed at its junction, and is planted. This procedure is detrimental to mother plants, and should only be used with caution. For Vines, Currants, many Roses, etc., cuttings are made from two-year-old wood or crossette: a fairly long piece of a two- or three-year-old branch is removed with the cutting; the old branch is pruned and placed in heather soil, to a depth of about Om, 12; finally, it is watered and covered with mulch. Orangery and greenhouse plants; herbaceous plants, Dahlias, etc. are generally cut under a bell. Here's how it's done: In terrines or deep pots of Om, 1 or Om, 2 lined with heather soil and potting compost, with a bed of sand, you will prick with an unsharp planter, the cuttings leafy in May and June, leaving them only the top and two leaves, after having well packed the soil, you will water; you will let it dry out in the shade; you will put the whole on a covered bed or in a warm place, in the shade. The air must be renewed with care and precaution; you must be sparing with watering, removing the vapors that settle on the inside walls of the bell and the moulds that often form around the young plants. We have always found the months of May and June to be particularly favorable for bell cuttings. Orange trees can even be propagated by taking cuttings from their leaves, while lilies, etc., can be propagated by taking cuttings from their scales. The aim of grafting is to make the branch or bud of one plant grow on another. For the operation to succeed, two main conditions must be met: 1° coincidence in the development of the branch removed, called the graft, and in the development of the plant on which it is placed, called the subject; 2° identity, if not of species and genus, at least of family and certain relationships between individuals. Let's take a look at the most frequently used grafts. Grafting by approach consists in strongly binding together two branches of neighbouring trees, after having first cut them to a quarter of their thickness. The wounds are protected from light, air and water, using Saint Fiacre's ointment or grafting wax. Most of the twigs above and below the graft are removed; little by little, the stem is incised, waiting to be cut until the welding is complete, which takes place in the course of the year. This grafting process gives new vigor to old espalier trees. To this end, two wildings of the same species are planted next to the exhausted tree; they are bent crosswise, or more or less, on the trunk of the first, a little above the other; suitable notches are made to graft them by approach; the following year, once the weld has been made, the two wildings are decapitated, their sole task being to provide the old tree with fresh, strong sap, to make it fertile, as in the days of its youth. Approach grafting can be used to form espaliers, hedges and fences. It is especially useful for delicate plants, at a time when the sap is moving. Herbaceous grafting is a type of approach grafting, with the difference that instead of operating on woody branches at least one year old, it is performed on herbaceous or barely woody plants, mainly on the terminal shoot of coniferous Fig. 9. Slot grafting. trees during the height of their vegetation. After cutting away the terminal shoot with a triangular section, the bevelled scion is placed in its place. Ligation must be done with care and skill; the lack of consistency of this type of graft makes it much more likely than the previous one to be strangled if it is too tight. Slot grafting, the most commonly used method for fruit trees, consists in cutting the tree or branches to be grafted horizontally at the base; on the flat part thus cut, or tray, a slot is made in the middle from Om, 03 to Om, 04 with a serpette and a hammer; the graft or scion (a well-aruged branch from the previous year) is taken. Fitted with 2, 3 or 4 eyes, this graft is beveled from the large end on both sides, and inserted into the slot so that the bark is at the same level and perfectly aligned. Two, three or four scions can be inserted if the tray is wide enough; then, without shaking the scions, the small wooden wedges holding the slits open are removed, tightened with woolen thread, and the wounds healed with grafting wax. For this operation to be successful, it is important that the subject is in good health, and that the scions have not yet grown. They can be cut in advance and kept fresh by burying them in the north; they can be grafted in the first spring, or in September (sleeping-eye grafting). In the latter case, the graft and subject will begin their winter sleep at the same time, and will be revived together in the spring. This does not destroy the general rule for scion grafting, which is as follows: the subject must be in sap and the graft must be about to enter it. Crown grafting is performed on subjects too large to be split. After horizontal amputation, a wedge of very hard wood is used to insert the scions, beveled on one side to a length of Om.04 to Om.05, between the sapwood and the bark, with the bark separated from the sapwood. A tie is placed around the scion, and grafting wax is applied. In this way, there are no cracks on the surface of the cut, which remains perfectly intact; such is the character of crown grafting. In this way, a large-diameter branch can receive ten or more scions. Flute or whistle grafting consists in amputating a branch at an eye, at a very smooth point, splitting the bark without damaging the wood, into two or three strips of Om, 06 in length; a ring of bark with its buds is removed from the graft, which should be as large as possible; it is threaded onto the sapwood of the subject, and the strips are raised without covering the eyes. If the subject's diameter is greater than that of the flute, the ring is trimmed to fit the designated place, and covered with one of the loose, falling strips; if the diameter is smaller, a strip is removed with a whistle, and the edges joined; grafting wax over the slits. This type of grafting is used mainly for walnut and chestnut trees. Grafting with a growing eye is done in May-June; grafting with a dormant eye is done in April and September; their distinctive feature is that they do not introduce any part of the grafted wood onto the subject. Here's how it's done: 1° After detaching a well-formed bud from the branch you wish to graft, you remove the leaf, but keep the part of the petiole adhering to the eye, which you remove with its bark, squarely above, pointed below, in the shape of an escutcheon, hence the name of this type of graft; 2° At the point reserved for the escutcheon, make an incision in the shape of a straight T or an upside-down ], penetrating all the way into the wood; using the spatula of your grafting tool, pull the two lips of the bark apart upwards; 3° Slide Fig. 10 - Escutcheon. Fig. 11. - Grafting the escutcheon. between the bark and the wood in the incision you've made on the subject, determining the most exact coincidence. Then bind the lips of the bark together to secure the bud, but without covering it. Don't cut the stem or branches just as you're grafting; you'll create an untimely shoot that's exposed to a lot of bad luck; so wait a few days, until it's perfectly fused; then pinch the end of your scions; cut halfway through; then retrench. If you have grafted with a dormant eye in autumn, postpone this part of the operation until the following spring. Loosen ligatures if they cause constrictions, and carefully remove any eyes that develop at the same time as the bud. English-style grafting or grafting by copulation can only be applied to subjects of exactly the same diameter as the scions; the top of the former and the bottom of the latter are cut at an angle so that all their parts overlap and the edges of the two barks coincide at all points. Ligature; grafting wax. Pontoise grafting, particularly used for ornamental trees and shrubs that retain their leaves in winter, is performed as follows: when the winter sleep is over and the sap begins to run again, you cut a triangular slit in the side of the subject, into which you insert the bottom of your wedge-cut graft, then bind it; but you must keep the shrubs thus grafted under a frame for two weeks or so; otherwise, the scion used, being loaded with leaves and often even flowers or flower buds, will perish through evaporation. As faun grafting is only a minor variety of flute grafting, we won't go into it here. Remember that, for all grafts, you need to choose a mild, rather humid than dry temperature; take trees rich in sap; don't let the sap abandon the grafts and, to this end, remove all the buds underneath them; loosen, a fortnight after the operation, the ligatures which often create harmful swellings; have sharp, clean, simple instruments that have been adopted 3. by experienced people: a clamp, a screwdriver, a scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors, a pair of scissors. by experienced people: ordinary serpette, serp, hand saw, slotted grafting tool; ties made of wicker, woollen thread, lime bark, iron, etc.; liquid grafting putty, Saint Fiacre's ointment; grafting wax to be used at a degree of heat such that it can be handled without burning. This phenomenon is often observed in plants that have been cultivated for a long time; these varieties sometimes remain so similar to themselves, through a long succession of generations, that one would almost believe oneself entitled to declare them true species: Onions, cabbages, melons and many vegetable plants; roses, carnations, daisy queens, azaleas, pelargoniums, dablias, etc. If you want to fix these new characters as much as you can, you will breed away from all other individuals of the same species, those in which these manifestations have occurred: this is what selection is all about. If you protect your chosen subjects from crossing with typical plants for a very long time, they will provide you with new types. Perennial varieties can be preserved by grafting, cuttings and layering. When a species has naturally produced a few clear-cut varieties, you can increase the number by artificial crossing or métissage, which is performed as follows: after allowing the races or varieties you wish to obtain mixed-breed forms to flower simultaneously, you place the pollen of one on the stigma of another. The operation is therefore quite simple; only it requires certain precautions to succeed, of which we'll say a few words. As soon as the foreign pollen has been deposited on the stigma, protect the flower from all contact with pollen from the plant itself or from other varieties. To this end, cut off all the other flowers, cover the artificially fertilized flower with a piece of canvas until the ovary swells; the smallest insects could bring it this pollen, the action of which would render all your care null and void. For hybridization, you will operate in the same way as for cross-breeding, with this crucial difference: the cross-breeding no longer takes place between races or varieties of the same species, but between different species, resulting in much greater uncertainty. There is a chance of success if the two species not only belong to the same natural family, but are also of the same genus, without differing too much from each other. Half-breeds and hybrids often take after both the father and the mother; half-breeds themselves are almost always as fertile as the typical species; hybrid flowers are affected by total sterility in the case of simultaneous abortion of their pollen, which has thus become unsuitable for fertilization, and poor conformation of the outer parts of the ovary or ovules; hybrid flowers are affected by partial sterility if the pollen alone is lacking, the ovary and ovules remaining in normal conditions. Some hybrid Petunias are very fertile; they fertilize themselves or cross between themselves, giving rise to many hybrid varieties. More or less fertile hybrids do not form a species; from their second generation, there is a tendency to revert to specific forms; then complete or incomplete return to these forms. In general, cross-breeding between races of the same species, or between different species, gives plants the ability to vary. Hybridization should never be practised for vegetable plants, to which it could only be harmful; we hardly know the results it would have on fruit trees - it is best to refrain from any imprudent attempt here. - ==="" ~πu======" =-==mo==-- -r==e=-==----=n =-"Sru FIRST SECTION. - PLANT DISEASES. There are diseases which, like plethora in men and animals, arise from an overabundance of life in plants. For example, certain branches, called gourmands for this reason, appropriate almost all the juices and leave the other parts of the plant without vigor; cut out the gourmands, reduce the manure; replace soil that is too fat, too substantial, with leaner, lighter soil. Trees that produce leaves instead of fruit are known as phyllomanes, or portfolios. Carpomante is the opposite of phyllomania: the tree has far too many fruits, but they are small, of poor quality and difficult to ripen; the problem is remedied by pruning and disbudding. Fasciation, so common in Compositae and Roses, consists in the accidental flattening of stems in branches that are naturally cylindrical; moreover, fasciation does not rob plants of their vigor and, because of this very monstrosity, makes them sought-after as ornaments. We can remedy the loss of vigor and languor of plants in poor soil or exposure, by moving them to another location and giving them manure, light, air and warmth. The ordinary causes of sterility are: cold, which freezes the fruiting bodies; heat, which dries out the stigma and makes it unsuitable for retaining pollen; and rain, which removes it from the stamens before it has fertilized the pistil. Hyacinth and gladiolus onions, etc., are subject to white glanders, caused by very bitter cold. Without destroying the outer forms of the tubers, white glanders changes them into a white, slimy, odorless, slightly acid and semi-liquid pulp. The progress of this serious disease is sometimes halted by removing the infected tunics and placing the onions in pots lined with sandy soil, in a warm location, but out of the direct action of the sun's rays. The garden potato species Marjolaine, Chaville, Schaw and Sept-Semaines are subject to a disease that manifests itself as frisole, or a peculiar folding of the leaves over themselves, changing from green to dark brown, and the appearance of brown spots on the surface of the stems; if you divide the tuber, you'll see that it has taken on a yellowish tint, then brown, spread irregularly in length and width. Immediately cut off all the outer parts of the plant at ground level; when you pull them out, you'll have small but mostly good potatoes. Just leave them exposed to the sun in the open air, then bring them in when the weather dries out. Don't be afraid to eat slightly damaged potatoes, as long as the damaged parts have been removed beforehand; starch factories will even buy the worst ones. 51 Dry sulfuring of vines is best carried out in full sunlight, from 10 a.m. to noon: once, when the vines begin to vegetate; again, when they flower; and again, when the bunches are in verjuice. Grapevine disease consists of the rapid multiplication of a microscopic fungus, Oidium Tuckeri, on leaves, shoots, bunches and grapes, which then appear to be covered with a whitish dust or powdery down. Examined under the microscope, this fuzz is made up of fine, ragged, compartmentalized filaments and oval or elliptical spores, joined end to end like grains in a necklace. Leaves twitch, fruits crack and dry out. The only remedy is sulphur flower, spread with a Gontier bellows on the diseased individual, which has been well bathed beforehand. The value of the crop saved in this way, in whole or in part, compensates for the pain and cost of the medication. Blanc de meunter is another disease similarly due to the development of a microscopic fungus, erysiphe, which attacks fruit trees in particular: Plum, Apple, etc.; ornamental shrubs: Rose, etc.; and some annual plants: Pansy, etc. We prevent erysiphe by keeping vegetation vigorous and energetic; curative means have yet to be discovered. Ulcers, from which gum or other juices from decomposed sap flow, gradually eat away at the heart of the wood and kill it, unless the damaged part is promptly and deeply cut away, taking care to line the scar with Saint-Fiacre ointment or grafting wax; later, the bark advances on both sides and covers the wound. The same precautions are taken against root ulcers. The rounded, more or less voluminous growths that appear on elm and other trees are called burls; if they are not so numerous as to interfere with the plant's growth, they are allowed to remain. Chlorosis or variegation alters the green color of leaves, making them mottled or variegated: Aucuba, Phalaris rubane, etc.; this morbid state is often seen, in reality, as an improvement, an amenity of the individual; variegation sometimes disappears when plants are given more substantial soil. Lightning shatters tree trunks and branches, stripping bark and wood, dividing the latter into longitudinal fibers: cleavage. The cold causes the champelure of the vine or death of young shoots and even branches, which are cut and disarticulated at the nodes, sometimes to such an extent that there is no wood left for pruning. Cracks in tree trunks are called frostbite, sometimes in the bark alone (simple frostbite), sometimes in the wood itself (interwoven frostbite). There is no remedy for champelure and gelivure; they can be prevented by sheltering trees with hedges and windbreaks; by shodding their feet with sand or dry earth; by rolling a rope of dry straw around their trunks, etc. Mercury, arsenic and baryte sprays, soda prussiates, copper acetate, quinine sulfate; soluble oxides of tin and copper; quicklime; sulfuric, prussic and muriatic acids; alcohols, ethers and extracts of hemlock, jimsonweed and black henbane poison plants. We therefore need to keep a close eye on the waste from soda factories and other chemical products that may be found in the vicinity of gardens. External causes, such as high winds, often lead to the decortication, tearing or fracture of certain parts of the tree; grafting wax should be used in preference to St. Fiacre's ointment to hasten the healing of these wounds; trees are often preserved by tying up branches that are too heavy, too laden with twigs or fruit. If the tear does not extend too deeply, we apply pieces of wood hollowed out like gutters to the two points opposite the split, and tighten them with hemp or iron ties, which should not be removed until five or six years later, when the wound has healed perfectly. Grafting wax, ointment of Saint Fiacre. If the broken part cannot be reattached, saw it low enough below the fracture to remove all traces of splitting or tearing on the amputated surface, then pare with grafting wax. Not only does hail cause purely mechanical damage to the parts of plants it hits, but the nitrous acid it suspends disorganizes them, as you will easily notice on the leaves and stems of melons, where hailstones leave spots that grow, turn from yellow to brown and kill the part hit. No remedy, but preservatives: glass frames and mats. Nails, bullets thrown by firearms, stones, animal horns, often remain in the wounds they have made; you will leave them there, unless you can extract them without enlarging the wound too much. Healing takes place very well on its own, and the bodies gradually buried in the wood do not interfere with the circulation of the sap. Necrosis or death of the wood is caused by bruising, burns, poor pruning, cold, etc., and can be seen in a portion of the wound. It is indicated by a dry, dead portion of wood, almost always longer than it is wide, enclosed in healthy tissue. Sometimes you leave necrosis to nature, sometimes you attack it with a gouge. You won't give rise to necrosis if, when pruning and trimming a tree, you cut obliquely from top to bottom, to make it easier for rain to run off, and if you cover the bare surface with tar or pitch. Entophytic helminths, small, thin, elongated, almost transparent worms, sometimes fill the pericarp of wheat (haggard or stunted wheat) and destroy it; humidity is favorable to their development and life; no means of destroying them is known. Entophytic fungi attack all parts of plants. Ergot is a disease of grass seeds, which elongate, become purplish-black, and emerge from between the bales in the form of small black horns or ergots. All these ravages, comparable to those of gangrene on living creatures, are probably due to the presence of a poisonous fungus. No cure. Oecidial fungi attack Compositae, Leguminosae and Umbelliferae; rœstélies fungi grow mainly on Pear trees; peridermium can be seen on Pine leaves; phragmidium on Roses, Raspberries, Strawberries, etc.; red rust destroys the leaves of Grasses; smut is found on Wheat, Oats, Barley, Millet, Corn, etc.; liming is often successful against smut. Fumaginia and honeydew, vegetal-animal products, cover the stomata on the upper surface of leaves, causing them to perish. Orange, lemon, myrtle, rockrose, maple and olive trees are particularly prone to the latter two diseases. The disease attaches itself preferentially to apple trees, willows, poplars, lime trees, etc.; more rarely to oak trees. Cuseute takes Serpolet, Flax and Vine as its victims; orobanche lives at the expense of Leguminosae, Serpolet, Mugwort, Hemp and Broad Bean. Melampyrus, rhinanthus and euphrasia feed on the juices of many plant species, exhausting them. These dangerous parasites must be destroyed by careful weeding. Byssus or root white develops on the roots and rootlets of Roses, Apples, Peaches, etc.; it looks like plaster. This fungus is very hardy; it often only shows its presence after killing the individual on which it has attached itself. Some plants affected by this kind of secret leprosy can be saved by uprooting them, cutting off the roots that are too diseased, washing all the roots that are still healthy or nearly so with plenty of water, even brushing them, and then replanting them in loose soil, away from the heat of the sun. SECTION TWO. - ANIMAL PESTS. Among the mammalian animals that are harmful to plants are the rat, the mole, the dormouse and the ferret. Rats like to take up residence in old, degraded walls, where they multiply and emerge, especially at night, to gather fruit from espaliers.
