The best trap for them is the "four de chiffre", so called because of the particular arrangement of the small sticks that hold a plank loaded with a heavy body in balance; it is primed with pieces of roasted bacon. The mole provides a real service by destroying insect larvae and certain bad plant onions, but on the other hand, it disturbs garden soil to such an extent that it has to be chased away. They are most active between ten and midday, when they are caught and killed with a spade. They live on fruit, and are caught in wicker cage traps, primed by a ripe fruit, but different from those available on the espalier tree; young dormice and ferrets can be tamed like squirrels. Among molluscs, slugs, especially in autumn and spring, cause great damage in gardens, multiplying rapidly and becoming a real nuisance; have the duck, which is very fond of them, chase them away; or throw cabbage or romaine leaves flat on the ground; The slugs will feed greedily on them, even sheltering in them at night so as to be ready for the next day's feast sooner; in the morning, before sunrise, remove them; instead of crushing them, feed them to your farmyard animals. Snails or escar gots are easy to catch after heavy showers in the summer, when they spread out everywhere in search of suitable and varied food: vegetables, ornamental plants, fruit, flower buds, etc. Destroy them mercilessly.You can protect your fruit trees and dahlias by defending the base of these plants with a circular pile of crushed oyster shells, whose sharp blades will be enough to keep your enemies at bay. Snails are widely traded in Burgundy and elsewhere. Insects. - The most harmful insect in our climate is the cockchafer. It usually appears in France in the second half of April as a larva, feeding on plant roots for three or four years; as a perfect insect, it devours the green parts; shake or warp the trees in the morning, and the beetles fall off; crush them and feed them mixed with other foodstuffs, to pigs, hens, etc., which are very fond of them. Female cockchafer beetles lay their eggs in the ground, in holes from Om, 08 to Om, 10 deep; the larvae that emerge from these eggs are called mans, worms or tures; moles destroy them, but by themselves upsetting the ground in such a way that the remedy is almost as damaging as the disease. Cantharids are more formidable as perfect insects than as larvae in May or June; in a single day, they destroy all the foliage of the Ash, Oak and Privet trees they attack: Cantharides fall numb on cloths previously laid on the ground; immediate immersion in vinegar kills them; well dried, enclosed in glazed pots inside, they can be kept for thirty or forty years; pharmacists buy them at a fairly high price. Fig. 12 - Lucane or Kite, Fig. 13 - Hind or Kite (female). The kite larva (lucania) lives in the woody tissues of old oak and fruit trees; it can sometimes be partially destroyed by emptying the galleries it digs and injecting them with a burning liquid. The larva of the grain weevil appears in early spring and multiplies until autumn; we recommend covering piles of wheat devastated by this enemy with leaves of Walnut, Elder, Hemlock and Tansy; we doubt the effectiveness of this remedy; the best preservatives are silos, jars and dry, well-stoppered barrels. The green satin weevil pricks the leaves, making cones in which to deposit its eggs, from which white, hairless larvae emerge, already very voracious in this state. The following year, they're just as dangerous, in their perfect insect state; remove leaves, eggs and larvae and burn them. Destructive and type-setting bark beetles attack elms and pines; remove the rough outer bark from trees where they settle. 8 Fig. 14 - Prionc. - Hero beetles, parasites of old Oaks, and saperids, parasites of Wheat and Poplars, should be removed along with the parts of the plant they have eaten away. The prione, so called from a Greek word meaning saw, in allusion to the shape of its mandibles, is a nocturnal insect that is highly destructive to oak trees. The flea beetle, a beautiful metallic blue color, can be found on young cruciferous plants such as cabbage, rapeseed and turnip. As soon as they germinate, they gnaw their cotyledons and riddle their seminal leaves with an infinite number of tiny holes. Carefully weed and gently shake the affected plants, moving wide boards coated with glue or tar in their vicinity; the flea beetles jump on these boards and get caught. The grapevine eumolpe, gribouri, bud cutter or writer, owes this last name to the marks it leaves on the gnawed leaves, which represent the letters A, I, L quite well,

V; before sunrise, remove and shake the vines invaded by these insects, which fall into a basket placed under the branches beforehand; then destroy them. The beetle and eucampe attack all kinds of trees; their larvae are very voracious. Fig. 15 - Beetle. Grasshoppers and crickets must be hunted down and their corpses fed to poultry, or used as fertilizer. The courtillière or mole-cricket devours many larA 1 1 . Fig. 17 - Earwigs. Earwigs or forficulas are nocturnal marauders, harmful to carnations, etc., fruit and vegetables; place pigs' dewclaws in the places they frequent, where the forficulas will gather as day approaches; you'll catch them there and then throw them into the fire. Luciferous termites, all the more formidable for being invisible during the day, attack almond, plum, peach and other trees with gummy juices, withering leaves and drying fruit, When you find them, send chlorine or hydrogen sulfide through glass tubes into the depths of these insects' shelters, where they will die of asphyxiation. They seek out shade: let the sun shine on the plants, which is a simple and effective way of preserving them. The common bee feeds mainly on the ripest and tastiest fruits; flood its nest or beehive, a kind of large ball about Om, 30 to Om, 33 in diameter, hidden in the ground, with boiling water; or asphyxiate the whole cursed colony by introducing a sulphurous, flaming wick into its home. If the nest escapes your search, attach to the branches of fruit trees : 1° flasks full of honey water, where the wasps will soon drown; 2° superimposed boards, coated with honey, set in motion by a string; they will come to be crushed in this primitive but safe trap. Bees do no harm to flowers; on the contrary, they facilitate fertilization by transporting pollen from one to another. It is therefore as odious as it is absurd to destroy these useful insects. Grasshoppers. - Frémobia limbata, Bradyporus dasypus. Grasshoppers can be recognized by their hind legs, which are much longer than their front ones, enabling them to make great leaps: hence their name. The male makes a kind of song by rubbing his thighs against his elytra; the female deposits her eggs in the ground, enclosed in a thin membrane. Its larvae differ from the perfect insect only in the absence of wings and elytra. It is mainly the locust or grasshopper -4 Fig. 18. - Grasshopper. that causes damage in fields; against the grasshopper and against Eremobia limbata and Bradyporus ti Fig. 19. - Eremobia limbata. Cochineal. - It would be unfair to say nothing but bad things about this insect, several species of which - cactus mealybug, Polish mealybug, etc. - are prized for their beautiful red color, Several species, such as the cactus mealybug, the Polish mealybug, etc., are prized for the beautiful red color they provide, but the following must be destroyed without mercy: the mealybug of the fiquier, the mealybug of orange trees, the mealybug of greenhouses, the mealybug of the peach tree, so harmful to the leaves and fruit of trees; mealybugs accumulate in patches at the insertion of large branches, The female, thus fixed on the tree, secretes a cottony material with which she wraps herself and in which she then deposits her eggs; in dying she bequeaths to her Fig. 21. cochineal. offspring its dried-out skin as an impenetrable shelter from rain and cold. Cockroach. - This insect is only seen at night, and attacks not only leaves and fruit, but also bread, Fig. 27. cockroach. flour, fish, etc.; it is frightened by light, and is easy to catch. Crickets are blamed for similar damage. Fig. 23 - Cricket. The black ant harms plants by its subterranean work and feeds on ripe fruit, peaches, apricots, etc. So lure it into the shade. Lure them into vials full of sweet, honeyed water; spread a mixture of crushed ripe fruit and white arsenic or sublimate corFig. 24. - Winged ant. Cynips, diploptera and cecidomia create excrescences on the various parts of Oak, Pine, Willow, Elm, Maple, Rose, Ivy, etc., called galls if they are closed on all sides; false galls if they retain some communication with the outside. Vegetable garden pentatomas cause damage to flowers and vegetables; shake the plants affected by them over a wide-mouthed, well-glazed vase; the insects will fall in and you'll burn them. You'll easily recognize aphids by the two horns or nipples usually pointing upwards and attached to the tip of the abdomen; from these nipples emerge a sweet liquid which ants are very fond of sucking; hence the name "ant cows" given to aphids; these improvised mothers and their infants live together in harmony, frequenting each other day and night. Aphids sting and suck leaves, distorting them and soaking them in a viscous, highly-nuisible substance. The green aphid is a particular enemy of roses, destroying young shoots and buds; cabbages, cauliflowers, turnips and radishes. To get rid of them, smoke tobacco under a bell in the morning or evening, when the weather is calm. Remove the bell as soon as the aphids are asphyxiated, so that the smoke dissipates without harming the vegetation. The black aphid, which is rightly considered to be the same as the black aphid, and which undoubtedly owes its black color to the bean's juice, of which it is very avid, always attacks this plant from the top leaves; remove these leaves and burn them. The woolly aphid, quite different from the green and black aphids, has only one thing in common with them: its frighteningly rapid spread. It lives at the expense of the apple trees in our orchards, and of the ornamental apple tree with semi-double flowers, on whose bark it causes cankers of a bad nature, very often fatal. This consists in walking, in winter, Fig. 26. A large aphid. on trees infected by this insect, the moderate flame of a torch 4. made of twisted, resinous straw; this grills the hairs of the Fig. 27. - Southern tenthrush (female). woolly aphid, which soon perishes; the action of this fire is too short-lived to attack the bark of the plant. The sawfly is so called because it is armed with a kind of saw with which it cuts into the bark of the plant to which it entrusts its egg. This egg grows and lives at the expense of the plant, and changes into a false caterpillar, then into the perfect insect whose figure we give here. Dragonfly or damselfly. - It is wrong to wage war on the dragonfly, so aptly named by Linnaeus as the sparrowhawk of insects, as it destroys hundreds of them in a single Fig. 28 - Depressed dragonfly (female). day, without harming any plant. Nightingales and warblers destroy caterpillars by the thousands; so attract these insectivorous birds by placing troughs full of water and boxwood cribs lined with mealworms at their disposal in the quietest part of your vegetable garden, in the shade of the cribs; You'll be amply rewarded for these inexpensive acts of kindness, but don't let this stop you from destroying all caterpillar nests at winter pruning, and looking for the eggs where the branches meet, in the cracks in the bark. If, in spite of your diligent care, the caterpillars multiply, catch them in the evening, just as they are about to go to sleep in bundles on the branches, and kill them by spraying them with soap water; then wash the area flooded with this corrosive liquid with fresh water. Cabbage worms pierce through the leaves of this plant, often leaving only the ribs and veins; hunt them at night, by candlelight, or in the morning, before sunrise; they hide in the ground to protect themselves from the heat of the day. Pigs' droppings, used to fertilize hops, sometimes protect them from their dangerous enemy: the hop hepiracle. The tent caterpillar, the most common of the caterpillars, owes its name to the longitudinal lines of various colors on its back; it is destroyed in spring in its easy-to-find nest. Spare the larvae of the sycophantine beetle, and even encourage their development: these larvae devour hundreds of processionary caterpillars in their nests. The golden-tailed caterpillar is very common, very fertile, hairy, brown and has sixteen legs. It lives on all kinds of trees, sticking large white silky bundles to their branches. The willow caterpillar chops small fragments of wood and, mixing them with silk, forms a very hard, very solid shell that must be destroyed. The peacock and half-night peacock are very common in gardens around Paris; they have thick, broad, gray wings, with brownish patches and zigzagging reddish lines; a wide, brown eye, edged with black, black in the center, occupies the middle of each wing. The moth is about Om, 020 long, with a brown head and first segment, green or yellowish in the other parts; it rolls the leaves where it shelters during the day, taking advantage of the night to commit its frightening ravages on the stems, flowers and bunches of the vine. Eggs are found covered in a greenish mucilage on the leaf surface. Warm up the vines, put the stakes on the fire; give chase to the perfect insect, the moth that comes dazedly to burn itself at the small fires lit at night, from distance to distance. There is no known preservative against the olive leaf beetle, which is so harmful to wheat, rye and barley. It is useful to wash the bark of fruit trees over winter with a strong milk of lime, to destroy, at least in part, the larvae of apple and pear moths, which are responsible for wormy fruit. The olive tree's enemy is the chiron or Dacus oleœ larva. Carefully defend your greenhouses and frames against armadillos (woodlice), and especially against the common sowbugs so numerous in cellars and damp places; they cut seedlings and eat them to the core. Use the same method as described above for earwigs. Perennial vegetables and vegetables proper: 1° Artichokes, Asparagus; - 2° Peas, Beans, Lentils. FIRST SECTION. - ARTICHOKES, ASPARAGUS. ARTICHAUT is a plant with long, strong roots, native to Barbary and southern Europe (Compositae). Its most remarkable varieties are: L'ARTICHAUT DE PROVENCE, or artichoke without hay, with an elongated head; it is less fleshy but earlier than those we will discuss below; it fears frost and can only be grown successfully in our southern departments. - The A, gros camus de Bretagne, with its broad, flattened, slightly dark-green head, is fairly early, but not very fleshy; it thrives particularly in Touraine and most of the departments of central France. - A. gros vert de Laon, with pointed, divergent scales, is the best, most esteemed and most widely grown artichoke in the climate of Paris and the surrounding area. - The early purple A is small, but very delicate, and is best eaten raw, with peppers, rather than cooked. Artichokes are propagated by separating the offshoots or eyelets, usually in mid-April, from the old stumps; each plant can produce 6 to 12 offshoots; the two or three most beautiful are kept on the stump, and the others are broken off as close as possible to the root, so that they can be removed with their heel; shorten the leaves to the length of Om, 16, and plant in a chessboard of Om, 80 at a distance of 1 meter, in previously well loosened and smoked soil; if possible, manure should be spread throughout the artichoke patch. The soil should be well moistened and watered every two days until vegetation is vigorous; the first harvest will take place in autumn. Artichokes fear the rigors of winter. As frosts approach, cut the stems close to the roots, and deadhead each plant in dry weather, so that the soil does not retain moisture that could lead to rot. In addition, cover each clump with leaves or litter, which are easy to lift when the air is warm, and easy to pull down when the cold returns. Towards the end of March, litter and leaves should be removed, and the mounds on each plant destroyed; the levelled soil should be ploughed well, and only two or three of the strongest eyelets should remain on each clump. It's always a good idea, as a precaution, to uproot a few plants before the frosts, which, if planted in a healthy cellar or storeroom, will escape the ravages of the bad season and form a valuable reserve. However, if winter has destroyed all the plants, there's still sowing. This can be done in February or March, on a warm bed and under a frame, in pots or in potting soil. Sowing can also be done at the end of April, or at the beginning of May, in beds, separating two or three seeds by each earthen dimple. An Artichoke plant will only give a good yield for four years, so renew it in the third year. At the end of summer, you can stuff and blanch your old shoots like cardoons. Artichoke seed retains its germinative properties for five or six years; it 3 curdles milk without imparting a bad taste to the cheese. ASPERAGE (Liliaceae) is a perennial root vegetable native to many parts of France, especially the Var region, with a claw-like perennial root that produces new stems every year, which die off at the end of summer. There are two main varieties: A, green or common; grosse-violette or A, from Holland. Asparagus seed is usually sown in nurseries in October, or from mid-February to the end of March, either broadcast or, better still, in rays. Choose light but fertile soil, strong but well-amended with plenty of ash or silica sand; cover the seed with Om, 02 to Om, 03 of soil and compost; water, weed and hoe gently, lest you remove, along with the weeds, the nascent asparagus, at first as small as threads and barely visible. This plant can be used from the first year, although it is customary to leave it to grow for two years; if this practice is followed, the care given in the first year will be continued in the second. The important and difficult thing is to establish the plant permanently. Some gardeners dig pits about 1 metre deep, from which they remove all the soil and replace it with more fertile soil spread on a bed of plaster, coarse sand, broom and heather detritus, etc., with fertilizer: peat, muds and ditch and pond curings, etc. Other gardeners content themselves with planting the plant in a pit of their own. Other gardeners simply make well-sealed trenches Om, 30 to Om, 40 deep; others simply plough and smoke the bottom, then plant and sow flat. They also vary the layout and spacing of the plants; sometimes they combine up to three or four rows of Asparagus in a pit or bed Om, 90 to Om, 60 wide with ados; sometimes they plant in isolated rows equally spaced Om, 50 to 1m,60 apart, using these large intervals for other crops. Without in any way wishing to criticize these methods, which have good results (despite the teens declared here to be harmful by experience), we recommend proceeding in this way; after smoking your soil, draw your lines with a chalk line in the beds running from east to west, mark the width of each bed with a small mound of earth, then on this very bed, and without delay, establish your claw, which fears the dryness of prolonged exposure to the air and wants its hair to be spared with the greatest care; top-dress each bed; water, hoe and weed. Towards the end of October or in November, cut off the dry stems. For subsequent cultivation, take the soil you need for your annual autumn replenishments from the gaps that are becoming increasingly gullied as the soil rises around the asparagus; in spring, do a light ploughing or forking; every two or three years, fertilize with short manure at the beginning or end of winter. If the subsoil is ungrateful and always damp, it's best to plant flat, maintaining intervals of Om, 60 between beds. If, instead of planting claws, you sow on the spot, here's what you need to do: With your soil well prepared, well smoked, as we said above, your lines drawn with a chalk line, and your distances fixed, dig a ditch, where in the first case you would have raised a mound, and sow at Om, 03 one from the other 3 or 4 seeds that you will cover with compost. When the plant has sprouted, pull out the weakest plants and keep only the most vigorous; all the rest of the seedling cultivation is similar to the claw cultivation, but you won't get the products of your seedling until a year later. In Paris and the surrounding area, asparagus is harvested as soon as it emerges from the ground as a slightly green and purple shoot, about a decimeter long. Use a short knife to avoid damaging the central plateau of the claws. Never try to grow Asparagus after Asparagus itself; it would tire the soil too much; it can only grow in the same place after an interval of at least ten years. The best Asparagus to force are those that have been planted for at least five or six years. When you want to heat them, dig up and remove the soil from the paths separating the beds, replacing it with warm, trodden manure; refill the beds with the soil from the paths mixed with fresh manure, and cover them with the glass frames. This operation is carried out in December or March. From time to time, we remove the manure from inside the frames and cut off the shoots, known as white asparagus, because they have little color. New manure replaces exhausted manure; at night, straw mats or bedding protect the asparagus from cold and damp, and are removed during the day if it doesn't freeze. The plant is left to rest for a year, then heated the following year. A similar process is used to produce forced green asparagus. SECOND SECTION. - PEAS, BEANS, LENTILLES, Peas (Papilionaceae) are native to southern Europe. It requires light, healthy soil, and should not be grown for several years in a row if it is to be rich and beautiful. It can be sown in clumps or rays, in warm locations. Make rays about Om 20 apart, and in each hole, dug Om 35 apart, plant 5 or 6 peas. Until harvest, hoe, weed and rake large species, and pinch out early species at 3rd or 4th flower. Peas from warm climates can only be grown in the open air when the frost has stopped; however, St Catherine's Peas can be grown along walls exposed to midday sun, even when the cold is still to be feared. For early plants, build layers covered with Om, 25 to Om 30 of soil; sow in November, December, January; tarpaulins, layers or frames. Pinch off 3 or 4 flowers; replant the nursery plant when it has reached Om, 08 or Om, 10. As soon as your Peas have reached Om, 22 to Om 25, before flowering, lay them down at the bottom of the frame, gently placing laths on their stems to keep them flat for three or four days; once the laths have been removed, the heads straighten up and continue to grow, with only their lower part remaining flattened; this artificial curvature greatly enhances the fruiting of this vegetable. Here are some of the most important pea varieties. Dwarf shelled peas. The early dwarf is usually sown before winter, at the foot of south-facing walls; its distinctive feature is that it flowers from the 2nd or 3rd node. - P. nain de Hollande, smaller than the preceding variety and of average season, has small pods and grains, and can be planted in borders in ordinary soils; it can also be raised under chassis. P. très-nain à châssis barely reaches Om, 16 in height, it is very early; its pod contains five seeds. - P. gros nain tardif produces large, good-quality seeds; strong and stocky, it requires more space than the preceding varieties. - P. dain vert petit and P. dain vert de Prusse are two productive species. - P. Michaux de Hollande is sown at the end of February or the beginning of March; pinched, it does not need oars; it has difficulty with winter and does not grow in damp soils. - P. Michaux or Petit Pois de Paris, as early as it is good, is sown before winter at the foot of south-facing walls; pinched at 3 or 4 flowers, it is rowed in good soils; its sub-variety, P. de Ruelle, requires the same care. - P. de Clamart or carré fin gives a tight, sweet grain; it is sown in open fields without oars, for the late season; in gardens it must be rowed. - P. gros vert normand à grandes rames is a late bloomer, but produces excellent quality dry grain. - P. géant is extraordinarily large, but soft and not very tasty. - P. carré blanc is good and sweet, but too often loses its stalks and leaves. - P. fève, a large, late variety, has large, tender berries that are not very sweet. - P. vert de Noyon is content with very ordinary soil; it is semi-hardy and productive. - P. wrinkled, originally from England, is unrivalled for its large, wrinkled, sweet kernels, enclosed in a long, large, full pod. - The dwarf P. wrinkled white heath combines the precocity of P. Michaux with the qualities of P. wrinkled. P. turc has two varieties, one with a red flower, the other with a white flower; it is very heavy and yields a grain that is not highly esteemed. - P. with a volette pod produces a grayish, very large, floury, slightly hard grain, enclosed in a purple-purple pod. When cooked, it takes on the hue of roasted coffee; it tastes like a small marsh bean. Peas without parchment or mange-tout. P, the dwarf, early parchmentless pea from Holland, does very well in the open ground; it is also grown under frames. The white P, without parchment, with large pods or ram's horn, is late and produces a lot in good soils. - P. sans parchemin with half-stems, less late than the previous variety, has a less wide, but fuller pod. - The ordinary dwarf P. sans parchemin has small pods, but very numerous and very tender. - The giant P. sans parchemin, grown mainly around Paris, outshines all other species in terms of the width and size of its pods. "11 The P. sans parchemin with yellow pods is grown like the ordinary P. sans parchemin, whose qualities it shares and which supplied it. - P. sans parchemin with white pods, so called because of the whitish color of its pods, is of fairly good quality; it wants to be rowed. Peas, especially the earliest ones, are enemies of the bruchid beetle, whose female lays eggs in each barely-formed seed, so that its larvae can find abundant food there from birth. The ravages of this insect could perhaps be avoided, at least in part, by sowing at the beginning of May or in April; besides, peas pierced by the bruchid beetle are still good for sowing when the germ remains intact, which often happens. Ripe Peas, kept as seed grains, should not be shelled until they are ready to be planted; they retain their germinative properties for several years. CHICKEN PEAS. - A close relative of the pea, but of a different genus, this plant is sown in spring and harvested in autumn in the Paris climate, where it does not ripen completely. Its naturally slightly tough grain, which is difficult to digest, is used to make crouton purées. In warmer climates, it is sown in autumn and harvested in summer. The low, stocky chickpea doesn't need oars. For northern Europe, we rightly prefer the brown-grained red chickpea, which is earlier than the common chickpea. HARICOT (Papilionaceae) is native to India; it does not require as much care as Pea. It needs a soft, light, slightly fresh, well-smoked soil with a well-consumed fertilizer; sow the early species in clumps around April 20: five or six seeds per hole; hoe twice, re-sowing lightly in the second. For the other species, wait until the first half of May; June and July for the Swiss species and, in particular, for the early Dutch dwarf and flageolet, preferred by market gardeners around Paris. If your soil is clayey and compact, increase the quantity of fertilizer, delay sowing, and cover the seed very little. In strong soils, rather than sowing in clumps, sow in rows, grain by grain at about Om, 08 apart, with row spacing of Om, 30 to Om, 40. Mix a small quantity of wood, peat or coal ash with the soil. Green bean sowing continues through July and even into mid-August; if you sow them in well-sheltered beds when the year is not too dry, these late products will not ripen before the frosts arrive, but as green beans or as fresh shelled beans, they will amply compensate you for your trouble. With the help of frames, manure and stoves, you can have new green beans for most of the winter and throughout the spring, by sowing on a warm bed; you transplant the very young plant onto less warm beds, in clumps of three or four plants, at intervals of Om, 30; you need to aerate from time to time and especially at flowering time; wipe off the steam that attaches itself to the glass; finally, water abundantly. The preferred variety in this case is the Dutch dwarf bean, which yields after two and a half to three months of cultivation; it should be neither bedded nor topped. New varieties include : Bicolore d'Italie without parchment. - The early yellow H. of six weeks, the early dwarf H. Valentin. dwarf early without parchment. The runner bean is an ornamental plant known for its beautiful bunches of scarlet-red flowers; its turnip-sized root is pulled out and stored in fresh sand in the cellar for replanting in March; it flowers and produces seeds indefinitely. 4- Fava (Papilionaceae). - Beans are happy in any soil, but prefer cool, well-smoked soil, not exposed to full sun. It is sown in February, March and April, in rows or clumps, with 3 or 4 seeds in each hole, spaced about Om, 30-35 apart; at the second hoeing, they are resown to strengthen them; as soon as the flower has passed, the ends of the stems and branches must be pinched to hasten the formation of the lower pods. If the bean is removed to a quarter or half of its normal volume, the stems are cut to ground level, defended against drought and, in this way, a second harvest is obtained in autumn. Among the main varieties are: the Fêve des marais, native to Persia; the grosse F, ordinary; the F. the red dwarf, the earliest and most dwarf of all; the late green F., a very productive breed, originally from China; the F. à lonque cosse, early and well-filled. Beans kept in their pods retain their germinative powers for more than five years. Lentil (Papilionaceae). - Lentils are sown in March and April, preferably in dry, sandy soils, in rows or clumps. A variety known as Lentille à la Reine, L. rouge, has a small, reddish, rounded seed; it has a finer taste than the common L.. Some people, in keeping with ancient practices, sprout Lentils before cooking them, to increase their sweetness; Lentils should not be threshed until they are ready to be sown or eaten, in which case they keep well for two years. GESSE is sown like Peas and eaten mashed. 5. Raves, Raiforts, Salsify, Scorzonera, Beetroot, Potatoes, Jerusalem artichokes (1). CARROTS. - The carrot is a biennial, indigenous plant (Umbelliferae). It has a large number of varieties; we will mention only the main ones. Among the reds, the most esteemed are: the short red of Holland, the English red of Altringham; the common red lonque; the red demi-lonque or Meaux carrot. - Red carrots are generally tastier than yellow and white ones. The short red Dutch carrot is early and of good quality, preferable for autumn and early spring sowing. Gardeners near Paris have obtained a highly sought-after sub-variety for stews; Altringham red and Meaux red have a beautiful color and a very pleasant flavor. R. lonque commune and R. pâle (1) These last two plants are wrongly classified as root vegetables, since the edible parts of the Potato and Topinambur are tubers and not true roots. de Flandre keep better from one year to the next than all the other carrots. Among the yellows, only the short yellow and the lonque from Achicourt, near Arras, are grown in gardens, and both are of excellent quality. Among the white varieties, the most esteemed for their sweetness and ability to keep for a long time are: common white, Breteuil white, transparent white, green-collared white; violet, yellow inside and very sweet, but prone to bolting if sown early, and very difficult to keep in winter. - The Spanish violet carrot, almost black in color, or the Indian black carrot, is more an object of curiosity than a real vegetable. Carrots need deep, rich soil or open, soft soil, smoked in the year or, at the latest, in the autumn before sowing. Sowing begins in February, when there is no need to worry about spiders or snails; sowing in March, April, May and June is more exposed to the attacks of these two dreaded enemies. The early Carrot is sown in September, and spends the winter in place, producing new roots in spring and early summer. Sow by broadcasting, or in rows spaced 15-20 cm apart, about 40 to 50 grams of seeds per acre; cover with a rake; sometimes add a little potting soil; if the soil is light and sandy, pack it down with a roller. Carrot seeds take a long time to germinate and require diligent care. Weed them and thin them out to leave at least an Om.15 space between each plant. When a plant is missing, transplant it, paying particular attention to the root tips. The seed used should be two years old, well scrubbed and free of hairs. Short carrots can be harvested early; long carrots can withstand frosts if protected by a layer of sawdust or litter; it is always wise not to wait for the harsh winter to bring them in and select the seed-bearing roots. For forced cultivation, we prefer to sow the Bell Carrot under a frame and on a bed filled with 20 to 25 cent. of potting soil; we thin out the plant if it is too tight, and give it air when the sun spreads a little heat. For spider mites (Theridium), water with a quantity of soot; or with an infusion of corporal tobacco: 60 grams in 12 liters of boiling water, which must be allowed to cool completely before use. NAVET. - Turnip is biennial and indigenous (Cruciferae). It requires light, slightly moist soil; drought makes it hard, stringy and pungent; it is broadcast from late June to October; spring sowing is sometimes dared with two- or three-year-old seed. The quantity of seed varies with the season, averaging 15 to 20 grams per acre. We cover with a rake; we thin and weed; we wage war on flea beetles. In fact, the surest way to protect turnips from this enemy is to choose ploughed, well-smoked soil for sowing, so that the vegetation is quickly strong enough to resist this insect, which attacks budding leaves in particular. Among dry turnips, let's mention: N. Freneuse, the most esteemed for stews, is russet, small and half-long; N. Petit Berlin, the smallest of all; N. jaune long, which does well only in sandy, soft soil; N. de Meaux, blond, shaped like a tapering carrot: Among the tender turnips, we grow the following in particular: N. blanc-plat hâtif, rouge-plat hâtif, précoce; N. des Sablons, white, half-round, very flavorful; N. des Vertus, N. de Claire fontaine, gros long d'Alsace, rave du Limousin, N. rose du Palatinat, etc. Among the semi-tenders, N. Boule-d'Or, N. jaune de Hollande, N. jaune d'Ecosse, N. gris de Morigny, N. jaune de Finlande, are very good and strong enough to withstand the first frosts. Turnips should be harvested before the first frosts, which, without destroying them, alter their tissues, give them a bad taste and even make them indigestible. They can be stored in a cellar or in a healthy yard, covered with fresh sand. Turnip seeds retain their germinative properties for three or four years. PANAIS. - Parsnip is a native, biennial plant (Umbelliferae). Sow outdoors, preferably in February or March. It is grown in the same way as carrots. The plant should be thinned out when the young individuals are the size of your little finger. The seed retains its germinative properties for only one year. There is a variety of parsnip known as the round or Metz parsnip, shaped like a top; it is preferable for soils with little depth, where the common species would be difficult to grow. RADISH (Cruciferae). - Annual plant from China. The main varieties are: early white radish, ordinary white radish, half-long radish, half-pink radish, summer gray radish, ordinary pink radish, early pink radish, summer yellow radish, early yellow radish, large winter purple radish, Chinese winter pink radish, etc. New: White Russian radish; Large winter radish, to be sown in June; keeps very well all winter. Most radishes, especially small round radishes, can be sown all year round. In spring, Radish seed is sown alone or mixed with lettuce and onion seed for transplanting; in other seasons, full soil is sufficient, at noon in winter, with a little shade in summer. The BLACK RADISH is sown in spring and harvested in autumn; well grown, well watered, in light, cool soil, it becomes very large, but remains tough. RAVE commune and its varieties, R. violette hâtive, R. rose, R. tortillée du Mans, R. rouge lonque, have the same cultivation and uses as Radishes; soft soil. Wild RAIFORT, with its pungent flavor, multiplies from seeds sown in summer in cool, shady soil, or from root sections planted in spring. Its grated root is eaten as German mustard or capuchin mustard. Winter Radish can be grown in a frame, with the same care as for early plants: warming, hoeing, weeding, etc. SALSIFIS. SCORSONERA. SPANISH BARK BEETLE. - These plants belong to the Compositae family and are all obtained by the same method of cultivation. They are sown broadcast, or even in rays, in March, April or July and August; they require substantial, well-disturbed soil, ploughed thoroughly, smoked from the previous year, and frequent watering in dry spells; they are thinned out, hoeed and weeded. The roots are harvested in autumn and into spring; summer seedlings only produce edible roots the following year. Salsify and Scorzonera seeds retain their germinative properties for two years; they never fully germinate. Scolyme d'Espagne should be sown in rows spaced about Om, 45 apart; plants should be placed at a distance of Om, 25 from each other; like Salsify and Scorsonera, Scolyme d'Espagne tolerates frost fairly well; however, if winter is too severe, Salsify should be covered with dry litter. BETTERAVE (Chenopodea). - Biennial plant native to southern Europe; its vegetable varieties include: ordinary red beet, the most widely cultivated of all and the least difficult to grow in the right soil; bark beetroot with chagrined bark; round red beetroot, a little rough, the most difficult to grow in the right soil. red, round, a little rough, very dark red; petite rouge de Castelnaudary with spindle-shaped root, rightly praised for its fine, delicate flesh; B. de Bassano, with delicate white flesh, much sought-after for the table; jaune ordinaire, very sweet; jaune globe, almost spherical with white, tight, sweet flesh, with orange stripes. Beet seed is broadcast in spring, in soil well loosened by one or two deep, smoky ploughings from the previous year; if sown in a nursery, the plant is transplanted when finger-sized; water often, until well established; weed and hoe. Plants should be about Om, 40 or Om, 50 apart. The roots are pulled out around Saint Martin's Day (first half of November). Once the leaves have been cut off, the beets are placed in a healthy cellar; the seed carriers are placed in gauges and covered with litter. These seeds retain their germinative properties for 4 or 5 years. POTATOES (Solanaceae). - Native to America. Gardeners cultivate only the earliest species. They should be planted in March, with good exposure, in sandy, light, unsmoked soil, simply covering each hole with a handful of litter to prevent frost. The tubers are planted either whole, or cut into pieces, each of which should have one or two good eyes. If planted in winter, the holes should be made deeper, leaving 50-60 cm between plants. Weed or ridge. For forced cultivation, tubers are taken from the germinator (1), where they have been placed since the end of November; (1) The germinator is a glazed frame with a box which is placed on a warm bed, under a frame, straw mats and bedding at night, but light is allowed to penetrate throughout the day; without this precaution, shrivelling is to be feared; a mild, uniform temperature is sufficient. The layout for each frame is 4 rows and 6 plants per row; at the start of vegetation, the spaces between rows will be advantageously occupied by Radish, Sorrel, Small Lettuce and Tomato seeds; but as soon as the Potato stems have gained strength, they need all the land; moderate watering at first, then more abundant: air as soon as the season allows. We only force the Marjolin variety. Potato seedlings are not to be scorned. These include: Pomme de terre de Jeaucé, half-ta rdive, round, yellow-fleshed, very good; P. comice d'Amiens, early, round, yellow; P. Shaw, yellow, round; P. Segonzae, early P. Kidney or Marjolin, elongated, yellow, very hátive; P. Châtaigne Sainville, one of the best; P. Truffe d'août, red and round; rouge lonque de Hollande, jaune lonque de Hollande, Descroizille, half-long, very delicate; Vitelotte, red, long, preferred for stews; Caillou blanc; Flocon de neige, Princesse, Quaran taine volette; marjolaine Tétard, pomme de terre ruban rouge, Belle de Browenell, a beautiful variety with an elongated, purplish-red tuber. TOPINAMBOUR or POIRE DE TERRE (Composites). - Native to Brazil. It is 2 to 2 m high, 60 cm with flowers resembling small suns, and tastes like an artichoke. It thrives without fertilizer in the poorest soils, where it can be planted in the first spring in medium-sized whole tubers, to be harvested in autumn. It thrives and perpetuates itself, so to speak, indefinitely at the foot of a warm wall; the potatoes placed close together are removed as soon as they have sprouted shoots and roots. With Topinambours planted very close together, you can form good hedges that will protect delicate cold or temperate greenhouse plants planted in the open during the summer from gales. Two varieties of Topinambours are grown in the same months as Potatoes: one purplish-red, the other white or yellowish; they are harvested only as and when needed for consumption. 4 Vegetables with edible bulbs: Onions (species and varieties), Leeks (species and varieties), Garlic, Chives, Shallots. ONION (Liliaceae). - This perennial plant, but treated as a biennial in cultivation, offers many varieties that are easily modified by the influence of soil and climate: the large white onion and the early white onion, both good and sweet; the bulbous onion with rocambole or Egyptian onion, whose stem bears bulbs joined at the head, or rocamboles, hence its name; the white onion from Nocera, eaten candied in vinegar as an hors-d'oeuvre with gherkins; the sulfur-colored Spanish onion. d'Espagne, sulfur-colored, with tender, pleasant flesh; O. cornede-bœuf with bulbs Om, 30 long; O. globe, beautiful, but rather difficult to preserve in its spherical form; O. potato, giving neither seeds nor rocamboles and multiplying by cloves; the O, pear, flesh a little coarse and of a very strong taste; the O. jaune ou blond des Vertus and the O. de Cambrat, both large, very good and long-lasting; the O. de Madère, pale red, very large and of a sweet flavor; etc.., etc. The white onions are delivered for consumption in summer; the others, which keep better, are eaten in autumn and winter. In the North, onion seedlings are left in place; in the South, they are sown in nurseries before winter and planted in squares in spring. Both methods have undeniable advantages; we prefer the first, but will briefly explain both. Sowing in place. - Choose substantial, light, smoky soil from the previous year; roll to pack well; broadcast at a rate of 100 to 120 grams per are, in February or March; lightly bury with the rake, put in a little potting soil and pack again with the roller; very moderate watering at first; weeding and thinning. In August and September, lay down the tops to slow down the drying process and speed up ripening. Leave the crop to dry for a few days on bare soil, then bring it in during dry weather. Transplanting. - You should sow very thickly in August or September, and transplant before winter for white onions; for other species, postpone transplanting until spring. Once you've made your rays with a hoe, place your onions about 10 cm apart, covering only the roots, but keeping your bedding ready to protect them from the cold; water and weed as described above for seedlings. Harvest in August and September. Many gardeners rightly praise and practice the Nouvellon method. Here's how it works. You sow very thickly in a nursery, in August, watering only as much as is necessary for the seed to rise; you then leave the plant to form and dry on the ground; each seed thus produces an Onion from the size of a pea to that of a hazelnut. These are reserves to be transplanted in rows in spring. Watering, weeding, etc. All onions have a tendency to bolt in winter, and to exhaust themselves through premature sprouting. So keep them in a place where it doesn't freeze, but where it's cold rather than hot. As soon as they have elongated, seed-bearing onions should be given sturdy supports to protect them from gales. Ripe seeds retain their germinative properties for more than two years; leave them in the head capsules, bundle them and hang them in a dry place until you're ready to use them. POIREAU OR PORREAU (Liliaceae). - Native to Switzerland. Biennial. Leeks come in several varieties, the main ones being: the ordinary long leek, very good for sowing in the open ground; the large short leek, suitable only for spring sowing or for layering in the Paris climate; the large short leek from Rouen, which in Normandy sometimes reaches arm's length; the yellow leek from Poitou; the monstrous leek from Carentan, very large and very rustic. Leeks are sown thickly in February, March and July, in substantial soil, preferably amended the previous autumn with horse or sheep manure, grape marc or charrée, and not with the fresh manure of horned animals; replant, as soon as the individual has reached the size of a feather pipe, in rays about Om, 12 deep and Om, 15 to Om, 16 apart, after cutting off the tips of the leaves and roots; frequent watering during droughts; weeding. Some gardeners cut the leaves of Leeks up to 5 or 6 times in summer, to give them strength. To strengthen Leeks, they are sown on beds and under frames in mid-December, with stoves and blankets; they are replanted in February and delivered for consumption in July. Any plants that grow too quickly in spring should be pulled out and replanted in deep trenches. The seed comes from new plantings, also made in March, and retains its germinative properties for two years running. Well-formed leeks do not freeze, but even mild cold destroys the young plant. GARLIC (Liliaceae). - Native to Sicily. Garlic, cultivated mainly in southern France, comes in four main varieties: common garlic, Spanish garlic, early pink garlic and Oriental garlic. This plant needs dry rather than damp soil, amended with horse manure for a year or two, and warm exposure. Garlic is propagated by planting cloves Om, 15 or Om, 20 apart, in beds or, better still, in borders; plant in February or March to harvest in spring; also plant in spring to harvest in August and September. As soon as the leaves and flower stalks appear, twist and tie them together to stop the sap flowing to the garlic bulbs or pods; don't pull up the plants until the tops are completely dry; leave them to dry out in the sun, tie in bundles, and hang in a dry place. If you let a few plants grow, you'll be able to produce seeds, albeit with difficulty in the Paris climate; but in the first year, they'll only produce bulblets for planting, and the harvest will be a year late. Propagation by separating the cloves is therefore quite rightly the only method used. CIBOULE (Liliaceae). - The Ciboule is grown as a biennial or as a perennial; its seed is sown in spring like that of the Leek. Transplant when very young, keeping a distance of Om, 16 between clumps; harvest throughout the summer; sow towards the end of July for autumn. It needs light, substantial soil. Some gardeners prefer to plant perennial Ciboule in borders like Garlic, letting it form large clumps that they split twice a year. In addition to Ciboule commune and C. vivace, we'd like to mention: Civette-Saint-Jacques and its varieties, propagated by cloves in spring or autumn, in borders. Ciboulette or Cirette indigène et vivace, smaller than Ciboule, propagated by separate cloves in March, planted in beds or more usually in borders; good soil, warm exposure, watered in summer. Keep Ciboule and Ciboulette seeds enclosed in their capsules; they are good for 2 or 3 years. ECHALOTE (Liliaceae). - Native to Palestine. Cultivated like garlic, at the same time, in soft, healthy soil, smoked the previous year. The bulbs are planted in rays 0b.15 apart, up to the surface of the earth, in February-March, for harvesting in July and August; or in October-November, for harvesting in June of the following year; the leaves are pulled off when dry; the Shallots are left to dry out in the sun for a few days; then they are stored in an attic, dry and protected from the cold. The two main varieties of shallot are Jersey shallot and Alençon shallot, which are larger than the common species, but less delicate and less easy to preserve. Edible leafy and flowering vegetables: Cabbage (its varieties), Kohlrabi, Turnip cabbage (varieties), Spinach, Tetragon, Sorrel, Celery, Celeriac, Cardoons, Watercress, Alen cress, Rhubarb, Parsley, Chervil, Tarragon, Purslane, Fennel, Nasturtium, Borage. Cabbage (Cruciferous). - Indigenous. Biennial, triennial and almost perennial (in some varieties). This vegetable requires a great deal of care, but it suffices to summarize here the general principles of its cultivation, which is simple and easy. First and foremost, it needs fresh, loose, slightly strong soil, with plenty of smoke and a little shade. Carefully weed your young plant, thin it out if it's too tight, and protect it from ticks, slugs and worms (see Insect pests). Place them permanently, in staggered rows, at distances of Om 50 for small species, and Om 70-80 for larger ones. If you're transplanting in dry weather, water gently. Seedlings are planted in the spring; their enemies are aphids, just as shaped cabbages have caterpillars. Cabbages, in general, resist cold well, and you will often be content to pull them up and leave them on the ground, root up; they would perish if rain and snow melted between their leaves and turned them into icicles; you can also, after removing their largest leaves, store them in a cellar; they are measured at the foot of a wall, at a good exposure, with litter and straw mats. To make the best use of time and land, some gardeners counter-plant, i.e. place cabbage plants between rows of potatoes or other early plants; once these have been removed, a square of cabbage is planted. Let's look at some of the main species or varieties, and the special care they require. First, let's establish five main series: cabbages, Milanese cabbages, green or headless cabbages, rooted or fleshy-stemmed cabbages, cauliflowers and broccoli. The main sub-varieties of cabbage are: C. d'York, with its small, elongated head, highly prized and very early. - C. cœur-de-bœuf, very good. - Large white cabus with the following varieties: C. de Saint-Denis or C. blanc de Bonneuil with large head; C. cabus d'Alsace, very quick to form head; C. conique de Poméranie with regularly conical head; large C. d'Allemagne which gives a huge head in good soil; C. de Hollande, medium size, fairly early; C. Joannet and C. Joannet tardïf; C. de Vaugirard or pommé d'hiver, which ripens at the end of winter; vert glacé, which withstands cold well, etc. - C. hâtif or pain-de-sucre with elongated apples, tender and sought-after. - Red cabbage, highly prized and highly prized in the north, where it is even eaten in salads. All large cabbages are used to make sauerkraut. York sprouts are sown in August-September for planting in October-November; sometimes they are transplanted in nurseries and only replanted in February-March. C. cabus are generally sown: 1° in February in beds, if antomne plants are to be replaced; 2° at the beginning of March, in beds; 3° during the same month, until April, in potting soil; 4° especially at the end of July and during August, until September 8th. August seedlings are replanted in nurseries from October to November, and planted out in November-December in dry soil, or in February-March in cold soil. Savoy or curly cabbage. Varieties include: Very early Savoy cabbage from Ulm, not very large, but very tasty and quick to pick. Pancalier from Touraine. - Joulin, gros Milan, etc. - C. de Milan des Vertus, remarkable for its size and beauty; it grows like cabbages, and goes well with them, especially in well-smoked soil. - Norwegian Savoy is winter-hardy. - Brussels sprouts produce small, tender, delicate apples in the leaf axils, which are picked as they grow. They can withstand severe frosts, and are enjoyed from autumn through to the end of winter by successive sowing. Savoy cabbages are generally sown in August-September, at a distance varying from Om, 50 to 1 meter, depending on the size of the variety. Kale is more of a fodder plant than a vegetable in the strict sense of the term; however, the vegetable garden admits the dwarf kale and its two sub-varieties, the variegated kale, and the prolific kale, whose curious foliage on the veins of their leaves even earns them a place among ornamental plants. Kale is generally sown from March to May and July to August, from Om,80 to 1 meter for the larger species; to Om 60 for the smaller ones. ~--En- Kohlrabi. - Although the leaves of this plant are not eaten, we thought it appropriate to group it with the other cabbages, since it is a true cabbage with a stem that swells above the ground and forms a sort of ball at the top; the leaves are planted on the sides of this ball. Kohlrabi tastes like cabbage and turnip, and can withstand fairly hard frosts. It and its varieties are sown in nurseries from the end of March to the end of May; the plant is placed in cool, well-smoked soil; it must be watered abundantly, otherwise the ball develops poorly and remains stringy. The root is kept in crates or gauges, like turnips, unless you prefer to leave it on the spot, since in the Paris climate, this cabbage winters very well outdoors. Other varieties include: C. rave with dissected leaves; its elegant leaves are reminiscent of those of the Artichoke; small but delicate apples. - C. navet or C. turneps, with its varieties (C. navet ordinaire, C. navet blane and C. navet blane à collet rouge), capable of withstanding the coldest weather; they are sown lightly, from late May to late June, without the trouble of transplanting. - C. rutabaga or C. navet jaune, with rounded, yellowish roots, can be sown in the field from late May to mid-July. Seedbeds should be sheltered with bedding and straw mats; the seed keeps for 5 to 6 years. Cauliflower. - Cauliflower needs soft, well-smoked soil, plenty of watering, and a temperature that's more humid than dry or too hot; that's why it does better in spring and autumn than in summer. Sow in September in potting soil, at midday; prick out in a nursery and protect from frost; plant out in March at a distance of about Om.60; it produces in June and July. Hard and semi-hard cauliflower are rightly preferred for sowing. You can also sow, but very lightly, on a warm bed, under a bell or in a frame, in February, for transplanting in March and at the beginning of April, with straw mats, and plant in the open ground at the end of April; you'll have produce in June-July. Choose hard or soft varieties for sowing. During the second half of June, sow the demidur in an earthen bed, in the shade and without transplanting; set out in July; water abundantly in the evening and morning; produce in October-November. Cauliflowers can be stored in a dry place, on shelves or hung in a cellar or a healthy cellar, throughout the winter. When transplanting cauliflowers, space them at intervals of about Om, 50 to Om, 60 in all directions. As soon as the heads start to form, cover them with a few pieces of their fresh leaves laid flat to protect them from direct contact with air and light. There are three main breeds of cauliflower: the tender or salomon, with a leaf that is plainer, straighter and less wide than that of the others, and a head that is quick to form, but not very compact; the durum, with its thick, short stem and tight, heavy, compact head, which often doesn't form at all; and the demi-dur, which is a combination of the salomon and durum varieties, and outperforms them all. The following are also grown as sub-varieties of the above: Lenormand cauliflower with very large apples, Roscoff (Brittany) cauliflower, Malta cauliflower, Cyprus cauliflower, Dutch cauliflower, etc. As seedbeds, we reserve cauliflower plants sown in autumn, wintered under a frame, with strong stems and firm, tight, clean, white apples. Broccoli. - This is a species of cauliflower with wavy leaves and larger dimensions; it is grown like cauliflower, but is more sensitive to the rigors of winter. Its main varieties are white, purple and early dwarf purple or Sicilian black cauliflower. Broccoli is sown in May-June: when cold weather arrives, the stalk is covered with bedding and harvested in early spring. England has supplied us with the B. mammouth, a late breed, but whose head is equal in volume to that of the largest cauliflowers. Broccoli can only be grown successfully in the South, from the Loire onwards. We'll just say a few words about: 1° Chinese cabbage or Pe-Tsaï, an intermediate between cabbage and turnip; it grows quickly and can be sown most of the year, but is more successful in summer and autumn than in spring; 2° Marinated cabbage, whose perennial root produces stems and shoots every year; it needs healthy, deep soil and, preferably, saline fertilizers. Multiplication by seed and cuttings. SPINACH (Chenopodea). - From northern Asia, annual. Spinach can be sown very lightly every month, from March to the end of October, in well-smoked, slightly cool soil; water quite frequently; summer seedlings should be sown in semi-shade, as heat causes this plant to bolt very quickly; round-seeded varieties are preferable for this purpose. Sometimes, in winter, spinach leaves appear frozen; soak them in cold water, then let them dry out in the air, on very dry boards. The rays should be spaced Om, 16 apart. The beaten seed, as soon as it matures, can be kept for several years; we use the two-year seed for spring and autumn sowings. Male plants should be removed as soon as they have finished flowering. The main varieties are: thorny-seeded E., common E., English E., Dutch E., Flanders E., Esquermes E., the last three with smooth seeds. E. monstrueux de Viroflay, highly recommendable. TETRAGON, from New Zealand, quite common now in our gardens, replaces, at least during the Summer, Spinach, of which it has the color and taste. Sow it in beds or in holes filled with potting soil and spaced om, 50 apart; a single plant can cover an area of one square meter, watering frequently during the summer. Tetragonia's branch tips and leaves are eaten. CLOVER (Polygonaceae). - Perennial, common in meadows. Sorrel can be propagated by sowing in rows, in spring and autumn, in any soil and at any exposure; frosts slow its growth, but without destroying it or even attacking its leaves; however, it is wise to cover it with straw or dry litter. Sorrel can also be propagated by dividing clumps in spring and autumn, although sowing is still necessary from time to time to maintain the properties of each species. - Varieties: - Belleville sorrel, less acidic than common sorrel, with broader leaves, widely cultivated in the Paris area; - Virgin sorrel, very delicate, with blonder leaves, broader than those of common sorrel; it can be prevented from seeding, its male and female flowers are on separate stems; it is propagated by shattering; - Slap-leaved sorrel, still not very widespread; - Snow sorrel grows even under the snow. Sorrel seeds retain their germinative properties for 3 years. CELERY (Umbelliferae). - Native, biennial. Requires naturally cool soil, or frequent watering. It is sown from January to April, on a bed or under a cloche and under a frame; in the open ground, from April onwards in a sheltered position, in rows separated by sufficient intervals to allow the soil necessary for buttages to be taken up. This operation is carried out three or four times before heavy frosts; as the plants lengthen, they are tied up; the soil is first covered up to the first tie, then eight days later up to the second, and eight days later up to the third; only the tips of the leaves remain uncovered. In gique and northern France, gardeners transplant Celery stalks to the bottom of a ditch, bring 6. the soil up on either side and mound. With plenty of potting soil and a good bedding, some Celery can be left in the vegetable garden; the rest is brought into the vegetable greenhouse, mounded with sand or light earth, and blanched according to consumption needs; some is kept in reserve in gauges; the cold is fatal to Celery. In our northern departments, we are obliged to overwinter seedlings under frames, whereas in the south of France, we simply transplant them to a warm location with a cover. The seed retains its germinative properties for two or three years; the newest are the best. Main varieties: Céleri court hâtif, C. plein blanc, C. nain frisé, C. violet de Tours, C. creux, C. à couper, the last two are non-stopping. CELERI-RAVE (1), still little known and not widely grown, has an aromatic taste, which is not to everyone's taste; it is nevertheless an excellent vegetable; it needs deep, naturally cool or almost continuously watered soil. Seedlings are treated in the same way as other species. Plant at a distance of Om, 50; surround the beds, if possible, with gutters that retain water and thus provide Celery with the perpetual moisture it craves. This is the German method. Look for fibrous roots and cheyelu. Celery root plants are tucked in and surrounded in winter by sand or light earth; a few plants left outside in healthy soil brave the cold without the help of mats or bedding. CARDON (Compositae). - Barbary, Biennial. The various varieties of Cardoon are sown in May, in the very place where they are to grow to their full potential, as they do not tolerate transplanting. (1) The root is the only part of the celeriac plant that is eaten; nevertheless, we have not thought it necessary to separate this plant from the others listed in this chapter under the heading of edible-leaf plants. You'll make holes spaced Om, 80 to 1 meter apart in all directions, well-soiled and in each of which you'll place two or three seeds, reserving yourself to leave only the most beautiful plant afterwards; you'll treat these plants like those of Artichokes, but you'll need to water them more often when the leaves have reached 1m,50 to 2 meters; around mid-June, gather them together with wicker or straw ties, cover them with a straw shirt, and protect the base from the wind by a small ridge Om 06 high, to prevent them from shrivelling; don't forget that the ribs must be eaten as soon as they are white, otherwise they will rot. So, at the end of autumn, lift your cardoons intended for winter canning, plant them in fresh sand in a dry, frost-free place, and only stuff them as and when they are needed for consumption. Don't bind your seedbeds, simply stub them out like those for Artichokes; they'll flower the following summer and give you seed that keeps for five or six years. Varieties : C. de Tours, with full, thick ribs, wrongly preferred to the following; - C. inerme, almost as full as the C. de Tours; - C. d'Espagne, with hollow or half-hollow ribs; - C. à côtes rouges, very wide and very full; - C. Puvis, remarkable for its volume. Puvis, remarkable for its volume, wide and half-full ribs; many gardeners rightly proclaim it the best of the Cardons; it is certainly worth the Tours variety, with its dangerous spines; it has few prickles, sometimes none at all. CRESSON de fontaine (Crucifers). - Perennial, native. Watercress requires a trickle of running water or tubs half-filled with soil, and almost continuously covered with a sheet of water. Multiply with seeds or roots. Large artificial watercress beds can also be created when the soil lends itself to this type of cultivation. Varieties: C. des prés is sown in spring in moist soil; same properties, same care as for Cresson de fontaine. C. de terre is sown in damp, light soil, in spring, in rows; it also replaces Cresson de fontaine. CESSON ALÉNOIS Ou PASSERAGE CULTIVÉ (Crucifers). - Annual, from Persia. This plant owes its name to the pungent, acrid flavor of its leaves, which is quite similar to that of watercress. Passerage is short-lived and quick to bolt; it needs warm, dry soil with southern exposure; in summer, seedlings are renewed every two weeks. Three varieties: C. alénois frisé, C. à larges feuilles and C. doré. An excellent addition to early spring salads, which are always a little bland; very healthy. Cresson alénois is worthless once grown. - Water frequently in summer. RHUBARBE (Polygonaceae). - From Asia. Rhubarb can be propagated from seed, sown as soon as it matures in March, in a bed of light soil, to be planted after the first year in deep, healthy soil, with a distance of about 1.30 m between plants. They can also be propagated by separating clumps; water frequently. The best species are: Rhubarb ondulée, Rhubarb australe or Népaul, Rhubarb groseille, preferable to the two previous ones. We know that the English eat a lot of Rhubarb for their pies. Tartreum is just one variety of wavy Rhubarb. PARSLEY (Umbelliferae). - Parsley is one of the most widely used seasonings. The seeds of common parsley take a long time to germinate; they should be sown early in the spring, or, in large quantities, as early as February, in well loosened soil; the gutters should be Om, 35 apart, so that the soil can be easily hoeed in these intervals and weeded. In winter, Parsley is sold by weight and at a high price, so Paris market gardeners transplant a certain quantity of Parsley to the foot of a wall, to the south, with glass frames, as a guarantee against frost. Parsley does not flower or set seed until the third year, but then it dies. Some people, afraid of confusing it with hemlock, prefer to grow parsley with crisp leaves. Cultivated parsley varieties are: P. commun, P. frisé, P. nain très-frisé; S E ~ S O 7 M 8 Fig. 57. - Parsley. The last two are not constant and often revert to common parsley; the only truly distinct species is parsley with a large fleshy root, much sought-after in Belgium and Holland as a seasoning for fish; we use it little in France. CERFEUIL (Umbelliferae). - Native and annual. Chervil is sown in March, at the foot of a wall, in a warm spot; in June, in the north and in the shade, and in any spot between the end of March and September. The seed ripens within the year and keeps for three years. Varieties: C. frisé, C. musqué or d'Espagne, aniseed-flavored. Multiply by sowing in spring and separating plants. - Tuberous or bulbous C., sow from September to December, in soft, carefully weeded soil. - M M 2 7a E 4 2 E les~ Fig. 38 - Chervil. Q 2 AM E - C. de Prescott (from Russia), with edible root, widely used for stuffing turkeys, geese, etc., sown at the end of April; harvested at the end of August; stored in a dry place until March. Chervil should always be carefully peeled to avoid leaving behind the small hemlock (Ethusa cinapium), which you can recognize by the darker green and sharper divisions of its leaves. Large Hemlock is easy to recognize by its stems dotted with livid spots reminiscent of snake skin, its repulsive odor, its very bitter taste and the pungency of all its parts; Its leaves resemble those of Wild Chervil; they are twice and thrice winged, large and a little soft; the leaflets are pinnatifid, acute and blackish-green; the flowers are white, arranged in very open umbels, with 3 9 4° 4 2l 2 Fig. 29 - Small hemlock. several leaflets. The calyx is entire; the petioles are unequal and heart-shaped; the seeds are oval and globose, with tubular ribs. Hemlock is found in wastelands, rubble, hedgerows, etc. (1). ESTRAGON (Compositae). - From Siberia, perennial. Multiply in April by the bursting of feet, placed at Om, 30 from each other; in the climate of Paris, it easily braves the rigors of winter; north of the Seine valley, one folds back . E E Fig. 40. - Great hemlock. stems in November-December; mound and top with dry litter. POURPIER (Portulaceae). - Native to southern France, (1) Hemlock poisoning is treated with plant acids, vinegar, lemon juice, and promptly administered purgatives. annual. Once eaten as a salad, this plant is now eaten stewed. It is sweet and refreshing. It is sown in the open ground from May throughout the summer. In both cases, it's important to remember that the plant spreads quickly over the whole ground, so you need to be careful with the seed. Frequent potting. Varieties: P. doré, P. doré à très-larges feuilles. In order to prolong the production of the leaves, the edible part of this vegetable, the tops are removed as soon as they are ready to flower. The seed retains its germinative properties for five or six years. FENNEL (Umbelliferae). - Native to the southern regions of Europe, and much sought-after by the Italians, who consume huge quantities, it is not widely grown around Paris. It is sown from February-March in light, sandy, well-amended soil; broadcast or in nurseries; in the latter case, replanted: keeping a distance of about Om, 20 between each plant. Hoeing, weeding and watering. Fennel is very hot, so don't eat too much of it. It can be used to garnish stews, macaroni, etc., etc. CAPUCIN (Tropéolées). - Native to South America. We will only discuss the edible varieties here. The large one and its brown-flowered variety, the small one whose barely-formed flowers and still-green seeds are candied and used in place of capers; the fresh flowers are used to season salads. Sown in March along trellises and arbors, Nasturtiums bloom all summer long. BOURRACHE (Borraginae). - Native, annual. Sow in the open, in spring and autumn; its pretty blue flowers adorn salads like Nasturtium flowers; its leaves are a good sudorific. No cultivation care is required, just prevent Borage from invading the garden with its seeds. Salads: Spring, summer and winter lettuces, Roman lettuces, Chicory, Scarole, Wild chicory, Chicory with Nasturtium beard, Lamb's lettuce, Rapunzel LAITUES (Composites). - From Asia. All the many varieties of lettuce produced by cultivation can be grouped into two general divisions: 1° Head lettuces; 2° Roman lettuces or chicory. Among the lettuces in the first division, the earliest of all are called spring lettuces; the most voluminous and least prone to bolting are called summer lettuces; and those treated as biennials from autumn onwards, to be eaten in the spring of the following year, are called winter lettuces. This division also includes non-heading lettuces for cutting. In general, lettuces grow quickly and set with difficulty in soil that is too light, too hot and too dry; they grow extremely slowly in strong, cold soil. The main spring lettuces are: L. crêpe and L. gottes or gau, which are of little value, hold poorly, i.e. are far too quick to bolt before they have formed their heads or almost immediately afterwards, but are very early; this is their only merit. One variety, the -~~~] 7es 1* gotte à cordon rouge (red-stringed), can be grown on a coucne or in the open on a hillside, in the south, from the first days of spring. The dauphine variety, highly esteemed in England, is the best of the spring lettuces. The main summer lettuces are: L. blonde de Versailles, which is very delicate; L. blonde laesseuse, L. chou or L. de Batavia, L. de Gênes, L. rousse de Hollande, etc. L. chou is a very good apple, with a high yield. In many countries, this plant is grown only for pigs, which are very fond of it. In mediocre soils, you can plant the blonde pa vesseuse and the rousse de Hollande, which are very hardy and succeed on their own when left to their natural vegetation. The following are grown as winter lettuces: Passion lettuce, morine, petite crêpe and black lettuce, all of which are not very delicate in taste, but are very hardy and can overwinter in the garden if planted in a good spot. They are especially suited to the forced cultivation we'll be talking about later. Market gardeners call lettuces "cuttings", not particular species, but plants of Pancake or Gotti lettuces, sown very thickly: they cut them off at the root collar as soon as the fourth leaf appears and deliver them for consumption; Chicory and Spinach lettuces have this particular destination. Among the true novelties, we admit only the large white winter lettuce, which is easy to grow. Romaine lettuces include even more varieties and sub-varieties than round lettuces. We'll just mention the main ones: the early spring and summer green, the summer market garden green, the market garden grey, the largest known; the winter red, fairly insensitive to cold; the thick-leaf alphange, the blonde marai chère, the blonde de Brunoy, very large and quite similar to the alphange, needing, like it, to be tied. All are good and advantageous, but you'll rightly prefer the green summer romaine and the grey summer and late-season romaine, to which you can even add the tender, delicate, very large artichoke-leaf romaine, capable of withstanding the first white frosts. All Romaines are easily capped, i.e. they have a tendency to fold the tops of their leaves over each other; you can encourage this natural tendency by tying the leaves together at mid-height with a wet straw tie. Lettuce seed (whatever the species) should be sown in light rather than heavy soil, broadcast, very light, barely covering each other. Round-headed lettuces do not require transplanting, as they pump very well in place. Plant in staggered rows at distances varying from Om.40 to Om.20 between each plant, depending on the size of the individual plants; don't pack the soil too tightly so that the roots of your Lettuces sink easily into the soil, and mulch the beds so that the water from your watering doesn't make the soil too compact. Sow spring lettuces in March for transplanting in April, and summer lettuces from April 15 to the end of July, so that they follow one another uninterruptedly throughout the summer and provide you with good plants for transplanting; Winter lettuces in three or four different sowings, from August 15 to September 15, so that you can transplant them before All Saints' Day and leave them in place over winter with mulch and litter; lettuces for cutting, which cannot be transplanted, can be sown on a bed and under a frame in January; on a dull bed in the economic tarpaulin from mid-December, when winter is not harsh. When spring comes, you'll be able to start sowing outdoors again on a terra-cotta bed, and continue fortnight after fortnight throughout the summer. Watering and weeding. Lettuce's enemies are grubs. Romaine lettuces are grown in much the same way as round lettuces; the Romaine maraîchère plant is raised under a bell or under a frame, for forcing or transplanting in the open air, so that it can be delivered for consumption as early as the first spring. The plant is transplanted in rows, in a checkerboard pattern about Om, 04 apart; it can be partially planted in the open air from February to April, and sometimes as early as the end of December. Beware of frost and sunburn. For the forced cultivation practiced on such a large scale around Paris, you sow the seed of early lettuces under cloches, on an earthen bed, at noon, giving your plant as little air as possible (étouffée cultivation); you transplant under other cloches (at a rate of five individuals per cloche), then on beds, leaving only the necessary space between the surface of your bed and the glass, as your lettuces must not become large; You'll need to maintain a moderate but constant heat by using manure stoves, or even better, thermosiphon heaters. Be sure to wipe the steam condensed on the inside walls of your frames; the mist that falls on the leaves when they're cold causes stains. Set aside the best Lettuces of each variety for seedbeds, without tying them up; keep birds out in general, and goldfinches in particular, by means of small mirrors attached by strings to slightly inclined sticks, or by other scarecrows made of rags, etc. Lettuce seed retains its reproductive properties for three or four years; for your seedlings, you should preferably take the two- or three-year-old seed; the plant it provides grows more slowly than that which would come from the previous year's seed. CHICORÉE (Composites). - Indigenous and perennial. Chicory's bitterness must be forgiven, in view of the other qualities of this salad, the healthiest of all. It is very sensitive to cold, and can only be grown outdoors from April onwards. Bury little, if any, of the seed, which you should sow on a well-soiled bed. When planting, keep a distance of about Om 30-32 between each plant in all directions; give Chicory the same care as Lettuce until its leaves, spread out in a circle, meet on top of each other; bind them to blanch them. From April onwards, seedlings are renewed month by month and planted until September; if autumn is fine, you'll have easy-to-keep reserves in the cellar for winter supplies. Beware of the slightest frost. Gardeners who use forced cultivation sow curly chicory seed on a warm bed, under a frame, in February-March, and even sometimes in January, for the primeur, on a very warm bed; they always transplant on a very warm bed, leaving intervals of Om 20 in all directions. The main varieties of chicory are: Chicorée d'été or Chicorée d'Italie, highly sought-after for the early seasons, and even for autumn, as it fills up quickly and well; C. de Meaux, which is prone to bolting if sown before June and fills out slowly; Rouennaise or corne-de-cerf, with a full, yellow, tender heart; toujours blanche, a semi-full-grown variety especially suited to the Midi climate; Scarole or Escarole, or Endive, which is grown like ordinary curly chicory, but without forcing it; At the end of autumn, it is blanched on the spot by laying the leaves down at their ends and holding them in this position with a weight of soil proportionate to their strength; it is harvested as soon as the weather softens. Wild Chicory or Coffee Chicory grows in uncultivated areas, along roadsides; you can sow the seeds very abundantly, and pick the smallest leaves as soon as they appear, to eat in salads, alone or mixed with Lamb's lettuce or Lettuce. Market gardeners use wild chicory to make the salad known in Paris as Barbe-de-capucin.
