In spring, they sow the Scarole seed, tightly packed in poor sandy soils; in November, they uproot the plant, and after removing all the leaves except the emerging central leaf, they entrust it, in bunches about Om, 20 in diameter, to layers of hot manure prepared in advance in cellars, in vast abandoned quarries where neither outside air nor light reaches. New: Wild Chicory with a large root, very happily cultivated in the Brussels area; it makes a good Capuchin Beard. Chicory seed retains its germinative properties for five or six years; the old variety is preferred, as it produces plants that are less prone to climbing. MACHE, BOURSETTE, DOUCETTE, etc. (Valerianaceae). - Annual, native. Three varieties are available: Mâche commune, M. ronde, M. d'Italie or M. régence. Lamb's lettuce is sown from August to the end of October, broadcast, in loose soil, smoked from the previous year; for example, on beds that are still available after having produced several vegetable garden products; it is lightly covered and watered in dry weather; the most advanced Lamb's lettuce plants are picked as and when needed for consumption. Since the seed falls out of its own accord as soon as it is ripe, the seedstocks should be removed before they are fully ripe, and hung up in a healthy but not too dry place. The seed retains its germinative properties for at least five or six years. Lamb's lettuce is considered best when it has withstood frost once or twice. Lamb's lettuce that grows wild on its own in the harvest is called wheat or hare's ear lamb's lettuce. New: lettuce-leafed M., a beautiful white-green; a recommendable and easy-to-cultivate plant. RAIPONCE (Campanulaceae). - Campanula raiponce grows naturally in woods, along hedges; its leaves and roots are edible. Sow in midsummer, in light, well-soiled soil, barely covering it with the tines of a rake; if you forget to water, the root becomes so hard that it ceases to be edible. Wild Rapunzel has a finer, more delicate taste than cultivated Rapunzel. ε παιι πωσ Edible fruit plants: Strawberry (species and varieties), remontant strawberry plants, non-remontant strawberry plants, biferous highbush strawberry; Pineapples; Melons, Cantaloupes, Embroidered melons, Smooth-skinned melons, Watermelons, Cucumbers, Gherkins, Squash; Tomatoes, Aubergines, Peppers. STRAWBERRY (Rosaceae). - Perennial. The Strawberry, which is not very fussy about the choice of soil, is the first in our climate to give us its delicious fruit, and it gives us this fruit for longer than any other plant. It braves the most rigorous cold, even those of Siberia and Greenland, which last nine or ten months; nevertheless, it prefers substantial, slightly cool soil. All strawberry plants, except F. buisson and F. Gaillon, send around them, in all directions, nets or runners furnished with knots from distance to distance; these knots take root in their turn and give other nets; let them take free flight in August-September, if you want the plant by this means; otherwise cut them off or rather prevent them from developing, as they weaken the mother plants. F. buisson and F. Gaillon can be propagated by dividing clumps in spring and autumn, taking care that each clump retains a few roots. 7. Strawberry seeds should only be sown when the aim is to regenerate or maintain good species unaltered, or to create new varieties; this is how we have obtained all these large-fruited strawberry varieties of American origin. To sow, take the best ripe strawberries, crush them in water to separate the seeds, then place them in light, soft, well-divided, terra-cotta soil. Cover with Om, 001 sifted potting soil or heather; Warm exposure, but sheltered from the sun and strong winds, by means of straw mats; frequent watering; the seedling takes about fifteen days to emerge; at two months, it can be transplanted in situ or in a nursery. If you don't want to sow until the following spring, leave the seed to dry out and store it in a dry place. Strawberry plants are planted in borders or beds, with a distance of Om, 30 to Om, 40 between each plant, in soil that is neither clayey nor too compact, well tilled, well smoked and free of bindweed roots and other perennials; weeding, hoeing and watering are carried out; a thick mulch will protect the fruit from contact with bare soil, on which it would become dirty, and will prevent many weeds from reappearing. Renew the plant every two or three years if you want to keep your harvests good and plentiful. In the first half of September, we pull up wild strawberry plants in the woods. Strawberries are usually planted around mid-September. Strawberry plants with very large fruit require a spacing of Om, 35 to Om, 40 in all directions; small and medium-sized varieties are happy with spacing of Om, 25 to Om, 30. Watering should be frequent during the hot season; you should stop when the first white frosts are approaching. Strawberry plants have an enemy in white grubs, which you can prevent if, before planting, you line the bottom of the soil with a thick, well-trampled bed of carpenter's shavings or chestnut leaves; then, with the soil back in place, proceed as usual. Warm cattle urine, spread over the strawberry field, kills the cricket mole, one of the strawberry's most formidable enemies. For forced cultivation, use the nets produced in July by the young Strawberry plants of the Four Seasons, the Princess Royal and the Count of Paris, planted in March of the same year, and raise them in a nursery in part-shade until the beginning of September; Then you plant three plants in each of the pots prepared in advance, with good, light, substantial soil. You then arrange these pots (about forty) in a frame that's not too deep, so that your Strawberry plants are at most a few centimetres from the glass. In December, you will successively force your pots by transporting them either to the pineapple greenhouse, or under a frame, in tannin or warm bedding manure, always as close as possible to the glass, as they need light. Strawberry plants left in the open ground, in a suitable position, can also be harvested much more quickly if they are sheltered (straw mats, frames). Strawberries that produce several harvests in the same year are known as remontant strawberries. The two main varieties are The F. des Alpes, or four-season or all-month variety, is the best of the remontant strawberry plants, indeed of all the strawberry plants; its fruit is almost as delicate as that of the F. des bois, and can be harvested from April until the frosts, in the open ground, and during the winter in frames and hot greenhouses. It is sometimes necessary to renew it by sowing, as it degenerates quite easily; it offers the following sub-varieties: F. with white fruits, Gilbert's black with strongly colored fruits, les quatre saisons meudonnaises. F. buisson de Gaillon produces fruit similar to that of F. des quatre saisons and requires the same care in cultivation. It flowers a little later in the spring. It is usually used to garnish flowerbeds along driveways; in fact, as it has no netting, it is very suitable for this purpose. It needs to be renewed often, however, as overcrowded clumps choke, wither away in the middle and stop producing. A light mulch or moss protects F. Gaillon from sunburn and direct contact of the fruit with the ground. Distance between plants, Om, 30. White-fruited sub-variety, very good, like Gaillon proper, it produces more in the second season and autumn than in spring. Non-remontant F. are those that provide only one crop each year; all attempts to make large-fruited Strawberries remontant have failed. F. des boïs: round or elongated fruit, small but excellent. - F. de Montreuil, the largest and most productive of the F. des bois varieties, requires a dry, sandy site for planting, and fresh, rich soil for permanent transplanting. - Caprons, with their lightly-colored, slightly musky fruit, have their male and female flowers on separate plants: the male plants, which are naturally sterile, therefore occupy a precious place in the vegetable garden, but to no avail. - Pineapple F. have a bland, musky flavor that is not to many people's taste; their stalks swell and thicken as the fruit grows. - American F. have provided, and continue to provide, many varieties through hybrid crosses and seedlings; their rounded fruits are sweet rather than acidic, while their conical fruits are acidic rather than sweet. Among the American species most worthy of a place in the vegetable garden, we'd like to mention: Virginte scarlet, with high, bluish-green foliage, small, round fruit, pierced with deep cavities, very early; it bears from the end of May to the end of June. American scarlet, very productive; oblong, dark-red fruit, pink flesh. F. du Chili, very large fruit; requires substantial soil and southern exposure. - F. svperbe de Wilmot, very beautiful fruit, up to Om, 22 in circumference. Goliath, fruit almost as large as Wilmot. The F. queen Victoria, very beautiful, large fruit, dark red, glossy, with a fine, fragrant taste. British queen, very productive, the best of the American varieties, with rounded fruit. F. haut-bois is the name given to a strawberry plant remarkable for the height and elegance of its flowering stems, and for its fruit, which has a vinous flavor. It is biferous, meaning that, without being very remontant, it flowers again in autumn and gives a second harvest almost as abundant as the first. As large strawberries only produce once a year, the deyra gardener must grow several varieties, ripening at successive times over a two-month period, in the following order: First fortnight. Princesse Royale, Black Prince de Cuthill, Keen's seedling. Second fortnight. Comte de Paris, Caroline, Pineapple. Third fortnight. F. Elton, Duchesse de Treviso, Superb Wilmot, Queen Victoria. Fourth fortnight. British queen, Barne's large White, F. du Chili. ANANAS (Bromeliads). - From South America. Pineapples are grown exclusively in France, etc., under forced cultivation. It is propagated by the eyelets that are born at the

The first two methods are almost always used; the third is used to obtain new varieties. Carnations and crowns are treated in the same way as cuttings from succulents; you don't plant them at the very moment you detach them, but let them dry out in the air, leaving the heel cut. Complete cultivation takes eighteen months to two years. You can start at any time. If you have planted your carnations at the beginning of November, in pots filled with heather soil and immersed in a layer that is always kept very warm, 20° centigrade at night and 25° centigrade during the day, you will wait until spring to put up a new, very warm layer, where the pineapples will quickly develop their roots; Ten or twelve hours later, they should be cut into pots larger than the first, filled with a mixture of equal parts of potting soil, heather soil and good garden soil. These pots should be placed in the tannage of a greenhouse, as close as possible to the glass. Watering and sprinkling from time to time; cleaning foliage with a damp cloth or sponge; high heat up to 35 and 40 degrees; strong fumigation with tobacco against parasitic insects, lice, cochineal insects and so on. Towards the end of summer, the plants are made or reeds; many gardeners, as soon as the flowering is over and the fruit has formed, subject the pineapple roots to an amputation almost as severe as that mentioned above. Replace each individual plant in a pot about Om.20 wide, immersed in new tannin, and make do with a temperature of -30°-32°. Distance between each plant, 0m,60. In recent years, many people have adopted the English method, which has always worked for us and which we believe is at least as good as the previous one. It consists in planting rooted Pineapples, after their first winter, in a tarpaulin soil Om, 60-80 deep, well heated by thermosiphon pipes; they are not transplanted or amputated later, distance between each foot, Om, 60. Frequent watering. Pineapple seeds, sown in spring in heather soil, sprout fairly quickly, but the plant only bears fruit after five years. Main varieties: A, providence and Enville, very early; Cayenne and its varieties: - A. Otaïti and A. de la Jamaique, very late; A. Comte de Paris, easy to grow; A. de la Martinique; A. Charlotte Rothschild; Princesse de Russie; Enville Pellevilain; Enville Mme Gonthier, etc. MELON (Cucurbitaceae). - Native to Asia. The first sowings are done in January, in pots of Om, 11 diameter outside, which you will bury in layers covered with frame and straw mats; at the rate of one seed for each pot; you can also sow in full compost, in small gullies of Om, 04 deep, leaving between each seed a dista nce of Om 08; or, finally, in terrines which you will entrust similarly to a warm layer. You'll need to provide air at the most favorable times of the day; you'll need to clear the glass of the fog that hangs on the inside walls. One month later, you'll set out, still on a bed, at a rate of two or three plants per panel, taking care to press each plant down to the cotyledons. When the plant has four leaves, you top it to encourage the growth of buds in the leaf axils; you prune the branches at the sixth or seventh leaf, and when there is a fruit, you pinch an eye above it. Bell Melons are sown in March-April, on a bed, under a frame, and are transplanted in clods and rows, at an average distance of Om, 80 to Om, 85, with a shaded bell, for a few days, on each plant until it has recovered well. Give air and light from time to time. Prune as above. In the South of France, sow four or five seeds in piles of manure, covered with soil. Choose the most beautiful plants as soon as they have emerged, and sacrifice all the others; the fruit obtained in this way is not very tasty. Don't grow Melons, Cucumbers, Potzrons, etc. too close together. The fertilizing dust, spreading in all directions, profoundly alters the species, giving you pumpkin melons with an unpleasant taste. Melon is sown in the open air, from May 10 to 20, on a bed of half-decomposed manure, inclined to the south, with potting soil mixed with good ordinary soil; there's no need to transplant; from time to time, spread a few handfuls of pigeon-hole around the melon plants, and water. The seed should only be harvested from fully ripened fruit, dried thoroughly and kept away from humidity; prefer old seeds to young ones. The best of all melons are Cantaloupes, and the best of all Cantaloupes are: the orange Cantaloupe, small, round, ribbed, with slightly firm red flesh; the earliest of all melons; consequently the most widely used early. Noir des Carmes, with round, green-black fruit, with ribs that are not very pronounced, but without scabs; red, vinous, very good flesh; very early under glass. - Prescott, the most esteemed in Paris; with more or less scabby ribs; shades varying from green to silver; small Prescott, with black or brown background, crown with protruding point in the center of the crown; - C. d'Alger, with red flesh; - C. de Hollande, etc. - M. maraîcher brodé. New: cantaloup d'Epinal; comes in at around 2 kilograms; fine, flavorful, beautifully colored flesh. For seedlings in the field, use preferably this last melon, the M. de Honfleur, the M. sucrin de Tours, the M. sucré vert d'Angers. In the south of France, we mainly grow: Melon de Malte, with white, melting, sweet flesh; early; medium size. - M. de Cavaillon or M. d'hiver, greenish-white flesh, juicy and fine. - M. d'Odessa, green with yellow stripes; melting green flesh; like the two preceding varieties. PASTEQUE or WATER MELON (Cucurbitaceae). - With red or white flesh, rather bland, can only be grown in France, in the departments of the Midi, where the orange tree lives; it comes in open fields, in holes previously filled with substantial earth and manure; we leave it to itself. Watering in the event of severe drought. CUCUMBER (Cucurbitaceae). - Native to India. Grows like melons, either on beds or in the open air. Sow in the first fortnight of May, keeping a distance of about 1.50 m between each plant from the center of the holes, each of which is about Om.50 in diameter and has been previously filled with fresh manure and good loam mixed with substantial earth. Mulch; water frequently. Concentrate all the sap on the fruit, by pruning and pinching. For early cucumbers, blane hâtif and hâtif de Hollande, remember what we said above about early melons. Bonneuil cucumbers are grown in the open air and turn into cucumber pomade. CORNICHONS (Cucurbitaceae). - These are cucumbers picked during the first period of fruit formation. The following are grown as Gherkins: 1° the small green Cucumber; in the open air, without pruning; frequent arr osage; 2° the C. serpent, so named because of the elongated, flexuous shape of its fruit; a less productive species than the previous one because it branches much less. Same care in cultivation. CUCUMBER (Cucurbitaceae). - They are sown in April-May, on piles of manure covered with potting soil, in damp, warm soil; holes are made similar to those for cucumber seeds, but larger, deeper and more widely spaced, depending on the size of the variety; when the seeds have sprouted, the best plants are selected, leaving one in each hole; They are usually left to develop naturally, with the exception of the Pumpkin, whose first stem is cut off above the second or third eye, and when the fruit is set, the branch bearing it is stopped two or three eyes above. Among the cultivated species and varieties, let's mention the main ones. The Pumpkin proper, often with huge, very heavy fruit, with the following varieties: P. d'Espagne, better than the previous one; P. de Chypre, with greenish flesh, not very thick; Giraumon-Turban, with yellow and greenish fruit, quite sweet. - The most esteemed varieties of squash are: Melonée or Muscade de Marseille, which ripens with difficulty around Paris, but does well in the south of France; - C. de Messine, with almost no inner cavity; C. de l'Ohio (America), with fruit weighing up to 3 or 4 kilograms; soft, sweet flesh; C. à la moelle or vegetal pith, with long fruit from Om, 14 to Om, 22; pale yellow; soft, melting flesh; highly prized by the English; the non-running Artichoke de Jérusalem; with fine, delicate flesh. - The Gourde de Pèlerin, the Courge Massue, etc., are not edible. Squash seeds retain their germinative properties for six to eight years. These plants are also propagated by cuttings and herbaceous grafting. New: Flat striped squash; waffle squash with spherical fruit weighing 6 to 7 kilograms; sweet, fine, golden-red flesh. TOMATO OR POMME D'AMOUR (Solanaceae). - From Mexico, annual. Like melons and squash, tomatoes fear the cold, and can only be sown in the open air from mid-May: the cold of autumn destroys many of these plants if they have not ripened by this time. It is therefore more prudent to sow in February and March, in the empty parts of melon plants; transplant to bed; then plant out as soon as the atmosphere is sufficiently warm. A little water and manure are all it needs to grow almost anywhere, with a good exposure to the south; pinch it to stop its wild shoots; press it to support its soft stems. Tomatoes are strengthened by sowing on a warm layer from the first days of January, then transplanting to another warm layer and planting out on a third warm layer; pruning is severe, leaving few fruits; harvesting takes place in June. For field tomatoes, if the autumn cold stops them ripening, simply place them in a warm greenhouse on shelves, or even on the kitchen mantelpiece, to turn red. New: Early dwarf red tomato; very productive variety; fine, tasty flesh. Seeds retain their germinative properties for three years. EDIBLE FRUIT PLANTS. AUBERGINE OU MÉLONGÈNE, Merangene (Solanées). - This plant, so widespread and easy to grow in the south of France, only produces good fruit in the Paris climate when it is forced, as the tomato is, with the difference that, to increase the volume of the eggplant, it is transplanted up to three times, eight or ten days apart each time, on very warm beds; it is planted in May and harvested in July. Sow on a bed in March, transplant on a bed in April, then plant in the open air, at the foot of a wall, in a warm spot. The following varieties can be distinguished: violette lonque, the most widely cultivated; V. ronde and panachée from Guadeloupe, with white, purple fruit; in the Midi, even the rather unhealthy fruit of A, poule-pondeuse or Plante-aux-Œufs is eaten. PIMENT (Solanaceae). - This plant is grown like eggplant. Sow on a bed in February-March or on compost in April; replant at the end of April or first fortnight of May, in a bed facing south, or in pots buried in a bed. Fruits are harvested within the year. Varieties: Long Pepper, Sweet Spanish Grey, Violet, Pepper-Tomato, with yellow, rounded, fairly sweet fruit, but which ripens with difficulty. - P. enragé des Antilles is a greenhouse shrub. Several chili peppers are grown as ornamental plants in the northern provinces of New France; Piment rouge d'Espagne, a very pretty breed of chili pepper with fruit 15 to 16 centimeters long, more or less cylindrical in its entire length. Fruit is a beautiful coral red, very fleshy and extremely sweet. Mushrooms. - Medicinal plants. - Economic plants We mentioned above (1) how to prepare manure for mushrooms; now let's talk about cultivating these cryptogams. When the spawn has penetrated all the way to the top of the millstone and has firmly attached itself, it is necessary to gopter, i.e. cover the entire surface with about Om, Ol of very finely sifted, slightly salty earth, which is applied with the back of the shovel; After harvesting, a little soil is added to the empty places left by the removed mushrooms; in this way, products can be obtained for another four or five months. In the English method, two or three wheelbarrow-loads of dung are taken from animals fed mainly on dry fodder, oats, field beans, etc.; it is crumbled as finely as possible; layers of Om, 20-25 thick are formed, which, by settling, are reduced, after a few days, to Om, 12-15; towards their center carefully chosen spawn is sown and, during (1) See the chapter on Fertilizers.  " -". - "*2 Pr "1-- 4. CHAPTER XI about two months, we have a harvest abundant enough for a family. Gardeners on the other side of the Channel don't lay these kinds of beds on the ground, but on shelves with ledges, in a warm yet dark shed leaning against the wall of a single-sided greenhouse; we have seen, in some Scottish homes, beds of Mushrooms in wide, deep drawers under large kitchen tables. Mushrooms, even the best and best-known ones, those that are grown in layers, become dangerous when they have aged, and should be eaten as soon as they hatch; if the edges have unfolded and the blades under the cap have turned from purplish pink to brown or black, throw them away; at the very least, they could give you terrible indigestion. Leaves and flowers are used. - LINSEED, seeds for herbal teas, etc., flour for poultices. - CONSOUD. Dry or green root. CHAMELINE or CAMOMILE, Myagrum sativum (Cruciferae). - Oil-bearing plant. Sown in spring, at a rate of about 5 kilograms of seed per hectare, in soil at (1) See also Fodder plants. 8 *3 T" 1" wheat or sandy soil; harvest as soon as capsules turn yellow. CARDÈRE, CHARDON A FOULON, C. A BONNETIER, Dipsacus fullonum (Dipsaceae). - Biennial plant armed with strong, numerous hooks suitable for combing sheets. Good soil, sow in the spring in northern countries, in autumn in southern Europe; weed; distance between each plant, Om, 32. As soon as their heads turn yellow, you cut the stems, leaving a length of about Om, 30 to be able to bind them. CARTHAMUS, SAFRAN BATARD, Carthamus tinctorius (Compositae). - Dry, deep soils, sow as a broadcast crop, or better in rays, from March or early May, depending on the climate, when frosts are no longer to be feared. Space plants Om, 33 apart. Harvest florets in dry weather; store in a healthy, well-ventilated place. The seed yields an oil much used in the Orient; parrots usually feed on it. HEMP, Cannabis sativa (Cannabinis). - Hemp needs light, free, substantial soil, well loosened by ploughing in autumn and again in spring. Sow immediately after a fertilizing rain, burying the seed very lightly. Weed, thin out. Harvest the male plants, the first to mature, as soon as they turn yellow, and the female plants when the seed is almost ripe. Stir the seed often to prevent fermentation; keep it away from wind and birds. It makes good burning oil. 4 hectolitres are enough to sow one hectare. Piedmont hemp, which is taller and stronger than the previous variety, is especially suited to making ropes for ships; it thrives in very ordinary soil with little or no smoke; however, set aside a square of your hemp field, richer in humus and more fertile, for the hemp plants that are to supply you with seeds for sowing. Chinese Hemp, with wider, more extensive branches than common Hemp, sometimes reaches heights of up to 4 meters, but rarely flowers or seeds in the Paris climate, COLZA. See also CHOU. CORCHORUS TEXTILIS or LO-MA. - It differs little from Corchorus olitorius or Corette potagère, and produces a coarse filament. GARANCE, Rubia tinctorum (Rubiaceae). - Perennial and indigenous. It needs light, fresh, substantial, well-tilled, well-smoked soil; sow in March-April, broadcast or even in rays, in which you spread the seed as evenly as possible, at a distance of Om, 03 to Om, 04. Weeding, reloading in the first year. New weeding, new reloading the following year in November; you'll cut the plant as soon as it's in flower if you want to use it as fodder. The third year, in August-September, you harvest the roots, which sometimes sink into the ground to Om, 40 to Om, 50; you carry them to a very clean area, stir them with a fork to remove laterre; you put them to dry in a dry, ventilated place or in an oven. GAUDE, Reseda luteola (Resedaceae). - Biennial, usually sown in July in dry, sandy soils, among beans, etc. Weed in autumn and winter. Weed the following autumn and spring; in summer, we pull up the stems as soon as they turn yellow; we dry them in small bunches placed at a distance from each other; if they are too crowded, they risk fermenting. GUIZOTIA OLEIFERE (Composites). - Several unsuccessful attempts have been made to cultivate this plant, whose stems burst very frequently and which, in France, yields far fewer seeds than in Abyssinia, where it originated. HOPS, Humulus lupulus (Cannabinae). - Native to northern Europe and North America. Flowers dioecious, the males in paniculate clusters; the females in a scaly cone at the end of an ovular stalk, composed of large, membranous, russet-white, oval scales or bracts, concave at their base. Stems hard, angular, reaching heights of 6 and 7 meters, with the help of supports. Leaves large, rough, petiolate, opposite, saw-toothed. Hops thrive in damp, sheltered places, hedges and woodland edges; they fear heat and flower in July. Cattle like to feed on hop leaves and stems. The cones have a strong, narcotic, slightly worm-eaten odor and a bitter taste; they are used by brewers to make beer. In northern Germany and other parts of the world, young hop shoots are eaten in salads or prepared like asparagus. Its shoots, softened in water, make strong, flexible bindings; they can even be used to make a kind of coarse yarn. Hops are also used to decorate arbors, cradles, trellises, etc. As the limits of this work do not allow us to go into any detail on the cultivation of this important plant, we refer the reader to the special treatises. Suffice it to say that Hops require very rich soil and costly care. LINSEED, Linum usitatissimum (Linaea). - Cultivated for the oil yielded by its seeds and for the spun yarn obtained from its bark. Linen is sown in spring and sometimes in autumn; winter flax is preferred for autumn sowing. Flax needs light soil, ploughed in all directions and well smoked. Flax is broadcast; 1° thickly to obtain very fine flax; 2° lightly, if seed is desired. Riga seed is the most esteemed of all; its white-flowered sub-variety, also known as royal flax or white-flowered American flax, produces stalks with remarkably supple fibers. Other main varieties include: L. froid, L. chaud, L. de mars, L. de maz. White-flowered flax is distinguished from other flax breeds by its medium-sized, sparsely-branched stems, with seeds that ripen as soon as the plant is pulled up; it succeeds easily, produces regular products and does not degenerate. Its yarn is highly sought-after for mechanical spinning. Flax is harvested when the bolls have turned yellow; after removing the seed immediately, either by beating the stems or by passing them between the teeth of a rake, it is rolled on the meadow or in water. It has been calculated that between 100 and 175 kilograms of flax seed are needed per hectare. VIVACE LINSEED, Linum perenne. - This flax should be sown fairly thickly to force the stems to stand upright and lengthen; substantial, well-smoked soil. MADIA DU CHILI, Madia sativa (Compositae). - Very-rustic, fast-growing plant; yields good oil for eating, but with a flavor not to everyone's taste; also used for making soap, fulling sheets, etc.; ordinary but deep soil. Sow from mid-March to the end of June, in rows spaced Om, 40 apart, with plants from Om, 12 to Om, 15 in the row. 12 to 15 kilograms of seed are needed per hectare. Harvest when seeds take on a grayish hue. Madia's strong, unpleasant odor protects it from all insect attacks. COTTONED NETTLE, Chinese A-Poo, Urtica nivea (Urticeae). - Sow in a terrine or on a bed of soft earth, barely covering; plant out in the second year; cotton nettle is more successful in the south of France than in the north. Cultivation experiments are not yet very conclusive. PAVOT, OLIETTE OR ŒEILLETTE, Papaver somniferum (Papaveraceae). - This plant is grown particularly in our northern departments; it produces an oil that is less delicate than olive oil, but has none of the disadvantages often attributed to it. The poppy needs soft, substantial soil that is well loosened. Broadcast sow from the end of March through May and June: even in September in the Midi, on cleared clover and alfalfa, but not after oats; spread the seeds as evenly as possible, barely covering them; hoe several times, and thin so that the stems have a space between them of Om, 15 to Om, 23. In September, you'll pull up the poppies, whose maturity is announced by the gray color of their heads; you'll gather them in small heaps, beat them on the spot and drop the seeds onto cloths spread out on the ground. Two or three kilograms are enough to sow a hectare. Blind poppies (Willette aveugle) are poppies with large heads devoid of holes and opercules. The white poppy (Papaver somniferum album) is cultivated less for its seed than for its large capsules, which are used for medicinal purposes; they provide opium through incisions made on the stem. PERSICAIRE, INDIGO, RENOUÉE TINCTORIALE, LOUREIRO; Polygonum tinctorium (Polygonées, from two Greek words meaning: plants with many knots). - Perennial in China, treated as an annual in France and best propagated from seed rather than cuttings. Sow in mid-March for the Paris climate, in nurseries for transplanting, or in light, healthy, fertile soil. Cloches and frames. Plant in rows separated by a spacing of Om,50 to Om 65 to facilitate hoeing and harvesting. One square metre of nursery will give you a hundred or so good plants to plant when they have 4 or 5 leaves, in May or late April. Hoeing, weeding and watering. You'll need around two kilograms of seed per hectare for sowing in rows, and six kilograms per hectare for broadcast sowing. Water Pepper Knotweed, not to be confused with the preceding species, or Peach-leaved Persicaria, thrives in damp places, along streams; it has a pungent, burning, peppery flavor, hence its name; its whitish flowers, washed with red, bloom in the course of summer. It is sometimes combined with sorrel and raisins; its seeds are used instead of pepper in the preparation of some foods. It produces a greenish-yellow color that is rightly preferred to gaude, and is used as a fertilizer. Bistort owes its name (twice torsoed) to its large, fibrous root, folded twice on itself; all livestock, with the exception of the horse, are very fond of it; its tender leaves, like those of spinach, are prepared and eaten, and its seeds can be used to feed barnyard birds. Its root, which contains a lot of tannin and gallic acid, loses its stypticity through successive lotions, and yields a starch which, when mixed with wheat flour, even in fairly large quantities, does little to alter the quality of bread. Viviparous Knotweed, which is closely related to Bistort, likes the cold pastures of the Hautes-Alpes and the Pyrenees. The Samoyeds and Tartars grind its roots into flour. Amphibian Knotweed, with its bushy spikes of pleasant red flowers blooming in August-September, is eagerly eaten by sheep, horses and pigs, but disdained by cows; it is mown and added to the mass of manure. Renouée d'Orient, Cordon de Saint-Jean, Monte-auciel, Cordon de cardinal, Persicaire du Levant, with a six-foot stem, provides very good seeds for poultry. OLEIFER RADIUS, CHINESE RAIFORT: Raphanus sativus oleifer (Cruciferae). - This plant, brought from China to Italy and from Italy to France, yields an edible oil. It needs soft, slightly deep soil. Sow in autumn in our southern provinces; in spring in the north. Weed, hoe and thin out to leave an interval of about Om, 25 between each plant. Glycyrrhiza glabra (Papilionaceae). - The best-known species is Licorice, with hairless fruits, a long, tracing, woody root, yellowish on the inside, and a mild, sweet flavor. It has several stems three to four feet high, topped with leaves composed of six or seven pairs of leaflets, with one odd, hairless, oval, a little slimy. Requires soft, deep soil. Multiply by suckers or rooted plants, planted in spring at intervals of Om, 30. Manure. Roots take three years to develop their full potential. RHUBARB, Rheum (Polygonaceae). - We do not recommend large-scale cultivation of this plant, which requires a great deal of care and only produces its main product, the roots, after four or five years. Rhubarb needs light, deep soil that's slightly dry rather than damp, with a sunny exposure. Multiply by seed or suckers. Distance between each plant: 1 m to 1.30 m. Weeding and hoeing the first year; simple plowing in spring the following years. Harvest in late autumn. The roots are cut in fragments, about Om, 06 long; after removing the epidermis, they are gently dried, grated, the angles softened and rolled in a barrel. Commercial Rhubarb belongs to the Rheum dalmatum or Palm Rhubarb species, native to China. Wavy Rhubarb or Muscovy Rhubarb is highly prized by Russians, who eat its leaves raw or cooked like those of vegetables. The pulpy Rhubarb, native to Lebanon and Carmel, is praised as a remedy for inflammatory diseases; its stalks, eaten raw, are very pleasant to the taste, slightly acidic and refreshing; they are preserved in honey, sugar or grape must, and in this way are easily preserved all year round. Rhubarb compaete, native to Tartary and China, has large, heart-shaped, hairless leaves; its root is tonic in small doses and purgative in large. Rhubarb raponthic, native to Asia, the banks of the Volga, etc., is cultivated in our gardens under the name of Monk's Rhubarb. It has a large root, yellow inside, a little reddish outside; a strong, fleshy stem; very broad, heart-shaped leaves; small, yellowish-white flowers in paniculate clusters. In some regions, its leaves and young shoots are eaten prepared in various ways; the whole plant gives a yellow color and is used above all for dyeing leather. It has the same tonic and purgative properties as rale (1) : Eyes, Buds, Buds, Twigs, False Twigs, Branches, Stings, Bourses, Slats, Twigs, Stems, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning, Pruning is an operation by which part of the branches or shoots of a tree are cut off, to give the tree a certain disposition, or to make it bear the most beautiful fruit, and, each year, almost the same number. Each variety of tree requires its own specific pruning; we will only discuss fruit tree pruning in this and subsequent chapters. Pruning varies according to considerations of age and terrain, as well as the state of health or disease of the plants, differences in climate and so on. However, practical experience has formulated general laws that should be known and followed. 1° Always direct the branches of fruit trees as close as possible to the (1) In the remainder of this book, we will indicate the specific pruning for each species of fruit tree. For grafting, the reader is referred to chapter III, Multiplication of plants. 2° Take care to maintain the balance between the various parts of the tree, inclining towards the ground any twig that gets carried away, and straightening, on the contrary, any twig that is not very vigorous, as the sap tends to rise. To apply pruning properly, you need to know some of the terms used in this operation. The eye is the part of the plant that gives rise to the leaves; the bud is the part of the plant that produces the flower; the bud is born from the eye; the lateral eye is the one on the side; the terminal eye is the one at the end of a shoot; the sub-eye is the rudimentary eye that is not visible, or the bud that replaces the one that has been destroyed; the gourmand bud is the one that grows larger; the single eye is the one that is solitary; the double, triple or multiple eyes are those joined by 2, 3 or more. Virtually every leaf has an eye at its base; this eye is the starting point for all pruning theories. The bud is the modified, developed eye, the proof of plant virility; it produces a herbaceous part, more or less covered with leaves, which is called a bud. Once the bud has completed its annual growth, it is called a shoot; when it is more than a year old, it is called a branch. The carpenter or mother branch is the one that determines the shape of the tree; - the wood branches, born from the carpenter branch, are much smaller than it; the fruit branches, born from the wood branches, give flowers or fruits; the replacement branches are those that are left to develop to replace the first ones struck by disease, and so on. Branches arising directly from the carpenter branch are also known as sub-mother branches or simply sub-mother branches; they are called lower sub-mother branches if they are below the mother branch; - upper sub-mother branches if they are above the mother branch; - lower sub-mother branches if they are below the mother branch. - Fig. 42. - Bursa and sting. above; - finally, the branches placed on the carpenters and intended to produce fruiting branches are called spurs. E Fig. 43 - Bourses with stalks and eyes. Fig. 44 - Twig on bursa. The short, not very large, mostly right-angled twigs, ending in a pointed eye, are stings; - the bursa is a nodosity developed by the influx of sap on a point where there are flowers or fruit. The lambourde is a short, thick twig, with closely-spaced leaves that seem to emerge from a fold or a kind of ring. Twigs are spindly, very branched branches, almost always indicating, for vigorous subjects, forthcoming fruiting; if the twig, instead of ending in a wood bud, shows a fruit bud at its tip, it is called a crowned twig. The "cochonnet" or "bouquet de mai" is a slender twig, simple if short, branched if large; it covers itself with buds and produces very good fruit; it is found on stone fruit trees, particularly peach. Notching is an operation which consists in making more or less tongued transverse cuts in the bark of trunks or branches in spring, before the sap rises, to temporarily stop the sap. If you want to speed up the development of one part of the tree, you make the cut above; if you want to slow it down, you make the cut below. De-budding consists in removing useless or misplaced eyes; this operation is performed during pruning or a little later on plants still devoid of their leaves. Disbudding is the removal of useless or misplaced buds, from the moment they have reached just a few centimetres to their full development; this operation is performed dry, i.e. on plants not yet covered with leaves; it requires great care. Pinching is an operation designed to stop or moderate the development of certain parts, by removing the bud tips. You pinch at one, two or three leaves, when you remove the tip of the bud above the first, second or third leaf from the base of the bud. Continuous pinching, which requires a great deal of reserve and care, is performed successively on the same buds, as they develop new production. Trellising consists in tying the buds of espaliered or counter-trellised trees along walls, trellises, etc., using rags or rushes. Trellising should be carried out as the tree develops, from June to August; only well-developed parts are tied. Breaking consists in breaking, towards the end of summer, the buds destined for the production of fruiting parts, stings, lambourdes, etc. Ring incision is designed to enlarge the fruit and hasten ripening. It consists in removing a piece of bark a few millimeters wide from the entire circumference of a stem or branch; a special instrument, known as shears or ring-nippers, is used for this operation. Bleeding is a longitudinal incision made in the very hard bark of certain trees: 1° to debride the tissues and facilitate the flow of sap; 2° to cure strangles in stone fruit trees. Arcure consists in bending rebellious branches to force them to bear fruit, or at least to assume a position favorable to the circulation of sap. Nursery. - General observations on tree and shrub seeds. - Stone fruit trees: Peach, Apricot, Almond, Plum, Cherry, Bigarreautier, Cherry, Olive. CONIFERS. - Many conifers are not cultivated in nurseries; their seedlings are taken from the woods, where they sow themselves. Before using the seeds, they are exposed to an oven, so that, under the action of moderate heat, the cone scales open and leave the seeds free. Cedar of Lebanon cones do not need to undergo this preliminary operation; they are carefully opened with a sharp instrument; the seedling will not remain in the nursery for more than two years; it is better to raise it in deep pots and then transplant it as a root ball; Cypresses should be treated in the same way. The PEACH tree (Rosaceae). - Native to Persia, has produced, through cultivation, a large number of varieties, either espaliered or in the open air. The different species are: - P. with downy fruit and flesh that does not adhere to the stone; - P. with downy fruit and adherent flesh; - P. with smooth, non-adherent fruit; - P. with smooth fruit and flesh that adheres to the stone. In the first class, the main varieties are: - the Pêche admirable, large, yellow, fine, sweet, which grows well in the open air; - the P. abricotée, a little mealy, reminiscent of apricot, from seed. - Avant-Pêche blanche, with white fruit, small, sweet, but not very fragrant; early. The tree that bears it is delicate. - Belle Beauce, very good quality, but a little late. - La Cardinale, marbled on the inside, like a beet; not much appreciated. - Chevreuse hâtive and Chevreuse tardive, of good quality. - Grosse mignonne ordinary, delicate and sweet, yellow on the shady side, red on the sunny side; divided into two lobes by a wide furrow. - Déesse hâtive and Galande, excellent quality. - Ma gdeleine blanche, with fine, musky white flesh. - Alberge jaune, yellow flesh around the circumference, red around the stone. - Belle de Paris, large fruit, marbled with red and very delicate. - P. de Vénus, with large fruit, topped by a nipple; delicate flesh, the best of all. The second class includes Pavie jaune, Pavie tardive, Pavie de Pomponne and Persée. The third class includes the small, apricot-like Jaune lisse and the red-mottled P. violette de Courson. In the fourth, we find Brug non, light red, very bright on the side exposed to the sun. In terms of ripening, peaches can be classified as follows: the white Avant-Pêche and the red Avant-Péche ripen in the second half of July. - Petite mignonne, Grande mignonne, Belle de Paris and Pourprée ripen in August. - P. de Vénus, Bourdine, Chevreuses, in September. - Pavie de Pomponne and P. abricotée, in October. - Brugnons ripen from early August to late October. - Pavie tardive, in November. Exposure. - South-facing: Chevreuse, Galande, Grosse mignonne, Avant-Pêche rouge, Belle Beauce, Nivette, Pourprée, P. de Vénus, Pavie, Violette de Courson and Jaune lisse. - The other species thrive in all exposures, except north. Cultivation. - Peach trees need soil that is light rather than strong, but deep and substantial. Some varieties can be propagated from seed: Pêche abricotée, Bourdine, Admirable, Grosse mignonne; for the others, grafting is used; sometimes hard-shelled almond trees are used for this purpose, as they are not very difficult to select and are not very prone to Peach tree diseases; sometimes plum trees and especially black P. de Damas, which does well in moist, shallow soil. Scion grafting is carried out from July to mid-September, at Om, 15 of soil for espaliers and at around 2 meters for open-air trees. Planting should only take place when the sap has been completely stopped by a hard frost. - Choose young plants with smooth bark, straight, moss-free stems and well-formed roots. The soil is loosened and even replaced with new soil, if it has already been used in previous years to grow peach, plum or apricot trees. The graft is raised to Om, 50 above the ground, keeping a distance of about 6 meters between each plant; a layer of manure Om, 10-12 thick covers the whole plantation. Watering, weeding, ploughing and fertilizing in autumn: in winter, use a fine broom to remove any frost or snow covering the branches. In summer, new watering should be stopped eight or ten days before the fruit ripens. Small mats to protect against the sun's heat. Harvesting takes place in August-September. Pruning. - As a general rule, the current year's branches alone produce flowers and fruit. When a young branch, born of a woody eye from the previous year, has borne fruit, we prune it on its lower eye, so that it opens onto a bud that will later become a fruiting branch. When a bud is born on one of the branches below the good wooden eye, it must be pruned to prevent the branch from growing longer and becoming exhausted. From the first year of transplanting, we begin to prune the peach tree and give it a particular shape; the rule, which must not be departed from, is this: provide a number of spur branches, regularly distributed, so that all parts of the espalier provide their quota of good peaches, and encourage, at the bottom of the branches that have borne fruit, the birth of replacement branches. The arrangements usually adopted are as follows: 1° SQUARE SPALER. - In February-March, the one-year-old grafted peach tree is cut back to a height of about Om, 25. Four of the best buds are selected Fig. 45. - Square peach tree (1st year). Fig. 46. - Square peach tree (2nd year). They are attached in a slightly oblique direction, but without obstructing the flow of sap. In the second year, from February onwards, the inner branches are arranged in an open V, forming a 45-degree angle, to form the mother branches; the other two branches, still in the same direction, but more oblique, will be the sub-branches; the mother branches are also pruned at the same height as the sub-branches, which will be born from the lateral eyes, while the terminal eyes, as they lengthen, will contain the mother9. branches. The first sub-mothers are pruned at about Om, 30, on a good eye that will extend them. If the first year has produced only two branches, instead of four, always prune in a V-shape, with two eyes, one of which, the upper, will continue the mother branch; and the other, the lower, lateral, will provide the first sub-mother. Fruiting branches should be pruned to a length of 10 to 15 centimetres, depending on their strength and position. Care should be taken to retain at least one eye (closest to the base), which will become the fruit-bearing replacement branch the following year. In the third year, if the tree is vigorous and well-balanced in all its parts, prune to encourage elongation, drawing in another sub-tree; if the tree is weak, simply prune the mother branches much shorter, without drawing in a sub-tree. The general care to be taken in the fourth and fifth years is the same as in the third; one or more sub-heads are pulled again, and any branches that get carried away are stopped. If everything is well balanced, attach the branches to the places intended for them; if the balance is badly established, lower the strong parts, straighten or free the weak par ties. Your tree should now have six lower limbs, three on each of its two branch-mothers. You'll dehair the youngest parts; you'll garnish the inside of your espalier, taking a branch from a spur of each of the mother branches, near the fork, which, raised almost vertically, becomes the first sub-mother. Keep an eye on its development. If it gets carried away, cut it back onto a weaker bud. Prune the other parts, taking into account their strength and the place they occupy; tie gently, without hindering the circulation of sap. When the tree is vigorous, you can prune several more branches that same year, at equal distances from each other, alternating as much as possible with the sub-mothers. A well-managed, complete square peach tree should have twelve limbs, six on each mother-branch; the well-filled carpenter-branches orffent numerous spurs which are themselves rich in buds. The square espalier very quickly covers the surface of a wall, thanks to the vertical position given to the inner limbs; but this upright position makes it Fig. 47 - Square peach tree (8th year) very difficult to maintain the balance of the sap. 2° The same can be said of the open V-shaped espalier or Montreuil espalier, which is similar in size but simpler than the previous one, and is rarely used today. 3° Espalier en Palmette. - This is the simplest, most widespread and most profitable type of pruning. For the simple Palmette, in February-March, the Peach tree (grafted one year old) is cut back to about Om.30 from the ground, giving a single shoot which will become the spire or central stem of the Palmette. The first pruning produces two lateral arms which are planted obliquely at the same height, one on the right, the other on the left; only force them to take up a horizontal position at the end of summer, when the annual shoot has come to an end; the following year, the arrow produces two other lateral arms which are extended parallel to the first two, and so on from year to year, until the peach tree reaches the height of the wall. Always leave the upper eye to extend the stem. Fig. 48. - Simple palmette (third year). Fig. 49 - Single palmette (2nd year). Double pallet. - Instead of one stem, you'll have two, due to the bifurcation of the main stem at birth; each of these stems has limbs on one side only. Here's how you get these two vertical mother branches. Assuming the tree has been grafted and planted in the usual way, cut back to about Om, 35 from the ground; as soon as the buds develop, choose two of the finest, as close together as possible and coming from either side of the stem; tie them obliquely or vertically, and only bring them back to the horizontal direction at the end of summer; make sure they strengthen equally. The following spring, prune these two branches over two eyes, one of which, the terminal, will extend the stem; the other, lower and outer, will form the first horizontal limb or first arm of the Palmette. Repeat the same operation every year, paying particular attention to pinching, disbudding and the overall balance of the tree. You can also form your secondary limbs with the end of the vertical branch, inclining it when it reaches the height where the lateral branch should be placed; you then continue your 5 OO Fig. 50. - Double palmette. vertical branch using the bud born at the point of curvature. In some fast-growing peach trees, it is not uncommon to obtain two levels of lateral branches in the same year: 1° by pinching the bud that extends the vertical part as soon as this bud is at the height where lateral branches are required; 2° by encouraging the birth of early buds, which are quick to appear after the pinching operation. The double pallet is less common and less advantageous than the single pallet. 4° To get an exact idea of the size of the U-shaped Peach tree, imagine the double Palmette described above, but without the side limbs. Once the two vertical branches have been established, all you have to do is keep them in balance, well furnished with fruit-bearing branches; if, despite all your care, the upper parts get carried away, bring them together on a lower bud. In other respects, same behaviour. Horizontal palms. - Plant several peach trees along an espalier, about 4 meters apart. Make the first pruning of all their stems on a front eye, keeping all the eyes below (except those in front or behind); they will give you the fruiting branches. At the next pruning, you cut all the shoots on a front eye to obtain the extension and, on a side eye on the right, to form the first row of cords. When the extension shoots have grown, prune them in the same way on a front eye; the side eye, on the left, will form the second row of cords at Om, 45 of the first. Follow the same procedure for the other three cords. As for the sixth, bend and palisade the end of the branch intended to form it as soon as this branch has passed the Om, 45 that should separate it from the previous one. Don't cut the ends of the cords until they touch the stems of the neighbor they're heading for, after which they should be bent over a branch or a false branch, suitably palmed and cut over a front eye. Sometimes, in order to obtain cords of the same height, you will bud in August at the appropriate place, and cut the following spring. 5° For the simple oblique cordon, you will plant one-year-old grafted peach trees with a single stem, at Om 75 from each other, inclining them all at an angle of 60 degrees, which you will reduce to 45 degrees the following year. Make sure that growth is regular, and that the eyes work together to form evenly-spaced spurs on the stem. This type of espalier should not exceed 3 meters in height. Fig. 51 - Oblique cordon. PEACH TREE DISEASES. - Blanc or Meunier is caused by a fungus and manifests itself as a whitish, farinaceous dust on the leaves and sometimes even on the fruit. - Grise is caused by the presence of myriads of small insects on the underside of the leaves, which they suck off and exhaust. Remedies: freshly slaked lime, 2 kilograms; 500 grams of sulfur flower, all mixed together; soak the diseased parts in the morning, before sunrise. Against peach aphid: tobacco water; soak 1/2 kilog. in hot water for 24 hours, add about 10 to 12 liters of cold water to this dissolution; sulphur flower sprayed on leaves with a bellows or a sulphur tassel. Water with a little iron sulfate dissolved in it. ABRICOTIER (Rosaceae). - From Armenia. Blooms in February-March; reproduces by sowing or grafting. The main varieties are: early apricot or Abricotin, second quality; blane with large fruit, peachy taste, very-good quality; A. de Provence, sweet and vinous; Alberge, abundant fruit; A, black, dark wine-red color, fiery-red flesh, third quality. A. d'Alexandrie; gros hâtif de Saint-Jean; gros rouge précoce, with large, orange fruit, first quality, ripens around mid-July and does very well in full wind; A. Angoumois hâtif, small and elongated, almost reddish-yellow, with strong odor, second quality, ripens in July; A, common, with large, round fruit, a little mushy when overripe, second quality; needs northern exposure and full wind; very productive; A. de Jacques, medium-sized, rounded, reddish-yellow, first quality, ripens around mid-August; A. de Hollande or Amande aveline, small, yellow, glassy, second quality; ripens around the end of July; A. Musch de Turquie, medium-sized fruit, rounded, dark yellow, pulp transparent enough to show through to the stone, fine and very pleasant taste; ripens around mid-July; requires espalier, first quality; A. Moorpark, quite similar to the peach apricot, ripens in mid-August; A. de Nancy, large, slightly flattened and rough, orange-yellow, melting, first quality; it is even rightly declared the best of all apricots; its stone is the only one where you'll notice a pinhole, reproduction by seed, many varieties; A. Pourret, with large, vinous fruit, second quality, ripens in mid-August; A. Beaugé, with large, rounded, yellow fruit, first quality, ripens at the beginning of September; A. précoce d'Esperen, with medium-sized, flattened, yellow fruit, first quality, ripens at the beginning of July; A. Royal, with round, orange, transparent, melting fruit, first quality; ripens at the end of July; all exposures, except north. Apricot fruit is only at its best when it comes from high-stemmed trees grown in the open air, rarely pruned in the first few years, then left to grow naturally. Pruning and cultivation. - Apricot trees thrive in light, deep soil with a southern or eastern exposure. Seedlings are selected from the best fruit, stratified and planted in autumn at a depth of about Om, 055. The Apricot tree is grafted in the form of an escutcheon, with a dormant eye; it is raVb M 2 Fig. 52. - Peacock's tail fan. beaten, at Om, 25 from the ground and, after the first year, two or four of the most vigorous buds are chosen and fixed in a slightly oblique direction. In the second year, prune longer or shorter, depending on the strength of the branches; pinch off any unnecessary buds. In the third year, we lengthen the carpenter branches, keeping some buds, so that if one of these branches is missing, it can be replaced by a well-placed bud that can be reattached to the same spot. Suppression of overabundant fruit production. The result is the Peacock's tail fan, the best shape for this tree. Protect the flowers from frost and the fruit from sunburn, using canvas and straw mats. AMANDIER (Rosaceae). - From Asia. Sow in autumn, in deep, chalky soil with warm exposure, using the kernels of naturally fallen fruit as seeds. It can be grafted and pruned for open-air or espalier planting, and requires the same cultivation care as the Peach and Apricot; the same precautions against frost and sunburn. Its varieties are classified into three divisions: 1° Ordinary hard-shelled almonds, ordinatre soft-shelled almonds, Princesse, Sultane, etc.; 2° Bitter almonds, 3° Almond-Peach, a hybrid of Peach and Almond. PLUM (Rosaceae). - From Asia. Blooms in March. Its varieties can be classified according to fruit color as yellow, violet, red or black. Yellow plums. - Apricot plum; elongated shape; yellowish on one side, red on the other; firm yellow flesh, more delicate than that of the apricot plum, with which it is often confused. Ripens in early September. - Reine-Claude, diaphanous, reddish-yellow, round; ripens in September. - Prune de Catalogne or de Saint-Barnabé, small, long and yellow. - Brignole, pale yellow on the shaded side, red on the sunny side; mediocre flesh. Used to make prunes. - P. de Briançon, inedible but whose kernel provides oil. - Goutte d'or, large and oval; good quality. - Pruneimpériale white, large and elongated; firm flesh clinging to the stone. - P. Jefferson, oval, reddish-yellow; very good variety; ripens towards the end of August. La Mirabelle (large and small) round or almost round fruit; fine, sweet flesh of first quality; ripens in mid-August - La P. de Monsieur, second quality; ripens at the end of July. P. de Monsieur with yellow fruit, yellow fruit with purple spots and washes; first quality, ripens in the second half of July. P. Washington, large globular fruit, greenish-yellow or red-tinted on the sunny side; melting flesh comparable to Reine-Claude; first quality; ripens in September. - P. Sainte-Catherine, medium-sized, oval, sweet; first quality; the best species for making prunes known as Pruneaux de Tours, ripens in September-October. Purple or red plums. - P. Damas-musqué, ripens in mid-August; Damas violet, top quality; ripens in late September; Damas de Tours or P. Impériale, very good quality; ripens in mid-August. All Damas perpetuate themselves through seed. - Red diaprée plum or Impératrice, with medium-sized, elongated fruit, cherry-red on one side, firm flesh, sweet, top quality; ripens in early September. - Impériale violette, as large as an egg, very often glassy and gummy; second quality; ripens in mid-August. - The Montfort plum. - P. Perdrigon red, small and oval. - Red Reine-Claude. - Violet Reine-Claude. - Early P. Royale. - P. Saint-Julien. - P. pêche, very large and oval. - P. Victoria, very large and oval; ripens in August. - Prunes d'Agen, delicious as prunes; an important trade item. Black plums. - Black Damask plum. This tree is used for grafting Peach, Plum and Apricot trees. - P. diaprée black, small and oval, wrinkling on the tree before falling off. - P. impériale de Milan, slightly elongated, medium size; black skin, dotted with grayish black spots. Very good quality; ripens in September. White plums. - The white P. Perdrigon, very healthy, very smoky, ripens in espaliers at the beginning of September. The tree reproduces from the pit. - PruneCerisette, white and red. Green plums. - P. bifère, used in compotes; ripens in mid-July and mid-September. - ReineClaude, green apricot or Verte-bonne, large, spherical, speckled with gray and red; the best of all plums; ripens in August. Plum tree cultivation. - This tree thrives in the most ordinary soil, provided it is not marshy, loamy or too sandy; in southern countries, it thrives in all types of exposure; in northern countries, it thrives in the east or south. It can be propagated by sowing stratified cores or offshoots; the former is preferable for trees growing in the open air. Sow in spring and care for the young plant, either in a bed or in a nursery; let it gain strength before grafting, especially before slit grafting. Scion grafting is done in summer. Plum trees are planted the year after the first graft, and are cut back by 4, 6 or more eyes, depending on their vigor. Care is taken to destroy the useless offshoots, of which this tree is very prolific, by cutting them off at the root. Palmette pruning follows the general principles indicated for this operation. Plums are rarely espaliered; they like iron even less than apricots. Plum trees grown in the open air only need to be cleared of dead wood, useless branches and moss. Like Peach, this tree is subject to Gum and sometimes Powdery Mildew. Remedy: renew the soil around the base; sprinkle sulphur flower on diseased leaves; remove gum and heal wounds. Oven-dried plums keep very well. Plum wood is highly prized in joinery. CHERRY (Rosaceae). - From Asia Minor, we classify its 70 varieties into three categories: firstly, the Cherry trees proper; secondly, the Prickly Pear trees; thirdly, the Cherry trees. All these trees produce their flowers only on the previous year's wood; their fruit is round or heart-shaped, red, white, yellow or black, depending on the species. Cherry trees proper. - The German or Chaux cherry, with large, dark-red fruit, ripens towards the end of July. - L'Anglaise tardive, firm, pleasant-tasting flesh, top quality; ripens in early August. - Belle de Châtenay, large red fruit, firm, sweet flesh, first quality; ripens in early August. - Belle de Choisy or Dauphine, large, round, transparent, lightly colored, sweet flesh, first quality; ripens in late June. - Courte-queue or gros Gobet or Montmorency, very bright red; many varieties; ripens in mid-July. - Common cherry, with its varieties: Grosse, Madeleine, Cerise de pied. Small, acid fruit. Cannot be grafted; propagates by suckers. - Griotte commune, round, large, almost black, dark red flesh, second quality; ripens in early July. - C. du Nord, Belle Griotte; heart-shaped, bright red, second quality; ripens in September. Like all Griottes, it is preferred for preserving in brandy. - Cerise du Portugal, small Griotte, sweet-fleshed; ripens in early July. - C. Royale, early ripening, one of the best varieties; large, rounded fruit, turning from bright red to reddish-brown. - C. de la reine Hortense or Seize à la livre, large round fruit, bright red, fragrant; first quality; ripens in early July. - C. de la Toussaint, produced by a small tree with hanging branches like those of weeping willows, acid; third quality; ripens in July. The tree blooms for four months. - C. à Trochet, bright red, acidic, inferior quality; sometimes as many as six or eight of these cherries are found attached to the end of a single tail. Bigarreautiers - These trees are larger and stockier than the Merisiers, their twigs are more pendulous, and their fruit has a crunchy flesh that differs from that of the Guigne. Varieties: the Bigarreau, with its large red fruit, is of the highest quality and ripens at the end of July. - The B. à feuilles de tabac or four to the pound, curious for its leaves, but hardly worth cultivating, since it produces only small, few and poor-quality fruit. - B. de Metzel, with large, dark-red fruit, very pleasant taste, first quality, ripens in July. - B. hâtif, small, heart-shaped, light red, firm flesh with white fibers, first quality, ripens in June. - B. cœur de pigeon, heart-shaped, crisp flesh, the best of all Bigarreaux, ripens in July. - B. blanc, second quality, a little red on the sunny side, ripens in June. - B. belle de Richemont, with large, heart-shaped fruit of a beautiful shiny red, flesh-colored variety, first quality, ripens in mid-July. Wild cherry and guignier. - Wild cherry, the type found in our forests, is cultivated more for its wood than for its fruit. It thrives in granitic and sandy soils; its preferred variety is the Guignier, which itself gives us the most widespread varieties: the shiny black Guigne, the largest, shiniest and best of the Guignes; it ripens at the end of June. - Early black, the earliest and most common, but of third quality; ripens in the first half of June. - Early pink G., tender red, very watery. - G. grosse ambrée or grosse merise blanche, heart-shaped fruit, sweet, long from Om, 020, third quality, ripens from June to mid-July. Cherry trees with red or white fruit are used as subjects for grafting good cherry species. Cherry tree cultivation. - It's an essentially hardy, robust tree, adaptable to any kind of soil, provided it's neither too wet nor too clayey. We sow its pits in the woods. They can be grafted in buds or in slits, although the latter is less certain of success. Some good species can be espaliered; others can be planted in the open. The bark of the cherry tree is very hard and tight, compressing the sap juices; hence the custom of making longitudinal incisions in the bark from time to time, to facilitate the development of the trunk. Same diseases as stone fruit trees; same remedies. Cherries can be stored dry or in brandy. They are used to make a type of wine, kirsch, maraschino, jams and raisins. OLIVE (Jasmine). - From Asia. 8 to 10 meters high. The most commonly cultivated varieties are: O. Aglandeau or Caianne, with round fruit, very bitter, but yielding a superfine oil. - The O. d'Espagne, which produces a very large olive with very good candied, bitter oil. - Amellon or Amelline or plant of Aix, almond-shaped fruit; very good candied olive; sweet oil. - Picholine or Saurine, the best of the candied olives; sweet, fine oil. - L'O. Pointue or Rougette, esteemed oil. - L'O. Verdole or Verdau, with ovoid fruit that rots quickly when ripe. - Galininque or Laurine, with reddish fruit, resistant to cold, etc. Cultivation. - In France, olive trees require a very special region, known as the Oliviers region, and are propagated by seed, cuttings and suckers. Cuttings are the simplest way to produce good fruit; seedlings only bear fruit after 10 or 12 years and, like suckers, must be grafted. Crown grafting is preferred, although cleft and escutcheon grafting also work well. Olive trees are planted in staggered rows, at the edges, with a distance between plants of 10 to 13 meters, depending on the vigor of the species and the goodness of the soil. From time to time, they are smoked with manure made from scraps of wool rags, animal hair, horns and nails. The earliest fruits appear in November, and are harvested from then until March. In many countries, they are left on trees or fallen to the ground, but the best oil is obtained by removing them on a fine, warm day, laying them out for a week or so on a dry floor or on racks, and then pressing them without crushing the stones, as the oil from the kernels is inferior to that from the pulp. To preserve olives, they are picked before they are ripe, and their natural bitterness is corrected with a preparation based on sea salt. They are eaten ripe with a seasoning of oil, salt and pepper. Apple, medlar, orange and pomegranate trees, COIGNASSIER (Rosaceae). - From southern Europe. It is cultivated more to produce offspring for grafting pear trees, than to harvest the fruit, which is particularly prized in the south of France. Quince gives jams a delicious aroma. The Coignassier is propagated: 1° sometimes by sowing seeds, as soon as they are ripe, in light, fresh, well loosened soil, with warm exposure; weeding, hoeing; - 2° more often, by marcottes or by cépées, after having established mothers which should provide, each year, more or less rooted scions, intended for grafting Pear trees. A distinction is made between: 1° C. with fruit, in the shape of Apples; 2° C. with fruit, in the shape of Pears. The C. de Portugal is preferred for growing on trees and making mothers; its fruit is used for jellies, preserves, marmalade, etc. PEAR (Rosaceae). - Indigenous. Height from 7 to 13 meters. Blooms in April; its main varieties are listed below, according to their usual ripening period, which begins in July. 10 Poires à couteau, Poire guenette, small, fine and sweet. - P. d'Angleterre, melt-in-the-mouth flesh, fine, pleasant flavor; long, arched tail blends with fruit. - P. de Ma dame, white, tangy, pleasant, smooth skin. - P. de Do yenné, white flesh, fine, buttery, very juicy, acidulous, more or less fragrant; smooth, bright yellow skin, a little red on the side exposed to the sun; fat, short tail, slightly embedded in the fruit. Turns quickly. - P. Évargne, with fine, juicy, melting flesh, with a flavor reminiscent of Crassane; long, arched stem, placed in the axis or on the side of the fruit. Subject to blettir. - P. Mouille-bouche, medium size; long stem; thin, smooth, yellowish-green skin, tinged pink on the sunny side; excellent flesh. - P.
