Other very good knife pears include: P. de la Pentecôte, P. Royale d'hiver, P. Bonne de Soulers, P. du Curé, P. Bon chrétien, P. de Rance, P. de Douville, Belle-Angevine. Yellow and red pears. We must confine ourselves to mentioning the most remarkable pears: P. Amirale, Belle alliance, Bellissime d'hiver, P. Arteaux, P. Chair à Dame, P. d'Amboise, P. d'Amour, P. du Cog, P. d'Abondance, P. Fin-or de septembre, P. Briffaut, P. Jacobs, Grosse queue, Louise bonne d'Avranches, Saint-Sanson, P. Tonneau, P. Vermillon, P. Truitée. No need to repeat the names of the P. du Curé, etc., etc., mentioned above. Red-fleshed pears. P. Betterave, P. Sanguine d'Allemagne, Sanguine d'Italie, Sanguinolle, P. de Sang. Pears for drying or making compotes. P. Chat-brâlé, P. Beguesne, P. Carisi, P. Caullau, Belle de Thouars, P. d'Amour, P. Catillac, Angélique de Bordeaux, P. d'Angleterre d'hiver, P. d'Aunois, P. Château-Renard, P. d'Angoisse, P. d'Angoisse, P. d'Angélique de Bordeaux, P. d'Angleterre d'hiver, P. d'Aunois, P. Château-Renard, P. d'Angoisse. d'Angoisse, P. Saint- Léger, P. Martin-sec, P. Martin-Sire, P. Gros-Gilot, P. Frangipane, P. Picru, Gros-Certeau, P. FrancRéal d'hiver, P. Double fleur, P. de Sain, P. de Loup, etc. Growing and pruning Pear trees. - This tree needs deep, cool soil that is neither damp, cold nor clayey. Pear trees reproduce from seed and sowing. In autumn and spring, sow pear seedlings that have produced a type of cider known as perry. Choose loose soil for this purpose, make rays Om, 33 deep, at a distance of Om, 16; a little litter covers the seedling and keeps it cool enough to germinate. Weeding, hoeing and thinning are carried out, and seedlings that have become strong enough are placed in a nursery. The pivot is removed to encourage preduction of lateral roots, and the nursery is visited from time to time. Frequent hoeing. Two or three years later, grafting is carried out in the form of a slit or a dormant-eye escutcheon, on franc, in deep soils with a dry surface; on coignassier, in damp, cold soils. In general, trees grafted in the latter way produce fruit quickly, but do not live as long as those grafted on Francs. A Pear tree planted in good soil and left to its own devices lives for several centuries; the same variety pruned into cattails or pyramids rarely exceeds sixty years, as it usually dies out in twenty-five to thirty years. The Pear tree is fairly indifferent to the exposure given to it, as long as it receives sun all day long; a warm exposure seems preferable for winter Pear trees, whose fruit is picked last. The Pear tree can take any shape, but is best pruned into Pyramidal, Cattail, Palmette and Peacock-tailed Fan shapes. Pyramid pruning (first year). - In February, the tree, grafted one or two years old, is cut back to a height of Om, 30 to Om, 50 from the ground, on a good eye which, as it develops, should continue the axis or stem; in summer, the Fig. 53. pyramid-shaped pear tree (1st, 2nd and 3rd years). buds are pinched off, especially the buds close to the terminal, in order to activate the development of the lower buds; above all, the base must be well furnished. In the second year, lengthen the spire by about Om 20 to Om 40; prune the supporting branches, leaving the lower ones longer than the upper ones, so as to give the whole plant the shape of a 10. cone. Keep all the stalks, twigs and shoots, and prune near their base (cut to the thickness of a shield), the twigs developed on the stem, near the spire, and which are later to be carpenter branches. Prune all your lateral branches, according to their position and strength, on one eye outwards, so as to make them take a horizontal direction. Are you forced to change this direction? Prune on a side eye, but don't forget the result you want to achieve. Lateral branches should be inserted in spirals around the stem, without any of them being perpendicular above another, without any of them having a common point of insertion with the neighbor. Sometimes voids form on the axis; in this case, you can resort to longitudinal incisions or bleeding, which you make from bottom to top, towards the weak parts and a little above them; you also have the following remedies: grafting close to a neighbouring branch, and annular or partial incision. If the shoot is languishing, cut it back below, on a vigorous shoot that will replace it. If the branch is exhausted or has only fruit on it, return it to its crown. Avoid bifurcations, or allow them only with reservation, to fill a gap, take them only on the lateral parts of the carpenter branches and not above. Pruning should be more elongated on vigorous species that are slow to set fruit. Don't neglect the use of stakes and ties, but never let them stop the flow of sap; your tree would become counterfeit instead of symmetrical. In the third year, remember the general indications given for the second year. The tree which now forms a pyramid of about 1m, 35-45, if it has been well cared for, requires above all: 1° that you maintain a suitable spacing between the carpenter branches, using small wooden forks to serve Fig. 54. - Pyramid pruning, perfect condition. of buttresses; and that those which are too far apart be brought closer together, using wicker lliens; 2° that the ends of the carpenter branches be pruned more or less long, according to their vigor; 3° that the spurs be pruned short. The spindle-shaped pyramid hardly exceeds Om, 40 to Om 50 in diameter, but can reach 7 or 8 meters in height. To obtain the Cattail shape, the tree is cut back to around Om, 50 and all the eyes are left to develop; those that are too vigorous are pinched out; in August, they are broken at Om, 20 or Om, 25 in height. The following year, branches are pruned to three, four or five eyes; excessively long twigs are shortened, respecting stings and stalks. Cattail pruning, which is often confused with pyramid pruning, is rarely used today, although it has undeniable advantages. The most sought-after shape for espalier and counter-espalier pear trees is the simple palmette. To obtain this shape, the tree is lowered to about Om.30 above the ground, and the spire or central stem is pruned successively over two eyes, one on the right, the other on the left; the upper bud continues to form the stem; the buds are lowered and palisaded into an almost horizontal position, thus beginning a tier of the framework. The following year, during which the tree has been allowed only limited growth, will produce a second tier by the same procedure. The progress of vegetation in the cords of the palmette should be monitored, so that they grow equally and are of the same strength. By pinching and breaking the buds, the balance is maintained, and fruit production develops (stings, stalks, twigs). Pinching and breaking should only be carried out partially. Visit the trees every two or three days. Each year, we continue to add a new tier of branches to the tree. If one of the branches is destroyed, it is replaced by a branch taken from a neighbouring branch. Generally speaking, you should act in the same way as for Peach trees grown as palms. Double palmette. - Start by pruning two eyes, leaving the buds to grow vertically; each of these buds becomes the base of the tree's framework; they are allowed to grow lateral buds at equal distance from each other. For the rest, we proceed as in the simple palmette. Peacock-tail or fan shape. - After bringing the tree down to about Om, 20 above the ground, choose two of the most beautiful buds, pinch off and remove the others; the two buds thus preserved will be branches the following year; cut them at Om, 30, Om, 50 and even Om, 60, depending on their strength; fix them obliquely to the wall and you'll have the first rays of the fan. Then, to fill in the gaps left between the carpenter branches by their continual tendency to spread apart, choose vigorous buds and palisade them. Keep the spurs as short as possible, and keep them clear to avoid confusion. Spindle or candle shape. - We were wrong to abandon this shape, which, while not very pleasing to the eye, has many advantages: it is very easy to obtain, requires little care, takes up little space and favors fruit production. Cut back to around Om, 50, and even Om, 70, above ground level, leaving all buds to develop. In June-July, any buds that are too vigorous are pinched off; in August, they are all broken off at around Om, 20 in length. The following year, branches are pruned to five, four or even three eyes; excessively long twigs are shortened, leaving stings and shafts intact. Pinching and breaking in the usual way; removal of useless branches; columnar, vase, basket and girandole shapes; trigonal, tetragonal, pentagonal and hexagonal shapes, which are rarely used and are less advantageous than the palmette shape; single or double cords, sinuous cords, etc., etc., etc., etc... The fruit-bearing branches of the Pear tree only bear fruit after two or three years' waiting, but they remain fruitful for several years running. They are first announced in spring by the lambourdes, which bear fruit in year L 2 Fig. 55. - Pear trees in sinuous cords, with a double tier of branches. the following year and are then called bursas. These bursae produce stings. Twigs are born on the bursas and twigs, and should be pruned to a good eye at about half their length. Every year, in autumn, plough the base of espaliered Pear trees; weed and hoe them several times during the summer; every four or five years, give the bed where they stand a layer of rotted manure, Om, 04 or Om, 05 thick; A good mulch, left in place from mid-May, prevents the soil from hardening and splitting, stops the growth of most weeds, keeps the roots fresh, and makes watering less necessary. All varieties of Pear blossom around mid-April in the Paris climate; bunches naturally lose most of their blossoms as a result of the season's bad weather, or through lack of fertility (1). APPLE (Rosaceae). - Native, medium-sized. Blooms in May and therefore fears frost less than the pear tree; its formidable enemies are caterpillars, woolly aphids and weevils (2). Cultivation makes apple trees delicate. When grown in open fields, with as much air and space as it desires, it thrives and bears fruit abundantly. Cultivation and pruning - Even easier than the pear tree when it comes to choosing soil, the apple is content with soft, slightly moist soil, where its roots, which are more tracing than taprooted, settle easily; it even accepts sandy, chalky soil. It is propagated by pepins, which come from cider pomace and provide franks (known as Egrins), which can then be grafted onto high stems to form large open-air trees or beautiful cattails. Apple trees are grafted in slits and, even more often, in escutcheons. Besides, it doesn't like iron, and the only pruning that suits it in espalier is palmette pruning. How to obtain this shape: Graft from Doucin and Paradis, never from Franc; take care to elongate the supporting branches and severely pinch the vigorous buds that develop on them. The same care is used as for pear trees (see Pear trees). Cordon pruning is also very popular. (1) See also: Insectes nuisibles. (2) See: Harmful insects. One-year-old grafted Paradise trees are cut back to about Om, 45 above the graft, leaving only one vigorous bud attached to a stake and free to grow Fig. 56 - Palmette apple tree. vertically. At the end of summer, it is fixed horizontally on a wire or on small spikes. Then, year after year, the mother branches are lengthened and Fig. 57. - Apple tree in cords. to fill out with fruit crops along their entire length. Remove unnecessary or exhausted branches; allow only small fruit-bearing branches to grow in the spurs. For the record, let's mention bush pruning and goblet pruning. Normandie is a plantation of alternating lines of half-dwarf apple trees in small pyramids 1.50 m apart, and dwarf apple trees only 1 m apart. There is a spacing of 1.50 m between these lines. If the apple trees making up a Normandie are on Doucin and Paradis, the grafts must not be buried. Fg. 58. - Apple tree in vase. Fig. 58 bis. - Pear tree in vase. Every year, the apple tree is given a shallow ploughing: every three or four years, in autumn, a layer of soil about Om, 15 deep is removed from around the base to a distance of 2 meters; after amending it, it is put back in place; a little fat manure for dry soil; marl or lime for fresh soil. Remove the lower branches when they have become too strong and too numerous, preventing air from circulating around the stem. 1 Among the hundred varieties of apples, let's mention the best: Pomme d'api rose. - Small, pale yellow, bright red on the sunny side, top quality; with the varieties : Api noir, Pomme rose, which smells of
