NÉFLIER (Rosaceae) native. - It differs from the Alisiers only in its bony cores, which vary from one to five, and its styles. The common medlar grows naturally in the woods of Europe. Cultivation causes it to lose its hardiness, giving us the large-fruited, monstrous-fruited, stone-less Beech. This tree is propagated by grafting onto Hawthorn or Coignassier. Seeds take two years to set. It thrives in all types of non-marshy soil and in all types of exposure. Pruning would reduce the number of fruits, so it is left to grow freely in the corner of a hedge or by the side of a path. The medlars are bitter and unpalatable; they only become good to eat after lying on dry straw for a while. ORANGE (Aurantiaceae). - From India and China. This tree, less fashionable in gardens than in the last century, because many shrubs with more varied flowers and leaves are rightly preferred to it, is still one of the most beautiful ornamental plants, and the object of considerable trade along almost the entire Mediterranean coast. The twelve dwarf species, forming one hundred varieties, are still cultivated with the care they deserve. The first orange tree was introduced into France in 1333; in 1500, there was still only one orange tree in the north of France; it was transported from Pamplona, then capital of Navarre, to Chantilly, then to Fontainebleau; in 1684, it was given to the Versailles orangery, where it has remained ever since: This is the Grand Bourbon, or Grand Connétable, or François I. The orange, lemon and all trees in the same family are propagated by seedlings and cuttings. A leaf or a piece of leaf can be rooted. Bigarade orange seedlings are preferred for sowing, as they grow more quickly. - Poire du Commandeur, Perrettes, etc., can be reproduced from seed without much alteration. Lemon seeds can be obtained cheaply from confectioners; they are planted Om, 035 apart in deep terrines of O 16 to Om, 25, lined with good heather; or one by one in small pots. Place terrines and pots in a warm layer. Ordinary care: heat, watering, etc. The seedlings sprout and, eight or ten months later, and sometimes only after two years, produce plants suitable for grafting. They are left to grow in varying degrees of strength, depending on the variety to be grafted. They spend their third year under frame in pots larger than those used the previous year; in the fourth year, they are placed in the open air for the whole of the summer. Orange trees can be grafted from three months to ten years of age; for small trees, mainly Chinese dwarfs, we use Pontoise grafting at Om, 20-30 from the ground; for larger trees, we use scion grafting. Cuttings are only successful for M 9 87e Fig. 59. - Orange tree. Poncires and Cedars. The marcotte technique is all but abandoned. Young orange trees must be changed into new pots every year; later, they are placed in crates which are renewed every two years, then every six or eight years; crates with movable panels, which can be removed separately, make it easier to examine the roots. The best time to repot and re-crate orange trees of all ages and species is the beginning of March. Pruning. - For field-grown orange trees, pruning is limited to a moderate amount every two or three years; the most important thing is to remove greedy branches and try to obtain as much fruit as possible; the flower gardener, on the other hand, wants flowers above all else; if he encourages the formation of fruit-bearing branches, it is with the intention of pinching them off as soon as they bear fruit. In the case of ornamental orange trees, as soon as the fruit has formed, the dead or diseased branches and those that cause confusion are simply cut back in September, in the open air. Every four or five years, the tree is pruned back thoroughly, removing any branches around the heads that would cause shocking irregularities in the overall shape. Orange trees can be rejuvenated very easily; the wood can be brought up to four, five, six and even ten years old; but, to be on the safe side, you should leave a year's interval between this operation and the re-growth. Sick orange trees are placed in very pure compost, even on the tarpaulin of a temperate greenhouse, after having cut off all the damaged parts of the roots and reduced the head by severe pruning on the main branches. Cultivation. - Like other orange-growing plants, orange trees don't like water; one or two wettings are enough for the strongest orange trees over winter. When they arrive in Paris in mid-October, they don't leave the greenhouse until around May 15th; but from mid-April onwards, you need to give them plenty of air, when the atmosphere outside is warm. As soon as they're out, they ask for the soil around their boxes to be ploughed, and for a layer of about Om, 055-80 of fat, well-wetted manure to be added; horse dung on the surface of the boxes prevents settling. Watering, showers on the foliage. The roots are left bare, as they like to be exposed to the direct influence of the air. Not only in Paris, but also in England and Holland, orange trees espaliered along the back wall of a forcing greenhouse produce almost ripe fruit. Bigarades and Limons are used to season meat and fish; small green oranges are candied in brandy. Orange blossom used to sell for 4 francs a pound, but today it's hardly worth more than a franc. It should be picked every other day, and even every day in hot weather, spread out on a dry white cloth and used two or three days later; the dwarf Chinese orange trees are the most highly prized flowers for making orange blossom water. Lemon, citron and lime trees and their varieties are cultivated and cared for in the same way as orange trees. There's no need to describe the orange tree and its fruit. Bigaradiers are lower in stem; their fruit is full of bitter, acidic juice. - Lemon or Citron trees have tapering, flexible, often thorny branches; their oval fruit is filled with an acid, tasty juice. - Citroniers differ from Limoniers in their shorter branches, larger, more warty fruit, firmer, thicker flesh, very good for candying. - Lime trees produce pale-yellow, oval, brownish fruit with a hint of nougat, bland or slightly bitter. - Grapefruits are recognized by their very large, rounded or pear-shaped, greenish fruit, with a more or less sweet taste (1). (1) See also Oranges: Ornamental plants, part 22. GRENADER (Myrtaceae). - From Africa; naturalized in southern France, etc. It is an evergreen shrub, with beautiful bright red flowers from July to September, and red, fleshy, watery and refreshing fruit3 23 Fig. 60. - Grenadier. sant. It is propagated from seed, cuttings and strangulated marcots in pots, whose soil must always be kept moist. These marcottes can be weaned at the end of summer. The Grenadier does not like iron; however, it is pruned like the Orange tree; naturally, it would only produce flowers on the tips of the current year's shoots; by pruning, we aim to produce annual sprays strong enough to flower. In winter, the tops of new shoots are pinched off as soon as they reach a certain length. In Paris and the northern départements, Grenadier is grown in crates and brought into the orangery at the start of winter, giving almost ripe fruit. It must be watered often and the soil changed every three years. When espaliered on the back wall of a single-sided greenhouse, it produces fully ripe fruit. Among the main varieties, let's mention the G. with acid fruit, which is very suitable for grafting with other varieties; the G. with white flowers and the G. dwarf with small fruit. Raspberry, Barberry, Fig, Mulberry, Chestnut, Walnut, Hazelnut, Pistachio. VINEYARDS. - We need only refer here to species or varieties whose fruit is intended for table use, and not to those grown in large quantities to make wine. Vines require light, deep, moisture-free soil; a warm, south-facing or east-facing exposure in the Paris climate; pruning and training. - The vegetation of the Vine and that of the Peach are exactly the same; the pruning of these two fruit trees is based on the same principles; its aim is to obtain branches to replace those which, once they have borne fruit, will no longer do so; in the absence of branches capable of providing fertile shoots, there is no hope of a harvest the following year. After breaking up the soil to Om, 50 of its thickness, and loosening and even smoking it, the marcottes are placed at the foot of the wall, in a trench Om, 20-25 deep, with their heads turned towards the wall, Om, 50 apart; they are then 11. covered with earth. These young plants are planted in winter, at a good exposure, so that they can then spread out in horizontal cordons, etc., with the help of wire, trellis, etc. It's also in winter, when there's no frost, when vegetation is suspended, that each shoot is cut back on the two eyes closest to its base; in spring, these eyes will become productive shoots; this is how successive shoots are born on a first spur; if more than two buds grow on each spur, the others are removed, in order to have beautiful, well-stocked bunches. After a few years, the spur, which has in the meantime provided many good shoots, ages and becomes too long; it should be cut back on its lower eye during winter pruning. A well-pruned vine produces flowers in June, at which time the growth of shoots must be severely stopped, as they lengthen, attracting most of the sap and causing "coulure": the flowers don't set seed, and the aborted bunches turn into tendrils. Annular incision, recommended as a remedy against coulure caused by untimely rainfall, and condemned by practice in almost all cases, would be of no use here; destroy useless shoots, and also, by disbudding often renewed, at the right time, destroy all open eyes in false buds above the cutting; Without these precautions, there will be little or no harvest; disbud again after the grapes have formed; cut off the ends of the shoots; your bunches will ripen better and the lower eyes of the shoot, on which the winter pruning must be based, will become stronger. This repeated disbudding during the summer months constitutes summer pruning. Thomery method or horizontal cordon pruning. - The best method of vine training is the Thomery method. The vines are planted at Om 55 from each other, and the cordons are spaced Om, 50 apart. To line a 3-metre-high wall, plant five vines to form five cordons; the first cordon will be at Om, 50 from the ground, and the fifth at Om, 50 from the coping. Prune at two eyes above ground level; the upper eye should extend the stem; the lower eye forms the shoot. Fig. 61. - Thomery vine. The first stock forms the first cordon; the second, the second cordon, etc., each cordon will be 2.70 m long, divided into two arms of 1.35 m each. This length is sometimes exceeded, but this is almost always an inconvenience. All vines are pruned in this way: after pruning on two shoots, as mentioned above, the shoots are palisaded; at the next pruning, the bud of the extension is cut, according to its length and strength; the lateral shoot, resulting from the first pruning, is cut on its lower eye, in order to start a spur or fruit-bearing branch. Once you have reached the height of the cordon, wait until the prolona Fig. 62. - Vertical cordon vine. bud has passed the cordon line, and bend it to the right; pinch it at the top; a false bud will then emerge, which you will leave to grow freely, so that it will provide you with the left arm; you will thus have a more or less regular "T". While you're forming your cords, severely remove any unnecessary production from the stem. At the pruning that follows the formation of the T, you will cut both arms of the cordon on a terminal eye below, to extend them, and you will leave to grow only the top buds, which are necessary to you; choose two of the most vigorous at about Om, 16 one from the other; let them develop in freedom; you palisade the terminal buds in the horizontal direction, and those of the top in the vertical direction, but by pinching them by the top as soon as they arrive at the cordon immediately superior. Do the same for the other arm extensions, stopping them at the last spur as soon as they reach the desired length. Each year, you'll still have to prune the cordon spurs; you'll pinch off the shoot resulting from the first bud, and bring it back to the next pruning on the two eyes located furthest below; you'll pinch off the buds as soon as their growth requires it and, if they bear fruit, one leaf above the second bunch. L 6 - - 3 * 2 4 Q ~ 3 E ~ Fig. 63. - Serpentine vine. The following year, completely remove the farthest shoot at its heel, and prune the other on the two lower shoots. If the spur gets madly carried away, take advantage of the bud closest to the arm and cut back on it. - All pruning is done in the same way, so there's no need to go into too much detail here. Pruning shears are to be preferred to any other instrument. When the grapes are close to maturity, water them with a hand pump, expose them a little more to the direct action of the sun by removing a few leaves, and enclose them in paper or horsehair bags eight or ten days before they reach maturity, if they are to be kept on trellises until the frosts. After picking, place in a dry, well-ventilated place and sprinkle with crushed charcoal or sawdust. Always follow the principles briefly outlined above, and grow vines in vertical rows when the layout of the walls to be covered makes it impossible to use the Thomery method, which is undoubtedly the best. Vines planted in ordinary counter-spaliers or arbors are treated in the same way. We should also mention the serpentine shape, etc. (1). The main varieties grown for the table are: - Chasselas de Fontainebleau, with large green or golden-yellow grains, top quality; Chasselas rouge, which takes on color as soon as it is tied, good quality; Royal rosé, very fine; Chasselas hátif; C. de Montauban, sweet and amber; C. de Montauban with transparent grains, very pleasant to the taste, amber and fine; C. musqué; Corinthe blanc with very small, round, yellow, seedless berries, very pleasant to taste; Gromier du Cantal, a beautiful red color and very succulent when ripe; Madeleine blanche, with large, well-filled bunches; Muscat d'Alexandrie or Muscat de Rome, oval, yellow, musky and very fine berries, very warm exposure; Muscat blanc (1) We have already discussed powdery mildew in the chapter on plant diseases; let's just remind you here that triturated sulphur flower is preferable to any other sulphur. or Frontignan, crisp, tightly packed, sweet, musky grapes, white-skinned, espalier; Muscat nain or de la mi-août, the earliest of all Muscats, round, small, black grapes, third quality; Muscat rouge, bright red, musky, but not as good as the previous one; Raisin Verdot, with short, ruddy bunches, fine taste, the best and sweetest of dessert grapes; on trellises, in good exposure; Précoce de Courtillier, with large, sweet, slightly musky berries; ripens in August. We didn't feel it necessary to go into detail about vine propagation by sowing, which is far too time-consuming and is almost never used. We prefer to propagate using marcottes or provins, and often just plant crossettes. Grafting is carried out using: 1° herbaceous grafts on growing shoots; 2° slit grafts on wood that is one or more years old; 3° shuttle grafts (see the chapter on different types of grafts). See also Plant diseases, Harmful insects). GROSEILLIER (Grossulariées). - The ordinary red currant, a small native shrub, has produced a white-fruited variety with a much more delicate taste. Currants thrive in all types of soil and exposure, but do best in soft, sandy, slightly cool soil. It is propagated from seed, cuttings or marcottes in February and autumn, and dead wood and branches are cut back in February. Currants must be replanted every five years, otherwise they lose weight and degenerate. Here are the main varieties obtained through cultivation: the white Dutch gooseberry, with very good, transparent, cherry-sized fruit; the G. cerise, with large, light-red fruit; the G. with pink, flesh-colored fruit; the G. Gondouin with white fruit, with small, acidic berries; the G. Gondouin with red fruit, a late variety with long bunches; the G. Queen Victoria, with large, light-red fruit; the G. Gondouin with red fruit, a late variety with long bunches; the G. Gondouin with red fruit, a late variety with long bunches; the G. Gondouin with red fruit, a late variety with long bunches. Queen Victoria, long bunches, red fruit, good quality; G. hâtive de Berlin, very early; G. rouge de Hollande, long, well-filled bunches, light red fruit, very large and very good; G. Versaillaise, with large bunches and numerous, large, light-red fruits. - Blackcurrant with large black fruit, three varieties: C. with variegated leaves, C. with brown fruit, C. with maple leaves. - Gooseberry, with fruit sometimes as large as an egg, sometimes green, sometimes white, red, purple, etc. Varieties: smooth gooseberry; bristly gooseberry, amber fruit, flesh color, large yellow, green-white, large round; thornless gooseberry (Billard); highly recommendable variety. FRAMBOISIER (Rosaceae). - Native, trailing, biennial stems. This shrub grows in stony places, on mountains, and likes shade and cold. The fruit of the cultivated raspberry is highly sought-after for its flavor and refreshing qualities; it is used to prepare syrups, jams, ice creams, etc.; it gives wine a very pleasant taste; when fermented, it produces an alcoholic liquor; the Poles make an excellent mead from it, and the Russians a kind of wine. Raspberry should be given a special place in the garden, as it is always a bad neighbor, crumbling the soil and harming other plants too close to it. It is content with ordinary soil and half-shade exposure, and needs to be moved every four or five years, unless it is given a little fertilizer every autumn. It multiplies by suckers planted from November to March; in February, dead stems that have borne fruit are removed; pruning again from Om, 60 to one meter. Light ploughing. Just to mention a few: the common raspberry or F. des bois, with yellow fruit. - F. du Chili, with its white-fruited variety. - F. with large white fruits. - F. Gambon or red fruit, elongated and very large. - F. with flesh-colored fruit. - F. des Alpes or F. des deux saisons, with red fruit. - F. des quatre saisons, with large red fruit; the last two remain productive until frost, even in cold countries, if you take care to stuff them in autumn. Falstaff. - F. Belle de Fontenay, red-fruited and remontant. EPINE-VINETTE, VINETIER (Berberidae). - Native, forming a bush 2 to 2.5 meters high, and for this reason very often used as an impenetrable hedge; the red, acidic fruit is prized for jams. Preference is given to varieties with seedless fruit, white fruit, large fruit, purple fruit, purple leaves and sweet fruit. Propagate by marcottes, cuttings, offshoots, seeds. Separate, burst and replant in autumn. Wood and leaves, macerated in an alkaline lye, give a yellow dye suitable for coloring woodwork. The fermented berries produce an acidic liquor that deposits a salt similar to tartar. Vinetier flowers in spring, at the same time as Hawthorn. The very irritable stamens snap to the stigma as soon as they are touched with the tip of a needle. FIGUIER (Morées). - Grows naturally in the southern regions of Europe, in Greece, the Levant, etc., and has been cultivated for centuries. Cultivated for centuries, it has produced and still produces varieties whose number we won't even attempt to determine. It lives indefinitely, if not by its trunk, at least by its roots, from which new stems sprout when the old ones are cut. All it needs is a bit of good soil around its base, watering during hot spells, and the removal of dead branches or those too weak to bear fruit; we pinch off the strongest ones to force them to branch out; some gardeners rightly remove the woody bud from a newborn Fig: Other growers stick the tip of a large pin dipped in olive oil into Figs that have reached two-thirds of their normal size: the air introduced in this way, at the very heart of the fruit, activates the conversion of starch into juice. Fig trees are propagated by offshoots, cuttings, grafts and seeds: seeds are only used to obtain new varieties. Flute grafting is preferred to other types of grafting, but is rarely used, because the tree takes cuttings and suckers very well, and there are always many suckers growing from the base. Fig trees planted in staggered rows in the open ground need to be spaced at intervals of about 4 meters. Fig trees easily provide two harvests each year, one in July, the other in September-October; you shouldn't count too much on the second in Paris's climate. New Figs are pleasant and refreshing, but not very nutritious; dried, they are a great resource for the populations of Mediterranean regions. The ancients made a kind of wine from them, called sicyte (from the name of the Fig, in Greek). The juice of the fig, refined for twelve hours after picking, becomes a delicate syrup. Eaten before it has fully ripened, i.e. before it has begun to wilt, the Fig burns the lips and tongue, and causes indigestion. In addition to the huge consumption of fresh Figs at harvest time, many are dried out by placing them on racks in the sun; they are flattened as they lose their moisture. They are emollient, and are used as poultices to resolve tumors, gargles to soothe sore throats, and herbal teas to combat inflammatory diseases. Fig wood is light and spongy, easily soaked in oil and emery, and used by gunsmiths and locksmiths to polish their work. The milky, corrosive juice of the bark destroys warts, curdles milk and forms a sympathetic ink: characters traced on paper with this juice become visible only when exposed to fire. The Fig tree is remarkable for the singularity of its fruiting: the sexual organs are hidden in this fleshy, pear-shaped receptacle (commonly called fruit), pierced at the top by an umbilicus or small hole in the vicinity of which are the male flowers, while the female flowers can be seen lower down; the ancients didn't believe in the existence of these flowers; Later, they recognized the presence of female flowers in the cultivated Fig tree; but, still convinced that well-developed male flowers were lacking in the wild Fig tree, they resorted to caprification, still advantageously practiced today in many parts of the Levant. Olivier observed caprification in particular on the island of Naxos. Here's what he has to say on the subject: "The growers' methods consist in placing on Fig trees that produce only the second Fig, the species known as Fig flowers or first Figs, which appear and ripen a month and a half before the others. The second Figs ripen in August, and continue without interruption well into autumn. The Greeks thread together ten or twelve of these first Figs and hang them in various places on the Fig tree whose fruit they wish to fertilize. "The cynips psenes, a small black insect, also carries out caprification; it penetrates to the heart of the Figs, emerging covered with the dust of the stamens, which it then carries to the other Figs where it deposits its eggs. If you want a certain number of these Fig trees to bear only autumn fruit, take care to detach the summer Figs when they are as big as the tip of your finger, and cauterize the scars with a little lime or very fine plaster to prevent the milk from leaking out: the branch lengthens, the autumn Figs appear earlier and ripen before the frosts. In the Paris climate, only four or five varieties of Fig are grown, giving: the round white Fig, two seasons old, the best around the capital; the angelic yellow F., yellow with small green dots, pink flesh, not very delicate; the large red F. from Bordeaux, round, red, very good; the Madeleine, white lonque, fairly fine; the long red F. from Provence, reddish flesh, mediocre quality; the violet F., good when ripe. MURIERS (Morées). - From Asia Minor, Persia, China, etc. We can't go into the cultivation of the white mulberry for the sericulture industry, as that would take us too far. Everyone knows that its leaves are the food of silkworms; poultry love to eat its fruits, which humans rightly disdain. The main varieties of this tree are: the white Murier with shiny leaves; the M, hybrid; the M. Moretti; the M. de Tartarie; the M. Lou de Chine; M. Gasparin; M. multicaule; M. intermédia. The Red Mulberry, from North America, 12 to 15 meters high, produces a delicious dark-red fruit. The black mulberry produces an oval, almost black-purple fruit with a pleasant, refreshing taste. It is grown in espaliers or in the open air. As with other Mulberries, it needs shelter from strong winds, and soil that is somewhat mixed with rubble. Mulberries reproduce by seed, marcottes and cuttings. They are grafted onto trunks in the form of escutcheons or flutes. They don't need pruning, just the removal of dead branches. Black Mulberry fruit is used to make: 1° a delicious syrup to soothe inflammation of the mouth and back of the mouth; 2° a very fine vinegar. Its juice is used to color wines, syrups, liqueurs and certain jams. Turners value its wood; its bark is used to make rope and paper; its leaves replace those of the White Mulberry as food for silkworms. CHESTNUT (Amentaceae). - The common chestnut is a large, beautiful tree with polygamous flowers; native to southern Europe, it likes light, sandy, deep soil, and does not do well in water. There are several varieties of chestnut: the C. pourtaionne, with large, numerous fruits; the early C. printanière; the C. verte du Limousin, easy to store; the C. exalade, the best of all; the Marron de Lyon, d'Agen, de Luc, the M. d'Aubray; the latter, the largest of all, is very good, containing a single seed in each shell, instead of two or three; it lacks the tough membrane which, in ordinary chestnuts, marks the inner divisions of the fruit. In Corrèze, the main chestnuts grown are: C. hâtive noire, hâtive de mai, hâtive rousse, Huminaux, Huminaux roux, Carrive and Mastronne. The best Chestnuts are stratified before sowing and protected from frost; in February-March, they are planted in loose, unsmoked soil, Om 08 deep, Om 50 apart, in rays separated from each other by an interval of Om 80, traced in a north-south direction. Light ploughing; hoeing, the following summer, and so on until each individual has reached the right size for the nursery; it is then lifted, put in place, and the lateral branches are cut back. Ridging, trimming with thorns, a little straw or manure around the plant. - The second year, FRUITS ARE Grafted into flutes or buds with dormant eyes: pinching, delimbing. Chestnut trees should be placed 15 to 20 meters apart. The fruit is harvested when it falls off naturally. They should be exposed to the sun for a few days, and stored in a dry place. In some countries, chestnuts are dried in special ovens. Chestnut wood lasts a very long time in water, and is used to make hoops, trellises, etc. WALNUT (Juglandées). - Native to Asia. A large, beautiful tree cultivated for its wood, fruit and oil. Requires sandy or even stony soil. Walnuts are stratified before being sown in place at Om, 06 deep; at Om, 08 apart. Ordinary care of nursery plants. Young trees are grafted in slit, flute or escutcheon with growing eye or ring when they are Om, 10 or Om, 15 in circumference and one or 2 meters high. Ordinary precautions recommended for grafting. The following year, planting in Om, 80 to one-meter deep soil. At twenty years of age, the Walnut tree produces a fairly modest product; at sixty, it reaches its maximum fruitfulness; it supplies about half of all the oil consumed in France. Twenty vigorous walnut trees, in full production, on one hectare of land, can be estimated, without exaggeration, at 2,800 to 3,200 francs. When a Walnut tree comes of age, the tips of its branches dry out and must be immediately pruned back from Om, 70 to one meter from the trunk, if it is to continue to bear fruit; new scions soon form a new head. No pruning as such: just remove dead, broken, misplaced or over-vigorous branches that are detrimental to the overall balance. It is also when the Walnut has shown the signs of old age indicated above, that it should be felled to sell it as wood for joinery, etc.; left standing, it deteriorates very quickly. WALNUT (Amentaceae). - The Hazelnut is a shrub with stout, flexible stems; male catkins in groups of three or four at the same point of insertion, appearing towards the end of winter, well before the leaves. We grow: N. franc, with elongated, very delicate fruit; this species has produced two varieties: one with an almond covered by a red skin, the other with a white skin; - N. avelinier, with larger, less elongated fruit, with the following varieties: Rouge ronde, with a semi-hard shell; Rouge de Provence, with a soft shell; - N. à grappes, with very large, very delicate fruit. Hazelnuts ripen and detach from the tree in August-September. They are preserved like walnuts; they are used to make sugared almonds, and produce a more highly prized oil. They become acrid with age. The wood is used to make stiles, trellises, stakes, forks, etc. Multiplication by seed, sprouting and suckering: place Hazel in a northerly position, at some distance from vigorous trees that would smother it under their dense foliage. It requires no cultivation care, PISTACHIUM (Terebinthaceae). - From Syria. This tree is 7 to 8 meters tall, with strong spreading branches; its flowers are of different sexes, placed on separate individuals, which naturally makes it necessary to cultivate male and female individuals to obtain fruit; these fruits are oval, the size of an Olive, russet in color, wrinkled on the outside, containing a sweet, oily kernel called Pistachio. Through cultivation, the Pistachio tree has produced several varieties, all of which yield almonds of varying degrees of fineness, much sought-after by confectioners, etc.; softening, emollient, etc. They can be eaten raw or used to make sweets, ice creams, creams, etc. The most prized pistachios are the Tunis Pistachio, with its delicate green flesh; pork butchers use the Sicilian Pistachio. The Pistachio terebinth is a beautiful Barbary tree on which the Pistachio franco is often grafted, to enable it to withstand cold temperatures of 8 to 10 degrees C. Multiplication of marcottes, seedlings on a warm bed or under a frame; transplanting into pots. For three or four years, ordinary care given to orangery plants. Pistachio trees need light, open soil, south-facing exposure, espaliered, in the Paris climate; they should be placed 4 meters apart, with one male between three or four females, or better still, a male branch grafted in the middle of the branches of each female. The Pistachier-franc often freezes at 6 degrees below zero. SECTION I. - GRAMINES. AGROSTIS. - Agrostis grows, for the most part, among low turf, in arid and sandy soils; sheep are very avid for it. The largest and most remarkable species of Agrostis is Agrostis jouet des vents, common in our wheat fields. The stem reaches up to a meter in height; rough leaves, long and light panicle, with capillary peduncles, finely branched and loaded with numerous small flowers that end with long deciduous edges. It can be used to dye wool green. Agrostis traçante, or Stolonifère, or Trainasse, or Eternue, must be destroyed in well-cultivated land; but, as fodder, it has real advantages thanks to its active vegetation and its ability to keep fresh for a long time in winter. It thrives in cold, damp, peaty soils. Sow at a rate of around 10 kilograms of seed per hectare in March and September. Agrostis d'Amérique (Herd-Grass) is no more difficult than its predecessor when it comes to choosing a site; it is less successful when planted than when sown, and is an advantage12 in the formation of permanent meadows. Rough but nutritious forage. - Sow in March or September; 5 kg of seeds per hectare. Fig. 64. - Oats. - TALL OAT, FROMENTAL. - This oat needs high, not too damp ground, and the proximity of Sainfoin or some other leguminous plant, otherwise it dries out easily. It rebounds very well in regrowth. - 100 kilog. of seeds per hectare. MEADOW BROME. - If you have a chalky soil unfit even for Sainfoin, etc., etc., give it Meadow Brome, it will do well there; it can last up to fifteen or twenty years without any manuring. Bromegrass succeeds as an ornamental grass in soils where other grasses would quickly wither. Meadow FLOWER. - This plant thrives in clay or sandy soils, where it produces up to 1,000 to 1,200 bales per hectare of coarse but nutritious hay. Sow in September, at a rate of 8 kilograms of seed per hectare. PERENNIAL RYEGRASS. - Used as a mowing plant in temporary meadows on slightly cool ground. It grows back under the very teeth of cattle and becomes more vigorous the more it is grazed and trampled. It is sown in spring or autumn, at a rate of 50 kilograms per hectare for pasture; 100 kilograms per hectare for lawns. Rolling, topdressing. Lasts five or six years on average. Italian ryegrass. - Sods less than the previous variety, but recovers better after cutting and offers great continuity of vegetation. It likes humidity. It can give up to 7 or 8,000 kilograms of good dry fodder per hectare, for several years. Sow in autumn or spring, at a rate of 40 or 50 kilog. per hectare, half with red clover. Bailly ryegrass. - Succeeds in stony, half-burned soils, dry in summer, half-submerged in winter; fertilizer oxen feed on it readily. Sow at 20 to 25 kilograms per hectare. SECTION II. - LEGUMES. FÉVEROLLE. - Sow late February to April, broadcast or in rows. Hoeing. Several varieties: F. de Picardie coarse-grained; F. de Lorraine small-grained. 2 hectoliters per hectare. - Very suitable for preparing wheat harvests in clay soils. Ploughed in green, they are a first-rate fertilizer. CULTIVATED CHASTE OR SPANISH LENTIL. - Strong, light soil, but not too damp. Sow in March and April: one and a half hectoliters per hectare. Chickling is cut green as soon as the first pods ripen; it is fed to livestock, mainly sheep. In the south of France, its seeds are used to make a highly prized purée. LUPIN BLANC. - Good pasture for sheep; seed macerated in water for oxen. Sow in our northern provinces around mid-April, at a rate of 10 to 12 decaliters per hectare. Clay and gravel soils. ALFALFA. - Alfalfa needs well-tilled, very clean, deep soil, fertilized in the year preceding sowing, or at the time of sowing, with consumed fertilizers. Alfalfa is sown on barley or oats in spring, or, in summer, on flax, buckwheat or beans; or, in autumn, with spurge or rye. Hoe, lime and harrow at the end of winter. Fight dodder in May or June by cutting alfalfa plants affected by this parasitic plant to the ground; cover with dry straw and burn Fig. 65. - Field vetch. Cattle should never be allowed to graze on alfalfa still laden with dew or rain. 20 kilograms of seed per hectare. GREY PEA, LAMB PEA, EWE PEA, STRAWBERRY. - This annual plant is particularly successful on fallow land; the early varieties are sown in March; the P. gris d'hiver variety is sown in autumn at a rate of 25 to 26 decaliters of seed per hectare. Good fodder, especially for sheep. Land sown with grey pea should be smoked if it is to be used for wheat. SAINFOIN, ESPARCETTE, BURGUNDY. - Sandy, chalky soils suit it and it improves them. Sow in spring or autumn at a rate of 35 to 45 decalitres per hectare. Sainfoin à deux coupes produces a very abundant second cut, whereas ordinary Sainfoin gives only a rather weak regain; but it requires a less poor soil and a more abundant sowing: about 46 decaliters per hectare. COMMON VETCH. - It can be sown until June on strong, cool soils; its winter variety can be sown in autumn at a rate of 24 to 26 decaliters per hectare, in association with Rye or Oats. This fodder is cut as soon as it flowers and fed to livestock, but only after it has dried out and wilted. SECTION III. - ROOT FORAGES. CAVALIER CAULI (Cruciferae). - Very popular in our western provinces; rich in leaves. - Caulet de Flandre differs from the previous variety in its red hue. - C. Branchu or C. Mille-Têtes, less tall than Chou cavalier, but well furnished with vigorous shoots, is very useful for fertilizing oxen. Once the leaves have been harvested, C. moellier can be fed to livestock, cut into strips. - Perennial C. or C. de bouture is resistant to severe cold; its stems elongate, bend and root. - C. de Lannilis, with its large, blond leaves, provides good fodder for horned animals when cut into strips after the leaves have been harvested. It can withstand the winters of our northern departments. - Northern green kale differs from the preceding species in the way its leaves are cut. It withstands the harshest winters in our climates. In general, cabbages need strong, well-smoked soil. The larger species are sown from March to August in nurseries; set out in April, May, September, and in 12. rows spaced at one meter to Om, 65 apart. Hoeing, weeding. 200 to 250 gr. of seeds for a one-hectare plant. Turnip cabbage, Turneps cabbage, Lapland cabbage. - Sow in April at a rate of one and a half to 2 kilograms of seed per hectare, if sowing in the field. Resists the rigors of cold. Rutabaga cabbage, Swedish turnip. - Similar to Turnip Cabbage. Sow in rows spaced Om, 70 to Om, 80 apart. Can withstand frosts, but does not like humidity. Cabbage-Colza. - Broadcast on wheat stubble, turned over as soon as harvested; 4 to 5 kilograms of seed per hectare. Good green forage in early spring. Cultivation for seed requires more care. Sow on a bed from mid-July to the end of August, weed, transplant about two months later, in well-smoked soil, in rows spaced Om, 32. Replanting. Hoeing. Sometimes broadcast sowing in place. C. de mars is sown in March and April and produces ripe seed that summer. BETTERAVE CHAMPÊTRE, DISETTE (Chénopodées). - Biennial plant; needs soil loosened by one or two deep ploughings; sown broadcast or in rays, from mid-March to May; thin out so that plants are Om, 30 to Om, 50 apart, depending on soil quality. Weeding; hoeing. Seedlings are also sown in nurseries, to be planted when the root is the size of a finger, taking care not to let the tip fold up at the bottom of the hole. The roots are harvested in November and stored in a frost-free cellar. For sowing in rows, about 3 kilograms of seed are needed per hectare; for broadcast sowing, 4 to 5 kilograms; for transplanting, 2 to 2.5 kilograms are sufficient to provide the planting material needed for one hectare. As main sub-varieties, let's mention the Camel Disette; its length is about twice its diameter, with veined flesh. - D. corne de vache (cow's horn) with a very long root, curved and partly out of the ground, CARROT (Umbelliferae). - One of the best forage roots: it needs soil amended with well-consumed fertilizers. Sow from March to May and even June, at a rate of 4 to 5 kilograms per hectare, broadcast and preferably in rays. Light harrowing, rolling, weeding and hoeing. Harvesting takes place towards the end of November or in December. Store in cellars, trenches or frost-free pits. Among the many varieties grown for livestock, we recommend: jaune d'Achicourt, grosse blanche de Breteuil, blanche à collet vert, very productive, blanche des Vosges, whose large, clean roots are easy to store, NAVETS and RAVES (Crucifers). - Great winter resource. Sow late June to early August, broadcast or in rows. Weeding and hoeing, light, dry soil; 2 or 3 kilograms of seed per hectare. Among the best Raves are: Rave d'Auvergne à collet rouge. - Rabioule or Rave du Limousin, improperly called Turneps. - Rave du Norfolk, very good, quite late. - Turneps hâtif de Hollande, quick-growing and very strong-rooted. ALPISTE, Long millet, Birdseed, Canary seed (Grasses). - Good fodder for horses and horned animals. Sow in April in well-smoked soil. OAT-POTATO or Potato Oats. - Good quality, but a little prone to smut. Georgia oats. - Very early and very good for livestock; can be cut green first and left to seed on second growth. Siberian early oats. - Quite similar to the previous variety, but more vigorous. Joannette oats. - Gives good quality black grain, but must be harvested before full maturity. Brie black oats. - Short grain, swollen, early, good quality. Winter oats. - Straw and grain production, early, more successful in western France than in the north and east. Sow in September or January, preferably on dry land. ORDINARY FRUIT, barbless. - Its main varieties are: Blé blanc de Flandre, very beautiful and very productive, with white ears, white grain, oblong and slender. - Blé de Hongrie, white ear, narrow, white grain, very short. - Blé Touzelle or Tuzelle blanche de Provence, white grain, oblong. - B. Richelle white from Naples, very fine white grain. - Blé d'Odessa (Odessa wheat), barbless, red ear, fine straw and bent at the bottom. Sow in autumn or spring. - Blue wheat, hardy, productive, early. Sought-after for meslin and as a March wheat. - Talavera wheat, from Bellevue, with loose white ears. Sow in February or March. - Blé du Cap sans barbes, frankly from March, large grain, tight ear. - Blé de Saumur, a good sub-variety of Blé d'hiver ordinaire, with nourished ears, reddish grain, very white straw and higher than that of ordinary wheat, - Hardy red wheat, good for sowing on cleared clover, - Red March wheat, no beards, long ears. - Sicilian March wheat, grain Fig. 66. - Red, hard wheat of ordinary quality. - Hairy wheat from Crete, red, hairy head, light yellow grain. Sown in spring in Paris climate. Ordinary wheat, bearded. - Main varieties: Saissette de Provence, the best of the Bearded Wheats and perhaps of all the Wheats. - Bearded wheat from Naples, with beautiful elongated grain. - Caucasian wheat, bearded, elongated grain, reddish, hard, heavy. Sow in February. - Cape wheat, March wheat with very long ears, yellowish grain, heavy, good quality. - Ordinary bearded March wheat, early, good quality, frankly March. - Bearded March wheat from Tuscany, which provides the beautiful, fine straw used to make hats. - Victoria wheat, short straw, yellow ear, reddish grain, good quality. - Hedgehog wheat, short grain, small, reddish, heavy, good quality; spring and fall. - March wheat, bearded red, very early, therefore good for late sowing. Blé renflé, Poulard, gros Blé. - Main varieties: Nonette, semi-fine straw, coarse grain; autumn wheat. - Common Rivet wheat, blue ear, very tight, golden yellow grain, medium size, good quality, very rustic and very productive. - Black Petanielle wheat, large, almost round grain, long, hard, strong straw, very productive. - Miracle or Smyrna wheat, with stalked ears, large, rounded, yellowish-white grain; full, hard straw; needs good soil, fears severe cold and degenerates easily. Polish wheat or Russian rye, with large, long ears, very strong bales, elongated, hard, almost transparent grain, good quality, but not very abundant. Sow in spring, or better in autumn. Hard or African wheat. - Main varieties: Trimenia barbu from Sicily, abundant grain, fine straw, but a little hard; needs rich soil. - Aubaine rouge, very similar to the above. Sow in February or autumn. Barbless spelt, very good for fodder; very rustic, successful in cold, mountainous countries. - White bearded spelt, very early, very vigorous, good for spring and autumn sowing. White starch or March spelt. Sow in spring. Engrain commun or Petite Epeautre hardy, successful on siliceous soils. Sow in spring and autumn. Double grain, with rough ears and soft, unglazed grain. Sow in spring and autumn in chalky soils. Blés de mars, Blés de mai. - Blés de mars are early wheat varieties that fear the rigors of winter. Strictly speaking, there are no wheat varieties that, when sown in May, give an assured and complete harvest in the same year. CORN or TURKEY WHEAT, INDIA WHEAT, ZEA. - After rice and wheat, corn is the most useful of all grasses, and also one of the most universally cultivated. It can be sown on fallow land every two or three weeks, from May to mid-July, and will provide excellent green fodder for three or four months. Lines at Om, 55 or Om, 65 apart. Hoeing and weeding. Cut when the male flowers are showing their tips. Dry corn is a useful winter forage. It is estimated, without exaggeration, that the benefits of growing Corn are 50 per 100. SPRING BARLEY OR SPRING BARLEY. - After two-row bare barley, this is the earliest barley. Sows in May and sometimes June. Naked square barley, Celeste barley, Six-row barley. - Highly productive; grain less likely to blacken with rain; straw coarse, but soft and very good as fodder. Good soil. Sow in first spring. Guimalaye or Namto barley. - Short, rounded, greenish grain; short, firm, coarse straw. SARRASIN, BLACK WHEAT, BUCAIL, CARABIN (Polygonaceae). - Gives an abundant grain which man does not disdain and which chickens, pigeons, pigs, horses, etc., feed on very willingly. Sow for CEREALS. seed, from mid-June to early July, at a rate of half a hectoliter of seed per hectare; sow to bury as excellent fertilizer; one hectoliter per hectare. Poor, cold soils. Harvest early to avoid shattering. MARCH RYE. - Sown in March; fairly short but fine straw; very good grain. St. John's rye. - Northern rye with longer straw and ears, with smaller grains than Autumn rye, of which it is a variety. Cut as green fodder in autumn, or left to graze until late winter, to be harvested as grain the following summer. SORGHUM, MILLET. - Grain for chickens and pigeons; stalks provide good green fodder. A late-planting variety that is difficult to select. Sweet sorghum. - Stems 2 to 3 meters high, leaves smooth, flexuous and drooping, panicles of flowers first green, then purple, then crimson, seeds black and shiny. Same cultivation care as for Corn END OF PART ONE. GARDENER'S CALENDAR (1) January. - Fine days are rare at this time of year, under the foggy, cold skies of northern countries; however, you can have : 1° in the open ground, sweet coltsfoot, black hellebore or Christmas rose, Japanese calycanthus, tin laurel, common laurel, red and white laurel or Bois-Gentil, Alpine Iberid; 2° in greenhouses, a few Geraniums, Narcisse de Constantinople, Tulipe Duc de Thol, Clérodendrons, Ruellie à fleurs bleues, Begonias, a few hardy Orchids, some Cape Heather, Richardia, Canna, a few varieties of Rose du Bengale, a few early Camellias. In your hot greenhouses, place: Narcissus, Hyacinths, Daffodils, Cinereas, Chinese Primroses, Parma Violets, Frutescent Chrysanthemums, Lilacs, etc.., (1) This calendar was drawn up for the climate of Paris; it must therefore be modified if applied to the south of France; you must also take into account the changes in temperature that occur every year, and bring forward or postpone sowing, depending on whether the season is early or late. 19 transplant them, lining them with gravel, rubble, coarse gravel, fine gravel or river sand; destroy worn-out lawns with deep ploughing, and prepare the places where you will plant your Rosages, Bengals and Heather in March, in clumps or beds. From time to time, remove the litter from your herbaceous plants, but don't expose them to the still frequent frosts. February. - In the open ground, you'll have : Daisies, a few sweet-smelling violets, snowdrops, herbaceous heather, hill daphne, spring saffron and its many varieties, helleborine, small periwinkle, rosemary, gardeners' corchorus, hepatica and so on. In greenhouses, Jasmine, Peonies, Purple Cinerea, Camellia, variegated Oxalis, several Corréas, Ethiopian Richardia. Remove dead wood and harmful branches; plough your groves and flowerbeds, using a forked hoe rather than a spade, for fear of cutting the roots; sow your lawns; line the pits intended for your March rosages with heather soil; your flowerbeds will receive Julienne, Giroflée, Soleil vivace, Astère, Poetic Carnation and a few other perennials and biennials that you won't have to worry about in autumn. If you're no longer afraid of heavy frosts, plant your borders with Sage, Lavender, Hyssop, Daisy, Mignardise, Boxwood, etc., and place them in borders or posts. If the sun shines and heats up a little, carefully open some of your frames and renew the air in your greenhouses; hoe the soil in your pots, water, remove dead leaves; wage war on insects. March. - In the open ground you'll have: Sorbs, a few Spireas, some Thorns and Alisiers, several heathers, the dwarf Almond tree, the satin Almond tree, the A. Georgia, Andromeda, Tulips, Narcissus, 1ris, Hornbeam, Primrose, Spring Cynoglossa, Bear's Ear, Spring Orobium, Saffron, Arbouria, Leucoïum, Iberides, Double-flowered Populus, Sylvie and Apennine Anemones, and so on. Thanks to the forced heat, greenhouses and frames will easily give you several Acacias, Roses, Diosmas, Carmantines, Indigotier austral, Heather, Alisier de la Chine, Sparmannia d'Afrique, Camellias, etc., etc... Complete all your ploughing and planting of trees, shrubs and perennials, reserving only green and resinous trees for April. Rake and sand your driveways, keep an eye on your lawns, and follow up your autonine seedlings with borders, clumps or beds of annual flowers such as: Larkspur, Reseda, Poppy, Poppy, Mahon's Wallflower, etc. Sow on beds: Quarantaines, Zinnies élégantes, Balsamines; lay Dahlia tubers bare on a bed, cover them with a frame; when the heat brings out the buds, divide the clumps and put them in pots, but continue to keep them on a bed or at least under a frame; remove your diseased orange trees and other plants and revive their roots by laying them bare in the soil of the bed. Start taking cuttings under cloche and marcottes; protect your greenhouses with a light canvas, where young shoots have to fear the sometimes scorching rays of the March sun. April. - In the open ground : Bear's Ears, Primroses, Anemones, Narcissus, some Tulips, Corydalis, Bulbous Fumitory, European and Asian Trollius, Pansies, Chinese Glycine, Paulownia, False Ebony, Lilacs, Double-blooming Cherry and Cherry Trees, Cytises, Garden Coronilla; in greenhouses and under frames, Rhododendrons in trees, Indian Azaleas and the rest of the Camellias, many of which were in flower the previous month, wage war on caterpillars and other insect pests; clean your trees and shrubs; mow your lawns; plough your flowerbeds and beds. Your field-sown annuals are in full bloom; save your châsssis for sowing tropical seeds, or for weak or diseased plants. Cut under cloche, graft on approach, etc., give air to the greenhouses; multiply your watering as the heat increases and vegetation develops. May. - It would be superfluous to name all the many and varied flowers that have given this month its name: Bengal Roses, May Hazelnuts or Cinnamon, display their beauty and herald the thousands of Roses that will soon be in full bloom. From May 10 to 15, put your Dahlias in place, if you no longer fear frost; hoe your flowerbeds and beds; remove weeds from your lawns and mow them. Carry out your repottings instead of postponing them until autumn; take cuttings under cloches and make herbaceous, English and approach grafts. Towards the middle of the month, take out orange trees, and, at the end of the month, all orangery plants. Clear your greenhouses of their frames; place your heathers facing east, but keep them out of direct sunlight; provide soft light for Protées and Diosmas. June. - Roses are opening and spreading their fragrance; Dahlias are blooming. Flowers everywhere that it would be pointless to enumerate. Water, hoe; dry out your paths; mow your lawns; stake Asters, Primrose Roses, Dahlias, etc., stiles for Cobeas, Convolvulus, Clematis and other climbers. Leave weak or diseased plants in your unoccupied beds, bare or in pots, for varying lengths of time. Protect plants kept in greenhouses from excessive sunlight. Take cuttings under cloche and approach grafting, as in the previous month. July. - If June brings with it the most beautiful and fragrant Roses, July still has Bengale-Bourbon and Noisette, the King's Rose, Perpetual Roses, Hopper Roses, Dahlias, etc., not to mention many foreign shrubs to which the heat of this month is favorable. Raking, pruning, watering, autumn flower nurseries, marcotting carnations, separating onion cloves - there's plenty to keep you busy. Shelter your chests, frames, etc., keep a layer for sowing seeds in warm countries, and for your sick or delicate greenhouse plants. Re-tan your layers if you weren't able to do so in June. August. - We continue to enjoy Perpetual Roses, King Roses, Bengal Roses, etc., Asters, Dahlias, etc., to which we add Petunias, Verbena, Clematis, Phlox, Perennial Sun, Matricaria, Calf's Mufles, Julibrizin, Japanese Privet, Constantinople Acacia, Chinese and Virginia Bignones. In temperate greenhouses, Gesneria, ŒEchmea, Tillandsia, Stéphanotis, Passiflora, Fuchsia, Polygala Cordata, Metrosideros, Melaleuca, Lagerstrœmia, etc., etc., shine. At the beginning of this month, if you haven't been able to do so in July, lift all your autumn annuals into rootballs and put them in place: marigolds, daisy queens, balsamines, etc. Sow in place: Poppies, Poppy, Bluets, Adonis, Larkspur, Thlaspis, etc.; re-pot plants whose pots have remained buried during the summer, re-cutting roots and branches that have become too long; if you leave these plants in the shade, their vegetation will be re-established when you return them to the greenhouse or orangery. September. - This month's most beautiful and abundant flowers include: Cinereas, Amaryllis-Belladonna, Autumn Colchicum, Sarretes, Goldenrod, Silphium, Coreopsis and other radiata; always Asters, Dahlias, Oenotheras, Petunjas, Balsamines, Queen Daisies, Indian Carnations, Clarkias and so on. Same care and cleanliness as in previous months; in addition, watch the seeds and harvest them as they ripen; sow Quarantaines for transplanting on soil or in crates; sow Renoncules, Anémones, etc. If you need to make considerable changes to your gardens, choose this time of year: the soil transported will settle sufficiently before the next planting. Towards the middle of the month, you'll bring in your warm greenhouse plants, and finish repotting your temperate greenhouse and orangery plants; moderate arrangements in the morning rather than in the evening. Replace panels on tarpaulins, frames and temperate greenhouses at the end of the month. October. - You don't have to say goodbye to flowers just yet, because you've still got them: Roses de Berigale, Noisette; Muscades; Scarlet sage; Dahlias; Late gladioli; Asters; Phlox; Datura; Erica; Zinnia; Menziesia polyfolia; Hélianthus; XimenesiaEnceloïdes; Cosmos bipenné; Capucine; Hibiscus speciosus, militaris, roseus, palustris; Œnothera tetraptera, odorata; Tamarix gallica; Symphoricarpus; Chélone campanulata, rosea, glabra; Véronica præalta; Plumbago; Fuschia; Begonia discolor: - if you remembered to put these plants in the ground in May. Carry out your planting and planting out, finish your paths, collect dead leaves, remove the stems of perennials that no longer flower: Balsamines, Indian Carnations, Dyer's Coréopsis, Queen Daisies; in your cleaned, ploughed and smoked flowerbeds, plant, without delay, Scabieuses, Campanulas, Greek Valerian, Poetic Carnations, Calf's Mufles, etc., which will give you their flowers in spring, which will give you their flowers in spring; bring in large Giroflées before the frosts. Around October 15th, orange trees and orangery plants will be brought in and arranged so that those plants that do not need light (rose laurels, orange trees) form the back of your greenhouses, leaving room in front of them for evergreen, woody, herbaceous plants that like air and daylight; plough pots and crates, water moderately; graft in slits and cut Camellias under cloches, unless you reserve this operation for spring. Remake your layers of tannin in the hot greenhouses, giving them a thick bed of new manure or leaves as a base, and letting them build up their great fire and settle properly. Push the pots right up to the edge into this tannin, the larger ones at the back, the smaller ones at the front. November - If flowers are now becoming increasingly scarce, look after the ones you have left all the better: Bengal roses, Indian Chrysanthemums capable of withstanding four or five degrees of cold, the latter, potted, will last a long time in the temperate greenhouse. Collect dead leaves to cover delicate plants and make compost. Replace wilted annuals with perennials; plant 1° most of your ornamental trees, reserving resinous trees and heather shrubs for spring; 2° from November 1st to 15th, Narcissus, Hyacinth and Tulip Onions, etc. Monitor the temperature of your greenhouses and renew the air carefully. Water and keep clean. December. - Despite the cold, you will still be able to have, at the foot of the walls, Violette odorante; in sheltered places, Hellébore noir or Rose de Noël, Chimonanthus flagrans and its variety, Jasmin à fleur jaune; under frames come: Violette de Parme, Tulipe duc de Thel, Jacinthes blanches; in greenhouses: Cyclamens, Narcisses, Jacinthes, if you have taken care to plant them in October. Ripping for new lawns, repairing paths, pruning. Maintain the temperature of hot greenhouses at 10 to 20 degrees above zero, renew the air, and water around midday: if you squirt the water on your plants in the form of rain, you'll get a steam that's favorable to the development of vegetation. The same temperature applies to the pineapple greenhouse, where you can also place pots of Strawberries of the Four Seasons, Comte de Paris and Princesse Royale on the shelves. All you have to do is not let the thermometer fall below O degrees, but let the sun shine in at 5 to 10 degrees, and take advantage of the moment when it shines to renew the air and expel humidity by carefully opening the frames and windows, which you will close again before nightfall. There's no need to water large boxes of orange, pomegranate and rose-laurel in winter. Cover and mat the glass roof and the front of the windows, day and especially night, to prevent frost. Parterre. - Borders. - Lawns. - Assortment of flowers of various colors; laws to be observed in this respect. - Bedding plants, Fancy plants, Collection plants. - Parterre accessories. The Parterre or florist's garden is particularly intended for outdoor, hardy or semi-hardy plants, worthy of cultivation for their fragrance, their beauty, the brilliant shine of their corollas. The Parterre should never be too large; otherwise, the eye would not be able to take in the whole and the main details at the same time. Place it close to your home, so that it can be seen from the windows of your apartments. In northern countries, where the sky is often dark and rainy, you'll prefer the parterre to face south, south-east or south-west; never north. The proximity of tall trees, high walls and monumental greenhouses is detrimental to the parterre; at most, leave a few clumps of trees near the parterre to shelter it from the cold winds. In southern climates, follow instructions that are in many ways contrary to the above; remember, as a general rule, that it's enough for flowers to receive the sun's rays for a few hours each morning and evening. Rainwater should not be allowed to remain in the flower garden; the best soil is an average soil composed of roughly equal parts of ar ́ N C AHStI alllolan E 18 * M VIEI Fig. 1. - Parterre. gileous substance, calcareous substance and siliceous substance; this soil should be very loose and not subject to hardening or cracking in the sun. Use guano and odorless fertilizers. For very special crops, you'll need to set aside squares where the soil will offer the most suitable elements for the flowers you want to plant; primroses need 13. clay soil; Kalmias and Rosages need heather soil; bulbous plants need silica, etc. Sometimes the Parterre is flat throughout, sometimes it offers artistic reliefs rising from Om, 10 to Om, 15 and even to Om, 25 above the alleys; these reliefs must form gentle, rounded slopes from their perimeter to the center. Sometimes the parterre is shaped like a square, rectangle, trapezium, etc., sometimes it presents an irregular figure bounded by curved lines, with islands of flowers amid green lawns; unless you need to preserve the very style of an antique garden or constructions of some kind, avoid stiff, purely geometric shapes. Straight or curved paths determine the design of the parterre; in the largest gardens, they are rarely more than 4 meters wide; in the smallest, one meter is enough; they are sanded or covered with a layer of gravel one centimeter thick. For edging paths and walkways, we recommend: 1° Boxwood, planted in rooted splinters in a single row, 7 to 8 centimetres apart; allow it to reach an average height of around Om, 12 to Om, 18 by 6 to 8 centimetres thick, or even more, depending on the size of the parterre, the relief of the compartments to be bordered, etc.; 2° Thyme, planted in a single row, 7 to 8 centimetres apart.2° Thyme, large and small Periwinkle, double-flowered Daisy, Strawberry, Olympus Grass, Ivy, etc. Dry borders are those made of wood, iron or brick. Wooden edgings are usually made from thumb-sized strips of Oak, Beech or Hornbeam. Bricks should be well-fired, very hard, Om.25 by Om.60 c. long by Om.07 thick. Iron borders have the disadvantage of oxidizing rather quickly, despite the layers of minium. Lawns are made of a common grass that is allowed to grow quite high, and to which less care is given than to Turf; the latter is better suited to gardens of little extent. Never cut your lawns or turfs abruptly, by your flowerbeds whose true place is at the sides or towards the middle of the carpet of greenery, but on condition that you do not destroy the symmetry and overall view. You can establish your lawns by sowing or transplanting. Sowing is done by broadcasting on soil that has been well loosened, levelled and smoked; then you tread and water. Among our common grasses, preference is given to perennial species, which grow tall and fill out the ground well. Meadow grass (Poa pratensis), Timothy (Phleum pratense), Cynosure (Cynosurus cristatus), Sweet vernal grass (Anthoxanthum odoratum), Sheep's fescue (Festuca ovina), Perennial ryegrass (Lolium perenne), Bentgrass (Agrostis canina and A. vulgaris); but we rightly recommend rejecting Cocksfoot (Dactylis glomerata), Bromes (Bromus erectus), etc.., Hoes (Holcus mollis and H. lanatus), large oats (Avena elatior). For transplanting, we take lawn tiles from the meadow and lay them, with all the soil adhering to their roots, on previously loosened and smoked soil; we carefully adjust them, roll them and water them. There are two main ways of distributing plants in a parterre: 1° Mixing species; 2° Growing or bedding flowers of the same species. In the mixing mode, remember that a parterre does not admit tall trees, and that simple shrubs two to three meters high are generally better on the sides than inside the compartments; a plant that is too large must not mask a smaller plant. The matching of flower colors requires special attention and a taste that nature inspires, but which the rules can leave a little guesswork. Especially in France, the harmonic arrangement of flowers is far too neglected in terms of color. The simple or primitive colors from which all the others are formed are red, yellow and blue. If you combine them two by two, they will give you orange from red and yellow, green from yellow and blue, violet from blue and red; mixing primitive colors two by two gives an unlimited number of intermediate shades between the darkest and the lightest. The complementary color is that which, added either to a combination of colors or to a single color, reconstitutes the triad of elementary colors; thus orange, derived from red and yellow, is complementary to blue; violet, composed of red and blue, is complementary to yellow; green, composed of blue and yellow, is complementary to red. Certain colors enhance, accentuate and nuance each other through their proximity. As a general rule, remember that: 1° All simple colors contrast happily with each other; 2° The binary combination of compound colors, presenting in each group the combination of three simple colors, is pleasing to the eye; for example, violet (red and blue) blends very well with orange (red and yellow) and green (yellow and blue); 3° Colors that are complementary to each other offer very attractive contrasts: blue and orange, violet and yellow; 4° White lends great relief to all colors, simple or compound, and softens the clash between colors that are naturally opposed to each other: red and orange, violet and red, blue and violet; 5° Black weakens the effect of all colors placed in too direct contact with it, with the exception of white; 6° Never bring simple colors closer to the composite colors in whose formation they enter; yellow contrasts unfavorably with orange (yellow and red) and with green (yellow and blue); blue with violet (red and blue) and with green (yellow and blue); red with orange (yellow and red) and with green (yellow and blue), and so on., etc. We've often seen binary and ternary combinations try out in flower gardens, rarely quaternary ones. The best binary combinations are obtained: 1° by contrasting white with all single or compound colors: bright yellow and white, orange and white, violet and white, green and white, pink or red and white, light blue or dark blue and white; - 2° by using single colors together or with their complementaries: yellow and blue, yellow and violet, orange and blue, red and yellow, red and blue, etc. Ternary combinations, where white almost always enters, are more varied than binary combinations: - white, yellow, blue and white; - white, yellow, green and white; - white, orange, white and violet; - white, orange, green and white; - white, red, blue and white; or white, red, white and blue; - yellow, red, white and yellow; - blue, orange, blue and white; or white, orange, white and blue, etc., etc., etc. The main bedding plants are: Balisiers or Canna. Caladium with green or colored leaves. Wigandia Caracasana. Juniperus and Biota. Cupressus sempervirens, etc. Retinospera. Ferns (Onoclea, Struthiopteris). Heather (Arborea, Scoparia, etc.). Tree Ricins. Broad-leaved Colocases. Rhubarb. Ferulas. Verbesina. Polymnia. Gunnera Scabra. Palms (Chamœrops humilis, Ch. Excelsa), small date palms. Japanese medlar (Triobotrya). Rose Laurel. Myrtles. Lagerstrœmia. Cocculus laurifolius, Agave. Passiflora, etc. Plants are divided into Fancy Plants and Collection Plants. Fancy plants must be hardy and easy to grow; for the most part, they are annuals or cultivated as such; they are almost always reproduced from seed; sowing is done in the field or in a nursery; the finer the seeds, the less soil they should be covered with (1).
