Parterre accessories are the various things that, without being necessary or really part of the garden, add to its pleasure or facilitate its upkeep, such as water fountains and basins, nurseries, benches, boxed trees, vases, fences, and so on. Don't make your fountain spin a tin dervish, or your pond carry badly-painted vessels: a water lily and goldfish will adorn it much better; no gilding on the seats; let them offer the means to stretch out comfortably and rest. As far as possible, and with a few exceptions established in practice, allow the trees in your boxes to grow naturally; it's enough for them to be cramped at the base, let the head spread out freely. Antique-shaped white vases are preferable to ugly pots laden with fantastic flowers which, even if well-painted, have too formidable rivals in your parterre itself. The nursery should be kept out of sight of visitors; it should contain all the utensils and equipment necessary for sowing, bedding, cuttings, grafting, etc., etc., etc., etc. It must be kept extremely clean. Landscaped gardens; their composition. - Rivers, streams, lakes, water features. - Paths and driveways. - Cuttings, embankments, artificial valleys and hills. - Tree and shrub distribution, etc. Fashion has caused the composition, arrangement and organization of landscape gardens to vary from century to century. If, in this area as in all others, beauty did not have its fixed rules, the simplest notions of taste and harmony would have been lost; On the one hand, French gardens sinned by an excess of symmetry; on the other, English gardens displayed a disorder that was not always an effect of art, nor an imitation of nature; moreover, this very imitation must have as its object only the beautiful and not the monstrous, the strange or the grotesque. Don't place, over an area of a few meters at the entrance to a middle-class house, confused masses of stones piled one on top of the other, under the pretext of representing rocks; don't dig under the feet of the over-confident walker holes of varying widths and depths which, in your opinion, will represent precipices, etc. Our fathers didn't admit the use of the word "precipice" in the French language. Our forefathers were accustomed to straight lines and flat surfaces; large areas of rough terrain were converted into terraces with rigorously geometric contours. Any tree, no matter how precious or ancient, disappeared if it had the misfortune to find itself out of Fig. 2. - Landscaped garden. alignment; in a word, our fathers proceeded in the construction of their vast landscaped gardens in a manner analogous to that of an all-too-famous modern aedile, whose love for the inflexible straight line will be attested to in future ages by the new Paris; but the Lenôtre, etc., thanks to their good taste, their skill and their ability to adapt to the new situation, were able to create their own landscaped gardens, thanks to their good taste and grandiose conceptions, created true marvels by means of processes that were in themselves ungrateful: the Gardens of Versailles, for example. Today, we prefer the picturesque effect to everything else; we're trying to get closer to nature; that's all well and good, but we mustn't conceal from ourselves the difficulties to be overcome. First of all, it's important to know all the details, all the accidents of the terrain on which you're operating; the general effect of the site, its natural resources, the objects to be preserved, their relationships with each other, deserve a great deal of attention. You draw your plan on paper, then on the ground. Milestones and stakes indicate the artificial hills and valleys, large and small elevations, the place of woods, copses and massifs; no steep, abrupt lines, but curves joined by gentle transitions; the capital S and half-circumferences are overused; good taste and the accuracy of the eye often replace the compass, an instrument that is always a little pretentious. If you have wide watercourses, trace or boldly modify their edges; don't let the detours be too close together; for narrow watercourses, you can multiply their sinuosities. Water is essential to a landscape garden; it gives it life. If your artificial river is 2 or 3 meters wide, you'll do well to divide it into two more or less equal arms, so as to form a small oblong island well planted with flowers, with a few rocks crowned with green trees or shrubs, with branches hanging down into the water: weeping willow, etc., etc... Artificial water features should be laid out in such a way that at least one of their edges is uncovered, allowing the view to extend over the entire body of water. On the shores, plant tall, isolated trees and low shrubs here and there. If the shores are too steep, boaters will be deprived of any perspective. There should always be a sufficient difference in level between the pond and the water outlet, so that the pond can be easily emptied when it's time to clear the water or fish in large quantities. In parts of the garden where you can easily bring in water, in basins or in tubs full of water, grow: Arundo donax; Typha; Lythrum; Butomus umbellatus; Spirea venusta and ulmaria; Epilobium spicatum rose and blane; PIris pseudo-acorus; Myosotis palustris; Caltha palustris flore pleno, etc.; Salix schubertea; Caltha palustris flore pleno.Salix schubertea; Cordamine pratensis flore pleno; Alisma flotant; Fléchière aquatique; Trèfle d'eau; Macre flottante; Nénuphar blanc, etc. If, with the help of a few millstones or other stones that retain moisture easily, you create an artificial rock, plant in the interstices : Cynoglossumomphalodes; Pulmonaria Virginica; Phalaris rubané, Marsh marigold; Trollius, Alyssum-saxatile; Crucianella odorata et stylosa; Alchemilla alpina; Doronicum caucasium; Ononis natrix; Rubus speciosus; Primula veris; Hepatics, Dianthus deltoides; Gypsophylla striata; Sedum sempervivum, Saxifraga géranoïdes; Lotus corniculatus flore pleno; Thymus serpillum; Thymus tomentosum; Narcissus poeticus; N. pseudonarcissus; Veronica-prostrata, etc. Populate your lakes and ponds with carp, tench, barbel, etc., and put goldfish in small basins. Bridges are built of wood, iron or stone; sometimes they are flat, sometimes domed; they must always retain a rustic appearance; ashlar abutments are preferable. Suspension bridges are becoming increasingly fashionable, but their elegance often detracts from their solidity. Alleys should not be so tortuous as to tire the walker; their edges should always be parallel to each other, whatever the shape and multiplicity of the circuits. Make the paths intended for cars 5 to 8 meters wide, and don't make them too winding; pedestrians need to be able to see crews and riders coming from afar. It's not in a landscaped garden that a coachman should have to show his skill at turning obstacles. Make pedestrian walkways 2.50 to 4 meters wide, so that several people can walk abreast, chatting, instead of being obliged to follow one another, as in a procession. A width of Om, 90 to 1m, 30 is sufficient for paths. If you can establish or preserve a few sunken paths, never incline their side slopes by more than 45 degrees; cover these slopes with Honeysuckle, Clematis and other shrubs or saplings with sarment stems; here and there, plant Hawthorn, Blackthorn, Dogwood. In general, remember: 1° don't over-multiply your paths, otherwise your tree beds and copses will look too thin and fragmented, and the picturesque effect will be destroyed; 2° don't let your paths end at the garden fences; 3° that the paths leading to the property's exit gates should only reach them via skilfully designed detours, so that the exits can only be seen from a short distance away; otherwise you'll destroy the illusion you've sought to create about the real extent of your garden. Once the layout has been completed, the earth and stones removed for the digging of lakes, basins, streams, etc., are immediately used for the artificial elevations, valleys, hills, etc. Assign a place in advance for the earth and stones to be removed. Assign a place for these materials in advance; organize the dumper service well, for the digging of bodies of water and valleys; always start by clearing a sufficient space for the dumper to circulate. When digging an artificial valley, proceed with transverse trenches in the direction of the width of the planned valley, rather than with random trenches; this way, you can easily follow the irregularities of the open line before your eyes, and the cross-section of the still-intact terrain will guide you as to the depth of the excavation. If you want to create a hill on level ground, place it not in the center, but at one of the ends, and let other well-planned undulations bring this hill into harmony with the garden as a whole. Leave the added soil to rest for a while, and then give the artificial hill its outward appearance, with plantings, pathways, etc.: the settling that occurs after these steps will have a positive effect on the garden. The settling that occurs after this work has been carried out will upset the hill from top to bottom. Tree distribution. - Here again, it's important to imitate nature as much as possible, which is never ridiculously symmetrical. There should be no overly fixed lines in your flowerbeds; no clashes in the contrast offered by trees of different greens; no trees whose strength of vegetation would so far outweigh the strength of vegetation of neighbouring trees, that the latter would soon wither away for lack of air, light and space. Each tree has its own particular kind of beauty: the Oak spreads its broad, rounded crown majestically; the Fir soars proud and bold; the Elm is both graceful and regular; the Poplar, noble and elegant. In general, Conifers have a serious, sad appearance, but their greenery withstands winter, and the snow that then covers their branches is very picturesque. Plant fir trees in groups of five, six or even ten; not far from them, plant trees of a lighter green. Here are a few tree associations that always succeed in favorable soil: Samaria Elm and Cytise, both with trifoliate leaves; Beech, Elm, Birch and Hornbeam. - False Acacia (Robinia), Japanese Varnish (Alianthus glandulosa). - Gleditschia and Sumae, etc., etc. Softwood trees recover with difficulty, or not at all, when planted after their fourth year; if they are over Om.03 or Om.04 in diameter, even if they are only three years old, nine times out of ten they die within a few weeks or months. A transplanted pine never acquires the vigor of one that grows in the place where it formed its pivot. Larch can be planted after two years in the nursery. Elm, Ash and Sycamore need only be nursed for one year. Oak, Beech and Chestnut need only spend two years in the nursery, provided they have been transplanted at one year old into deep, rich soil. Ornamental trees are planted in spring or autumn; the latter is generally preferable when the soil is of mediocre or poor quality and the plants have little strength; otherwise, it's better to choose spring. If a large tract of land is involved, planting should not be carried out simultaneously: light, dry soil should be planted in December-January; strong, moist soil in February-March; resinous trees, at the foot of which as much of their native soil as possible should be preserved, should be planted in the second half of April, if spring is far enough advanced. Care should be taken to compress the soil around the root collar. We recommend soaking the roots in a mixture of cow dung mixed with water. Stakes, thorn or iron surrounds, etc. List of some of the main trees and shrubs that go to make up a Landscape Garden. Constantinople Acacia. Alaterne, evergreen tree. Alisier (tree 8 to 10 meters tall). Sea Buckthorn, with silvery foliage and thorny branches, making it ideal for hedges. Alder, very easy to grow in swampy areas, where it can reach heights of 20 meters, braving both heat and cold; it forms coppices that are exploited after seven or eight years of growth. Birch, 14 to 16 meters high, grows without deformities or knots, growing only flexible, hanging, tapering branches at the top. It thrives in arid, stony soils. The Catalpa, 10 meters high, native to America. The Common Hornbeam, so well-suited to forming graceful, thick hedges. Oak, king of our forests. Cypress, 10 to 12 meters high. Cytise des Alpes, with its beautiful clusters of hanging flowers. Maple and Sycamore, trees of great picturesque effect. Ash, tall, with a straight trunk, well-proportioned in its size, ending in an elegant but somewhat loose crown; it fears neither shade nor the proximity of other trees, which it quickly overtakes. Flowering Ash, etc., etc. Juniper (Juniperus), a hardy shrub, bristling with hard, sharp, very prickly leaves, with misshapen, twisted twigs, gathered into bushes, with a wild appearance; it is suited to arid, stony sites. The Beech, with a very straight, very large trunk; 30 meters high; vast crown; thick, glossy, light-green foliage; slightly pendulous branches; rivals the Oak itself; suitable for isolation. Holly, not very tall, with supple, bending branches, white flowers and bright, cheerful red fruit; it keeps its beautiful foliage and bright fruit even in the harshest winter weather. Magnolia trees, evergreen, with very large, beautiful flowers; the large-flowered M., which grows to 8 and 10 meters. - Deciduous Magnolias, all of which can grow in the open ground; the Yulan Magnolia, the Cordata Magnolia, with very large and beautiful flowers.

greenish-yellow, etc. Horse chestnut, remarkable for its magnificent foliage and graceful flowers. The Larch, with its thin, narrow, cheerful green leaves, arranged in rosettes along the branches. Micocoulier or Celtis, 15 to 16 meters high, with a noble habit and numerous, well-spread branches. Walnut, which should be placed at a suitable distance from other trees, which it would rather kill than support. Poplar, which has such a beautiful effect along streams and ponds. Pine, content with dry, arid, sandy soil, where no other tree could succeed. The Platane d'Orient, with its superb foliage and rounded green head, spreads shade and coolness all around it. It's one of the most beautiful and suitable trees for forming avenues and bordering large clearings in parks. Robinia or False Acacia, with clusters of elegant, fragrant white or pink flowers. Sumac, with winged leaves and bright red berries; dry, stony places. Elder, very fast-growing. Lime, ideal for shady alleys. It is less common in the modern landscape garden than in the old French garden. Japanese varnish (Ailanthus), 20 meters high and fast-growing. Picturesque accessories. - A landscape garden should be enough to please the eye, without borrowing anything from architecture; however, it does not reject artistic decorations, provided they are appropriate, well-understood and in the right proportions. If you find some ancient ruins on your property, preserve them with care, framing them in the landscape. If you're building a few pretty temples to rural divinities, to Pan, Flora, Pomona or the Seasons, prefer Greek architecture to Roman architecture, as it is purer and more graceful. It's better to copy a familiar model than to expose yourself to the ignorance and bad taste of many modern architects who, under the pretext of inventing new orders or adorning old ones, make a ridiculous mix of different styles. Never allow pagodas or Chinese pavilions; the elegance of some of their details does not prevent the bad effect of their whole. Since the last Paris exhibition, some large landscaped gardens have featured remarkable reductions of Moorish buildings, more suited to the climate of southern France than to our northern provinces. The Romanesque, Gothic and Renaissance styles are the only ones suitable for private chapels; reserve the Greek and Roman styles for temples dedicated to the pagan divinities mentioned above. Obelisks are still allowed in landscaped gardens, provided they are well-cut and well-proportioned; pyramids are ridiculous. Isolated columns may be whole or truncated; in the former case, they are topped with busts or statues; in the latter, they are topped with urns in antique form, with a few inscriptions, preferably borrowed from poets who have sung of the joys and brevity of life: Horace, Anacreon, etc. The statues must be few in number, but of truly remarkable craftsmanship. To the Faun, the grove and the thick thicket full of coolness and shade; to the Nymph, the limpid, murmuring water, the pond, the Weeping Willows, the Water Lilies, etc., etc., etc., etc. A simple but clean thatched cottage, well isolated in clumps of trees where birds sing and nest, and whose path the unwelcome visitor could not easily find, forms a pleasant contrast to the magnificent châteaux, and has always seemed preferable to those pretentious pavilions, with facades adorned with moldings, with windows trimmed with yellow, red, white glass, etc. A few antique marble vases, garnished with flowers rather than left empty, are a fitting ornament for the landscape garden as well as the parterre (1). The first deals with horticultural architecture: construction of greenhouses, orangeries, layout of gardens and parks. Liebeck. Landscape gardens. Atlas of 24 plans with explanations, 1858. SECTION ONE. - CRYPTOGAMES. 1st CLASS, Acotyledons. - Plants lacking embryos, cotyledons, stamens, pistils and true seeds, but having reproductive bodies called spores: in this first class, only the following are cultivated as ornamental plants: 1° Ferns of our climates: cool, shady places; exotic ferns: special greenhouses. Among the most common are: Adiantum, Gymnogramma, Osmunda, Polypodium, Pteris; 2° Lycopodiaceae, as ornaments for hot greenhouse rock gardens; as borders: Selaginella, etc. SECOND SECTION. - PHANEROGAMS. Brief description and cultivation of ornamental plants in alphabetical order. A ABACA, textile banana from the f̂les Philippines, V. Musa. ABELIA rupestris, ABELIE DES ROCHERS (Caprifoliaceae). - Dedicated to Dr. Abel Clarke. Shrub with glossy green leaves; fragrant white flowers arranged in a similar way to those of the Honeysuckle. Light soil; temperate or cool greenhouse. Three species: one from China, the other two from the Himalayas. Multiplication by smothered cuttings. ABIES, SAPIN (Conifers). - Name given particularly to a species of the Pinus genus. Pinus Abies is a resinous tree that grows very tall, although its roots are proportionately very small. Leaves are smooth green above and glaucous green below; male and female flowers are placed separately on the same individual; the fruit is called Cone, hence the name Conifer given to these trees. Fir wood is highly prized as firewood, for masting, etc.; it is fairly resistant to decay and rot; its maximum growth, in favorable soil, is 115 years; 76 years in poor soil. Fir does not do well in chalky soils, where it is better to plant Scots Pine and Maritime Pine. - Propagation by grafting for rare species; by seed for others. o Main species. Sapinette blanche, rising to 15 or 16 meters, pyramidal in shape and closely resembling the Epicea; - Abies Balsamifera, with aromatic leaves, hence its name Gilead's Balsam, although Gilead's balsam comes from another tree; - Abies du Canada, 7 to 8 meters high in Europe, 25 meters high in America; remarkable for the elegance of its young twigs; its bark contains a lot of tannic acid and is very suitable for tanning leather, and its twigs are used to make certain beers. Stems twiggy; branches inclined and then rising; very small cones. It can be pruned, like Yew; - Abies Excelsa; graces our forests; at flowering time, it spreads a large quantity of pollen which, blown by the wind and mixed with water from the sky, has often resembled sulfur rains; very upright; severe appearance as it ages, whorled branches; dark green foliage; cylindrical cones; multiplication by seed; - Abies Douglasii; S. of Douglas. de Douglas, 35 to 50 meters high; slender, twisted branches, buds wrapped in scales, acute leaves; valued for reforestation of the Landes; - A. Dumosa or S. buissonneux, 20 to 25 meters high, very thin and very stuffy; fears the winters of northern France; - Abies Pectinata or Sapin Blanc, S. à feuilles d'If, S. de Normandie; beautiful pyramidal tree of the first size, verticillate branches, horizontal; leaves notched at the top, white underneath. Supplies Strasbourg turpentine. Wood valued by luthiers for stringed-instrument soundboards; good for carpentry, joinery, etc. ; A. Grandis, S. Géant; in California, it reaches heights of over 60 meters; obtuse, cylindrical cones, with connivant scales like Cedar; -A. Nobilis, S. Noble, with linear leaves, curved or reflexed towards the upper part of the twigs; silvery on the underside; cones covered by the tops of the toothed wings of the seeds; - A. Religiosa, S. Sacré, from Mexico, with whole leaves, silvery below; cone quite similar to the cone of the Cedar of Lebanon; - A. Pinsapo, S. Pinsapo, 20 to 24 meters high, very beautiful pyramidal shape, braves the harshest winters; - S. de Numidie, quite similar in size and arrangement of green leaves above, glaucous below; cones grouped from 3 to 5; monumental appearance; - A. Spectabilis, from Nepaul and the Himalayas, 25 to 30 meters high, with notched leaves at the top, white below; fears severe cold; - A. ABRONIA (Nyctaginea). - Herbaceous, perennial plant, native to California; leaves opposite, petiolate, very entire; remarkable long-stalked, sweet-smelling flowers; arranged in terminal clusters, pale in the center, bright pink around the circumference. Sow in August in a nursery; pot over winter in a greenhouse; plant out towards the end of May. AGSINTHE. V. Artemisia. ABUTILON (Malvaceae). - Native to Brazil; about 50 species are known. Striated Abutilon has heart-shaped leaves at the base, and solitary, bell-shaped, golden-yellow flowers with purplish veins; orangery in winter; planted out in May, flowers until the first frosts. Multiplication by cuttings. ACACIA (Mimosées), from a Greek word meaning spike (Leguminosae). - Not to be confused with the tree commonly known as Acacia, which belongs to the Robinia genus (false Acacia). Corolla funnel- or top-shaped, longer than the calyx, with qninquifid or, less often, quadrifid limb; indefinite number of stamens (8 to 200) inserted either in the style of the ovary, on the receptacle or at the bottom of the corolla; indefinite number of seeds contained in a bivalve envelope. - Trees or shrubs with or without prickles; leaves simple or compound or decomposed, stipulate; petiole and rachis often glandular; inflorescence axillary or terminal veryvaried; flowers sessile or rarely pedicellate, bracteolate, yellow, white or greenish. The Acacia genus comprises some 300 species, most of them native to the tropical regions of the Old and New Worlds: remarkable for the hardness of their wood and the materials they provide for medicine and industry; for example, Acacia Catechu yields a very astringent juice known as Cachou; Acacia Nigra is a tonic much-appreciated by Americans; tanners use the green fruits (neb-neb) of A. Nilotica; Senegal gum comes from A. Verck. Acacias with spines or prickles. A. AGREABLE, with flowers in clusters, numerous branches, bipinnate leaves; axillary heads of beautiful golden-yellow flowers. Temperate greenhouse. A. DE FARNÈSE, Casse du Levant, 5 to 6 meters high; unattractive appearance, but small, very fragrant yellow flowers; - A. à petites feuilles, approx. 2 meters high; solitary or geminate heads of pretty yellow flowers in March-April; - A. Grandis, with very fine foliage composed of 10 to 12 pairs of glaucous leaflets; beautiful golden-yellow flowers; heather soil; temperate greenhouse. Acacias without thorns. A. BLANCHATRE, from Australia. - Height 6 to 10 meters; small whitish fruits cover its stems, twigs and leaves; yellow, fragrant flowers. Grows outdoors in southern France; in cold or temperate greenhouses in the north. A. DE CONSTANTINOPLE. - Silk tree. Height 10 meters; pinkish-white flowers in paniculate heads, with stamens forming aigrettes; finely cut bipinnate leaves. Used as an orangery in winter in our northern provinces. Seed multiplication on beds and under frames in spring; - A. à deux épis (Australia), height 3 to 4 meters; small, acute, oblong leaves; sulfur-yellow flowers, blooming in spring. Orangery or temperate greenhouse. Acacias with catkin-shaped, cylindrical spikes of flowers, A. VERTICILLÉ. - Flowers bloom from March to May; this shrub is very reminiscent of the juniper; - A. with very long leaves, leaves long from Om, 16 to Om;22; -- A. with numerous flowers, height 2 meters; sulfur-yellow flowers in pairs, blooming in spring. A. WITH LONG LEAVES. - Height from 4 to 7 meters and more; long spikes of lemon-yellow flowers; all Australian species are grafted onto this species; - A. glaucous, hanging branches, oblique, mucronate leaves, integral, pedunculated spikes of yellow flowers. Acacias with flowers in globular heads; clusters of flowers in panicles. A. DE SAINTE-HÉLÈNE. - It has the habit of the weeping willow, with long clusters of yellow flowers blooming in autumn; -A. lame de couteau, thick, almost triangular leaves, long clusters of flowers; -A. à feuilles de lin, height 3 to 4 meters; pointed, linear, long leaves; fragrant, pale yellow flowers bloom all summer; -A. à fleurs bleuâtres; numerous flowers bloom in spring; -A. trompeur (Australia), flowers in pedunculated clusters; orangerie. Acacias with simple leaves. - Phyllodes. A. with Juniper leaves; pendulous, linear, prickly twigs with setaceous stipules; small, pale-yellow flowers bloom in spring; - A. fausse asperge, almost cylindrical twig, multiflorous, solitary heads of sulfur-yellow flowers bloom in April-May; - A. with heart-shaped leaves; acute leaves; sulfur-white flowers, blooming in April-May; - A. with round leaves; height: 1.50 m; golden-yellow flowers, marking the branches; stake or trellis. Acacias are propagated from seed, which should be placed in a warm bed in early spring; very early plants can be planted about three weeks after their birth, in a new bed at Om, 10 or Om, 12 from each other, lightly watered, well sheltered by glass at night and aerated during the day; carefully removed after a month, leaving as much soil as possible on the roots, and placed separately in small pots filled with vegetable garden soil; immersed in a warm layer of tan; kept in the shade until they have formed new roots and, finally, treated like other tropical plants. Some species can be propagated by separating old plants when they have put down roots; perennial species survive the winter in warm greenhouses and produce ripe flowers and seeds the following summer. In June, remove creeping species from pots and expose them to the south; under cloches they live through the summer, but without gaining much strength; they are then condemned to perish at the first cold of autumn. ACACIA BLANC, ROBINIA, Pseudo-Acacia (Papilionaceae). - H. 16 to 23 meters. White flowers in May-June. Multipl. by seed or offspring in March-April: R. de Decaisne; R. sans épines; R. visqueux; R. rose. Multipl. by split grafting. H. 2 meters. All soil; mid-sun. A. PULCHERRIMA, very elegant Inga (Mimosées). - Crimson-red flowers with brown stamens. Multipl, by cuttings; warm and temperate greenhouse. - Inga ferrugineux. Flowers with purplish aigrettes, Warm, humid greenhouse; sandy soil. - I. anomala. H. 1 to 2 meters. Greenish flowers with dark purple-purple aigrettes. Warm greenhouse. A. PUDICA. V. Mimosa pudica. ACANTHUS MOLLIS, Acanthus without thorns. Branc-ursine. From a Greek word meaning thorn (Acanthaceae.) - It was the beauty of its leaves surrounding a basket placed on a tomb that gave Callimachus the idea of the Corinthian capital. Perennial; single stem from Om, 70 to 1 meter; elegant, large, smooth leaves; graceful flowers; singular corolla: lower lip with three lobes, calyx with four divisions, one very large posterior, two very small intermediate and one anterior. Multiplication by seed or root division. Sow in dry, light soil towards the end of March, and if the season is mild, the plants appear in May; in damp soil, the roots rot over winter. Preferably grow A. du Portugal with long spikes of pinkish flowers; A. épineuse with tight, hairy, white flowers, spotted with lilac; in July and August. ACER, ERABLE (Acerineae). - Flower: calyx usually divided into five, more rarely into four, nine parts; pre-flowering imbricate; petals in equal number inserted on the circumference of a fleshy, hypogynous disk, sometimes missing; stamens inserted on this same disk, always in definite number, sometimes equal to the other parts of the flower, usually larger, rarely proportional to the number eight; ovary with two lobes; style. with two stigmas. Fruit separates into two mono- or di-sperm samaras. Seeds attached to inner corner of lodge. Fine, tight, white or gray wood, sought after by cabinetmakers for making musical instruments and for heating; it contains a certain amount of sugar. Maples grow to considerable size in our climates; they multiply easily by their seed, which should be sown in spring immediately after maturity and covered with about Om, 14 of light soil; it will then sprout within the year, fresh and substantial Sol. Propagate rare species and varieties by grafting them onto Sycamore or other common species. The native A. Pseudo Platanus or Sycamore, or False Platanus, reaches heights of 20 and 25 meters; yellowish-green flowers; - A. Platanoïdes or Plane is less tall, but more ornamental than the former; it has yellow flowers in graceful corymbs; - Montpellier Maple, very large, but not very tall. Yellowish flowers in corymbs; short-winged fruits that turn red when ripe. Finally, let's mention: the Country Maple, 6 to 10 meters high; the Cottony Maple, 10 to 12 meters high; the Sugar Maple, native to America, from which a sugar similar to cane sugar is extracted, etc., etc. ACER, NEGUNDO. V. Negundo. ACHILLEA (Compositae). - From the name of Achilles, pupil of the centaur Chiron; allusion to the real or supposed virtues of this plant. The main species are A, mille feuilles. Indigenous, perennial, cultivated for its variegated, blond, corymb-like flowers, which have an aromatic fragrance; purple-flowered variety much sought-after by sheep. Blooms all summer long. Multiplied by splinters. - A, tomentose, perennial, almost similar to A. with pectinate leaves; good for borders. Dry soil. Multipl. by splinters in spring. - Filependula, native to the East. Stems 1.50 m long; many yellow flowers in tight corymbs. Sow the seeds in a bed in March, and they'll give you flowers in September. Thanks to their long, fibrous roots, Achillea plants solidify embankments and slopes. LONG LEAF ACHIMENÈS (Gesneriaceae). - Native to Mexico. Stems from Om, 20 to Om, 30; flowers of a beautiful blue mixed with white. - Several species and numerous varieties: Achimenès with large leaves; A, with open leaves, etc. They are cared for in greenhouses like Orchids (see this word). Multipl, by rhizomes and cuttings. ACHYRANTHES. - From two Greek words meaning straw and flower (Amaranthaceae). Twelve species, most of which grow in the equatorial zone. A. de Vershaffelt is a tall plant of Om, 30-40, reddish-purple and crimson. Sought-after for borders and baskets. Multipl, from cuttings; warm, humid greenhouse; part-sun. ACONIT (Ranunculaceae). - From a Greek word: pointe, because savages used it to rub the tips of their arrows. A perennial plant growing in the open ground; from Om,70 to 1m,30 tall. Large, beautiful blue or yellow flowers, somewhat helmet-shaped and arranged in clusters or terminal panicles. Propagates easily by seed sown in light, soft soil in mid-shade, or by splitting and dividing clumps. Blooms in summer. The main species are: A, pyramidal, the first to produce long flower spikes of Om 70 in May and June. A, tueloup blooms from mid-June to August. A. des Pyrénées, 4.50 tall; yellow flowers in July. A. napel, 1.20 m high, with three varieties: one with white flowers, one with pink flowers and one with variegated flowers. Multipl, by splinters in spring or autumn. Water in dry weather; place in flowerbeds in the shade, leaving a distance of about Om 12 between plants. ACORUS CALAMUS, Sweet rush (Aroidea). - From two Greek words: without sloe, because, according to the ancients, this plant cured eye ailments. Native and herbaceous; attractive green leaves; fragrant flowers in cylindrical catkins. Japanese A. gramineus, with its green, white and pink ribboned leaves, is grown in orangeries or cold frames, in damp, shady heather soil. Multip, by splinters. ACROLINIUM ROSEUM, Pink-leaved Acrolinium (Compositae). - From Texas. Annual plant; stem about Om, 30 tall, leaves glaucous, linear: flowers with involucre of scarce leaflets, with golden-yellow disk in center. Sow outdoors in April. Sow outdoors in March-April; flowers keep as long as those of immortelle. ADHATODA VASICA, Indian or Ceylon walnut (Acanthaceae). - Height 3 to 4 meters. Leaves pubescent, large, acute, evergreen; flowers white, two-lipped; blooms in July-August. Orangery; orange soil; warm exposure; frequent watering. Cutting in spring on bed and under frame, in terrine. - A. à feuilles de Coignassier (Brazil); erect, twiggy stem; oval, entire, opposite leaves; geminate flowers with white upper lip and red at the top; yellow stripe on lower lip. ADIANTE (Ferns). The best-known species is A. Cheveu de Vénus, with light-green tufts and ebony-black stems. Warm, shady greenhouse. The Æ. attaches itself to trees and lives very sparingly at the expense of their bark; we have seen Ærids that borrowed their food from the air only, by means of their leaves. This genus includes many species, most of which are native to Asia; for example, Lindley's Æ., whose flowers are first lilac, then pure white. - Æ. très-suave, with long clusters of pink-washed flowers on a white background; a variety is Æ. noble. - Greenish Æ., with white flowers, petals empurpled at the tip and red-dotted labellum. - The fragrant Æ.; one meter high; clusters Om, 50 long, composed of beautiful white flowers; pinkish tint at the top of the perianth divisions. - The Æ. crispé, beautiful hanging, elongated clusters of white flowers, pink labellum edged with white. - The pink-flowered Æ. (Nepaul), with mucronate, slightly curved leaves; numerous flowers, mottled pink and dark red; red spots in the middle of the labellum, which is itself more colorful than the rest of the flower. ÆSCULUS HIPPOCASTANUM, Horse chestnut (Hippocastaneae). - Native to the temperate climates of Asia; cultivated in Europe as early as 1591. Beautiful tree, 20 to 25 meters high; beautiful flowers dotted with pink spots on a white background, arranged in numerous vertical pyramids at the top of each branch; ample green foliage; strong, majestic trunk; astringent, bitter bark used for dyes; fruits of a repulsive harshness and quite similar in shape to those of the chestnut tree; sown in the ground or in troughs to transplant the seedling in the nursery. - M. d'Inde lives in all types of soil, but prefers substantial, cool soil; it tolerates pruning and shearing. We also grow the red-flowered Æsculus, with more embossed, greener foliage than the previous species; it flowers in the third or fourth year. AGAPANTHE OMBELLIFERE (Liliaceae). - Native to Africa, Om, 70 to 1 m high; beautiful umbel of 30 or 40 blue, odorless leaves, blooming in July. Propagate from splinters or seeds; sow in heather soil. Cover with litter during severe cold. AGATHEA, Blue-flowered Cinerea (Compositae). - Stems of Om, 50; blooms almost all year round. Open, nourishing soil; orangery, multip, seeds, cuttings, marcottes and offspring. AGAVE OF AMERIA, from a Greek word: Admirable (Amaryllidae). - General characteristics: tubular corolla, six deep divisions, six protruding stamens attached to the tube; ovary integral with the base of the corolla; three-located capsule; leaves large, numerous, very thick, armed with prickles; bare, candelabra-shaped spike 3 to 8 meters long, each branch terminating in a corymb of yellow flowers. Originally from America, this plant is now widespread in Italy, southern France, etc. It flowers quite often. Flowers quite often. Seed and carnation propagation; orangery. Aloe cultivation. For example: American aloe. Its leaf fibers are used to make ropes, fishermen's nets, carpets, wrapping cloths, etc., etc.; a honey-like juice, wine and vinegar are extracted from its stalk. Strong thorns make this plant sought-after for forming sturdy fences. - Yucca leaves. - Artichoke. - Variety mediapicta, yellow in the center. - Other: stricta, with striated and variegated leaves. ALOE, Aloe (Liliaceae). - Native to the Cape of Good Hope. Corolla tubular, almost cylindrical, divided into six lobes at its opening, enclosing six stamens attached to the receptacle; free ovary which converts into a capsule with three chambers, occupied by seeds arranged in two rows; - leaves are stiff, very fleshy, overlapping one another and forming pyramids or rosettes, sometimes glaucous green, sometimes with yellow or blood-red stripes; most are thorny or have strong, prickly teeth along their edges; - stems are very strong, sometimes woody; beautiful, short-lasting flowers; leaves yield a juice widely used in medicine (Succotrin). Aloes are treated like cacti, in light, nourishing soil; in winter, temperate greenhouse, no watering. - A. from Bourbon Island. Tall stem; flat, broad, hanging leaves with red margins; spikes of greenish-yellow flowers. - A. à corne de bélier (Africa). Height: Om, 70; leaves strongly toothed, inverted; very bright red flowers - Socotrin or commercial A., with dichotomous stem, straight, lanceolate leaves, with white serration; spikes of red flowers. - A. féroce (Cape), spiny leaves on both sides, amplexicaules; dense spike of saffron-red flowers. - A. mitre (Cape). Height: Om, 70 to 1 meter; mitred leaves, spiny at the edges; red flowers. - The A, umbellate (Cape); with banded leaves, armed with red spines; saffron-red flowers, blooming in May-June. - A. fan (Cape); dichotomous stem; tongue-shaped leaves, slightly denticulate at the apex; single cluster of red flowers. - Cat's tongue (Cape), stemless; leaves marked with white; flowers green at the top, red at the base. - Duck's beak, with duck's beak leaves; white flowers with green stripes at the top, red and powdery at the base. - L'A. toile d'araignée (Cape spider web); rosettes of leaves covered with white threads; greenish flowers. - L'A, pouce écrasé (Cape Town), with thick, flattened leaves. ANEMONE, from a Greek word: Wind Flower (Renonculaceae). - Characteristics: bipinnate or digitate radical leaves, spike bearing a solitary flower at the apex; calyx replaced by an involucre with three simple leaflets. Varieties Fig. 4 - Anemone pulsatilla. numerous, double or semi-double, decorated with the most magnificent colors: purple, crimson, red, white, violet, etc. To obtain varieties, prefer single Anemones with large, regular flowers and strong stems. ARUNDO DONAX, Cattail Reed (Grasses). - Height 3 to 4 meters; purple flowers in panicles. Water's edge. Multip, marcottes, cuttings, by separating lateral sprays. ASCLEPIAS INCARNATA, Incarnate milkweed (Milkweed). - A beautiful perennial native to Virginia; 1 m to 1.30 m tall; in July-August, it produces umbels of beautiful purple-red, vanilla-scented flowers. Sun exposure, in cool, light soil. Propagate from splinters or seeds sown in May-July, in a nursery; plant out in autumn or spring. - A. de Syrie, cotton-wool herb; pinkish-white flowers, July-August. - A. tuberose, beautiful saffron-red flowers, July-September. ASPERULA ODORATA, Sweet woodruff (Rubiaceae). - Native, perennial; forms clumps of Om, 30; corymb of white flowers; likes the shade of trees and is content with all kinds of soil. Multip, by separation. ASPHODELUS LUTEUS, Yellow Asphodel, Jacob's Rod (Liliaceae). - Height: 1 meter; long spike of yellow flowers, May-July. Likes warm weather, requires no fertilizer. Multiply by seed sown in spring in the garden, or by suckers. Double-flowered variety. ASTER, ASTÈRE, Etoile, Reine-Marquerite (Composées), - Very numerous, tall species, forming beautiful clumps in large flowerbeds, thanks to their corymbs of white, purple, dark blue or lilac flowers. These plants would quickly exhaust the soil; they need to be moved every three or four years. Multiply by sowing, or better by separating clumps. For example: A. des Alpes, purple flowers with a yellow disk; white-flowered variety. Can be propagated from seed or splinters in any soil. - A. floribonde (North America). JAPANESE AUCUBA (Horned). - Height: 1 m to 1.30 m; evergreen; small, insignificant brown flowers, open in April: clean, light, non-moist soil; half-sun exposure. Some varieties, including: Himalayan azalea with dark, glossy green, toothed leaves; - other species, all brought from Japan, have yellow variegated leaves. AZALEA, Azalea (Ericaceae). - Azaleas are divided into two distinct groups: 1° the Azaleas of America and the Caucasus, all of which are grown in light soil, or better still, in heather; 2° the Azaleas of India, which require a temperate greenhouse. The former are grown in the open air, in slightly shaded positions; flowerbeds. Temperate-greenhouse Azaleas bloom from April to June. As soon as they have finished flowering, they should be repotted with fine, rich heather soil, put back in the greenhouse, or left in the open air, but in the shade, then bury the pots up to Om, 06 from the edge, in a place well exposed to the sun. Drain pots; water moderately, preferably with rainwater; basin leaves in hot weather; slightly damp Dutch greenhouse in winter; plenty of light. Pinch after flowering, as it sprouts. Multiply by approach grafting, split grafting, veneering; reproduce by marcottes, by rooted splinters, under cloches, in a hot greenhouse. Seedlings produce the most beautiful varieties. Open-ground azaleas. A. visqueuse (North America), red or white flowers, covered with viscous hairs; these hairs are also found on the leaf branches. Bare-leaf A. (North America). Quite similar to the previous one, but with longer leaves underneath. - A. gracieuse (Caucasus). Height: Om, 50; yellowish-red flowers bloom in June-July; several varieties. - L'A, pontique (Caucasus). Yellow or red flowers, with deciduous bracts. - Marigold-colored A. (North America). Scarlet or fire-colored corollas, topped with non-viscous hairs. - Bare-flowered A.; red, non-viscous flowers, appearing before the leaves. Several beautiful varieties. Temperate greenhouse azaleas. L'A, ponceau (China). Deep lilac-pink flowers, blooming in February-March. - A. à fleurs crispées; purplish-pink flowers, with fringed, frizzy divisions; blooms in February. - A. de l'Inde; flowers in twos and threes, in terminal clusters. In general, Begonias require temperate greenhouses, nourishing heather soil mixed with consumed compost; multip, by cuttings and rooted lateral stems, watered with guano every eight days from March-April; 500 grams of guano per 100 liters of water. Re-pot from time to time; once the vegetation period is over, suspend watering to let the B. rest. Some species lose their stems in autumn each year, and their tubers sprout new shoots in spring; others flower until mid-winter; in our southern provinces, we should try to put Bolivian and Clarke's B. in the ground, at a good exposure: they would probably succeed. 1 PANICULATED BEJARIA (Ericaceae.) - Height: 1 m to 1.30 m; pointed leaves with reddish edges; fragrant, purple-pink flowers, blooming from June to September. CALANDRINIA UMBELLATA, Umbel Calandrina (Portulaceae). - Native to Chile; pretty pink-purple flowers in clusters; used in borders and beds; sow in April or May, and you'll have flowers in July-September. CALCEOLARIA, Calceolaria (Scrophularinae). - Named for its yellow, purple, etc., clog-like flowers (calceolus). Woody species multiply by smothering cuttings and rooting splinters; herbaceous species multiply by dividing clumps. Prefer sowing in spring or autumn, in damp heather soil at moderate heat. Drain the bottom well and top with 17 CAL of heather soil. Sow and cover evenly with fine sand. Beat the top a little so that the seed receives the moisture more easily. Do not let the surface dry out; sowing should be done in half-shade. Transplant seedlings at their fourth leaf in potting soil or under a frame. When the young plants are strong enough, place them in pots of Om, 10. The best soil for them is a mixture of one-third heather soil, one-third potting compost and one-third free earth. Re-pot in November and December in om, 20 pots, leaving plenty of air and light. Smoke your plants every day to protect them from aphids. CALENDULA OFFICINALIS, Garden Marigold (Compositae). - Annual. Very bright, saffron-yellow flowers. CALLYSTEGIA DE LA DAHOURIE (Convolvulaceae). - Voluble, twining stems, 2 to 3 meters long; dark pink, funnel-shaped flowers bloom in June-October; - sought-after for cradles, arbors, etc. Multip, by root division, CAMELIA JAPONICA, Japanese Camellia (Ternstrœmiaceae). - Introduced to France in 1786. Height: 6 or 7 meters; in winter gardens or in the ground in temperate countries. There are over 700 varieties with double, white, red, pink or variegated flowers. Use heather soil to which you add a little leaf compost; dry your Camellias after a year and pot them; the following year they will be good for grafting; water with rainwater or river water, infrequently in winter, quite abundantly in summer. Take the Camellias out of the greenhouse in July and let them grow in spring under glass, in semi-shade, in a well-ventilated spot. If you wish to obtain single-flowered Camellias, cut off the previous year's shoots from the single-flowered plants, about Om, 12 in length, and plant them in cloche-covered terrines of fine heather in the shade of a tarpaulin; remove the mist from the inside from time to time, and baste with reserve. Despite some growers' claims to the contrary, new varieties can be obtained from seed. Camellias love the sun and wither in the shade. Repotting is best done in February or July. Here are a few varieties: Camellia Adrien Lebrun, cherry-pink mixed with brown. - C. Bella Romana, pinkish-white with carmine streaks. - C. Bice-Rosazza, ivory-white spotted with blood-red. - C. CAMPANULA MEDIUM, Sea Violet (Campanulaceae). - Biennial; height: Oo,65 or Om, 70; large, beautiful, elongated flowers, more or less pale violet-blue, pink or white, hairy inside, single or double; sow in spring and plant out in July-August. - C. pyramidale, biennial, hardy; height: 1 m to 1.50 m; beautiful blue flowers in long bunches and bouquets, July-September; very flexible stems; open soil, rock gardens, old walls, half-sun. White-flowered variety. - C. à feuilles de Pêcher, perennial; pale blue flowers. Varieties with single or double white flowers. CERASTIUM TOMENTOSUM, Cottony Ceratius (Caryophyllaceae). - This perennial, trailing plant forms a rounded clump; white flowers in May-June. Sow in March, or separate from traces; dry soil. CEREUS, Cierge (Cactaceae). - Cereus with large flowers; very large flowers indeed, white inside, yellow outside, opening in the evening, spreading a sweet vanilla scent at night, but flowers doomed to die at dawn the next day. Warm greenhouse. Cherry laurel, small white flowers; black fruit; its leaves are a poison that is wrongly used to give milk an almond taste. Multiplied by seed and marcotte. Shady exposure. Cereus, CINEARYA PEPPER LEAVES. - Canaries, Disc flowers with yellow rays; light soil. Same culture as Purple Cineraria. Multipl. by cuttings in summer or offshoots in spring. Activate flowering by watering once a week, starting in March, with Peruvian guano; 500 grams per 100 liters of water. Drain the bottom of pots to avoid humidity, which is harmful to these plants; plenty of air and light. Frosts kill Cineraria. Plant in clumps in gardens, apartments, etc. For use in temperate greenhouses around December 15 (see also Groundsel). MARITIME CINERARY (Composites). - Native; height: Om, 65; beautiful yellow flowers, open all summer. Exp. to south. Substantial soil; little watering. Sow in June-July in carefully sifted potting soil. Transplant into wintered pots in frames. Set up in May; orangery by autumn. - C. Perennial purple; h. Om, 33 to one meter. Light purple rays, darker purple disk. It is the purple Cineraria that has given rise, through its seedlings, to Cineraria with pink, purple, carmine, lilac, violet, white, azure blue or soft blue flowers, as well as hybrid Cineraria with such varied colors. D DAHLIA (Compositae). - This admirable plant was brought to France from Mexico in 1800; it was simple and orange in color; seedlings have produced all shades of white, red and yellow, pure or mixed in delicately blended hues; only blue has not yet been obtained. D. is propagated by cuttings, herbaceous grafts, seedlings and tubers; the last of these methods is preferred. Tubers should be planted in a warm layer, under a frame, leaving at least one growing eye on each portion; you then place in well loosened, substantial and soft soil, or in pots kept under a frame if frosts still seem to be a threat; frequent but moderate watering in summer; stakes. To obtain varieties, graft into slits on the sides of the tuber and keep in a bed under a bell. Sow in March-April, transplant when the plant has five or six leaves; flowers in July, August and September. Just to mention a few: Cosmos-flowered Dahlia, with purple disk and lilac rays. Multip, seeds and cuttings. - D. Decaisne, purplish disk with purple lines. - D. impérial with white half-flowers and yellow disk. Sow in April, transplant in nursery; plant out in autumn or spring; light, sandy soil. - D. azure. Perennial. Single or double azure flowers. Full soil. - D. scarlet; flowers with yellow inner divisions, red outer divisions. - D. de Barlow, with semi-double, brilliant azure flowers. Light soil. Half-shade exposure. Multiply by splinters. In general, Dauphinelles are best used either in clumps of a single color, or in clumps of various colors; in borders, wide flowerbeds, etc.; sow in place, in September-October, or in March-April, in light soil with a small layer of compost spread over the seed. The Œ.'s bitter enemy is the earwig, which is lured by sheep's leg debris; in the morning, it buries itself in this debris, of which it is very fond; it is caught and crushed. Cuttings; marcottes in lead cornet. Frequent watering. Return to orangeries or well-ventilated rooms at frost. DICLYTRA. V. Dielytra. DIE DICTAMNUS ALBUS, Fraxinelle (Diosmées). - Native to southern France. Perennial; height: Om, 70 to one meter; leaves quite similar to those of Ash; large clusters of purplish flowers striped with dark purple, in June-July. Multip, from splinters or seeds sown as soon as mature in terrines or flowerbeds; transplanted in nurseries; planted two years later. Especially in hot weather, all parts of this plant produce a volatile essential oil, flammable on contact with a lighted match. DIONÉE ATTRAPE-MOUCHE (Drocéracées). - Native to Carolina (North America), the cilia on its leaves catch and suffocate any insect that lands on them. In winter, this plant needs a constant temperature of 7 to 8 degrees centigrade. Multiply by sowing seeds when mature, or by taking leaf cuttings. Soil composed of plant detritus, covered with moss to maintain humidity. DIOSCORÉE CULTIVÉE, cultivated yam (Dioscorées). - Native to India; cooked, edible roots. ERICA, Heather (Ericaceae). - We can only indicate the general divisions into : Native heathers. - Cape heather. Over 300 species in all. A well-lit greenhouse that needs only to be protected from frost; we prefer the Dutch greenhouse. We multiply the B. by sowing; when the young plant is vigorous enough and Om, 50 high, we put it in a pot filled with heather soil, kept under a frame in a very slightly warm layer, in the shade; watering; air; light. Layering is difficult; it is only used for certain beautiful species aged 2 to 3 years, whose shoots must be carefully peeled before being laid down. Wean, lift and pot in October. Cuttings are taken in May and June, either in Om 08 pots, sheltered by a drinking glass, or in wide pots of Om,26 to Om, 28, under a bell; gravel at the bottom; moist, sandy heather soil, well packed. Take one-year-old twigs or sprouting twigs, strip them of their leaves at the bottom, and plant them, packing the soil tightly around them; leave them to dry out in the air and sun for a day; then press them up to Om, 28 from the edge of the terrine in a warm tan and cover with a cloche; light watering (for further information, see the chapters on cuttings and greenhouses). The main native heathers include: Erica arborea, with fragrant white flowers. - E. ciliaris, with purple or white flowers. - E. vulgaire, with small pink or white flowers, single or double. - E. scoparia, with small greenish flowers. - E. cinerea, with purplish flowers. Among the Cape heathers: E. Albertus superba, pale yellow flowers washed with pink. - E. hyemalis, with pink-white flowers, blooming from October to February. - E. Hartwelli, with red-white-pink flowers; March-April-May. - E. Capressina, large square flowers, tightly packed at the head. - E. Andromedœflora, spherical flowers, few in number, carmine pink. - E. jasminiflora nana, Fig. 13 - Erica. with white flowers striped with pink, May-June. - E. vestita alba, upright spike of tubular white flowers; March-May. - E. vernia ovata, yellow-orange flowers. - E. vernia coccinea, orange-red flowers, January-February-March; E. tubuliflora coccinea, hairy, coccine-red flowers, May-June. - E. translucens rubra, dark pink, reflexed flowers; January-February. - E. syndriana, campanulate flowers, dark carmine at the base, pale lilac at the throat and on the blade; May-June. - E. propendens, lilac-pink flowers; May. - E. persoluta alba, small reflexed white flowers; February-April. - E. patersoni, yellow flowers; March-August. - E. mirabilis, white flowers, March-May. FICUS ELASTICA, Figuier élastique (Morées). - An admirable tree for its foliage; it can be left in the ground during the summer, but must be brought in before the first frosts; a much sought-after houseplant in recent years. It produces one of the commercially available rubbers. Multiply by taking cuttings, leaving them to dry, and placing them in pots on a warm bed and under a frame; water moderately. FUCHSIA (Œnothérées). - According to a book written by President Porcher, here are the conditions that Fuchsia must meet in order to be perfectly beautiful: "It must have a pleasing habit; preference should be given to bushy or medium-sized plants, which are generally more floriferous. The peduncle must be elongated so that the flower has a graceful habit; the calyx tube must be proportionate in all its parts; if it's too thin, it's a capital defect; the calyx segments must be wide, reflexed or at least wide enough 19 to clear the corolla; when they're long and narrow, they give the flower an ungraceful appearance; the corolla petals must have fullness;.... as for coloring, although inviolable rules cannot be laid down, only bright, vivid colors should be admitted, and dull, false or mediocre shades rejected, etc..., etc." Fuchsia is easy to grow. Put it in a pot filled with light rather than heavy soil: give it light; water it. Take suppressed cuttings at any time of year, but preferably in spring. Sowing provides new varieties; in sandy heather soil. For further information, see the chapter on cuttings, etc. - F. éclatant, with light vermilion flowers. - F. corymbiferous, with red-carmine flowers. - F. toothed leaves. - F. remarkable. - It is impossible to list all the varieties, as they are so numerous. F.'s main enemies are slugs and puerons. FUSAIN. V. Evonymus. G GAILLARDE VIVACE (Compositae). - Perennial; height from Om 35 to Om, 70. Very pretty radiate flowers, with brown disc, orange and purple rays. Shards or seedlings; cuttings under frame. Light soil. Several sought-after species. GANT DE NOTRE-DAME, V. Aquilegia vulgaris. TURF (1) (Grasses and Legumes). - Base your lawns on English ryegrass, which should be mowed often to prevent flowering, and watered sufficiently: 600 grams of seed per acre; mix in a few legumes. Shallow harrowing with a rake; roller. Weed once or twice a year; remove dandelions before they flower. If frosts and thaws (1) Voy, part 1, word Ivraie. have raised and cracked your soil, consolidate it with well-consumed compost mixed with dry, pulverized pond sludge. HUMULUS LUPULUS, Cultivated hop. (Cannabinis). - Not strictly speaking an ornamental plant, but its stems can be used to garnish cradles and arbors. ORIENTAL HYACINTH (Liliaceae). - This plant, originally from the Levant, has reached its most perfect state of cultivation in Holland, where it produces a considerable number of outstanding single and double varieties in all shades of red and blue. Sowing takes place in September, and requires a few precautions against extreme cold. Onions are not lifted until the third year, when they are treated in the same way as flowering onions. Cultivation by onion is preferable to sowing; loosened soil, mixed with one-sixth leaf compost or heather soil; cultivation in pots; cultivation in decanters on the necks of which you place the onions so that the roots are bathed in the liquid and the crown only touches it; always keep the decanters full and renew the water every month; throw in a few grains of salt to prevent it from corrupting; plenty of air and light. - J. double white. - J. red and pink double. - J. single and double blue, etc. Here are some of the main varieties of Dutch Hyacinths: Nemrod. IMPATIENS BALSAMINA, Garden balsam (Balsaminae). - Native to India; annual; height Om, 60; 343 red, pink, purple and single or double flowers. Many varieties. Sow in a bed in April and transplant in clods, well top-dressed. Balsamines are, with R. Marguerite, autumn flowers and last until the first frosts. The name Impatiente was given to the Balsamine because its seeds are enclosed in capsules which, when ripe, open and curl up abruptly at the slightest touch. Balsamine double, ordinary. COMMON JASMIN (Jasmine). - Native to Asia; 3 to 4 meters high; pretty, fragrant, pure-white flowers; grows all summer in the open, in a warm spot; covers with thick litter in winter. LILIUM, Lily (Liliaceae). - This genus includes many native and foreign species, some with white flowers, others with yellow, saffron or purplish flowers, etc. Any light, cool soil with warm exposure is good for growing Lilies. Any light, cool soil with a warm exposure is good for growing Lilies; every three years, the onions are lifted at the beginning of October, the old roots removed and the plants immediately replanted, so as to have flowers the following year. Defend the lily against its particular enemy, the lily beetle, a beetle with red elytra, whose female lays her eggs in the thickness of the leaves. White-flowered lily. LONICERA, Honeysuckle (Caprifoliaceae). - A distinction is made between true Honeysuckles, which are climbing and voluble, needing stakes and support points, and Shamrocks, with twiggy stems, strong enough to do without foreign help. Honeysuckles proper. Garden honeysuckle; red flowers outside, blooming in May and June. Oak-leaved and variegated varieties. - Chinese or Japanese honeysuckle; flowers first white, then pink or carmine, with a very sweet fragrance. Nin-Too or gold and silver-flowered variety; flowers first white, then yellow. - C. des bois. Native; flowers white or pinkish, then yellow; very sweet-smelling. - C. de Brown, coccine-yellow or blood-red flowers; very broad foliage. - C. pubescent; long, infundibuliform flowers, yellow inside, red outside, with a short, almost regular blade. - C. evergreen, very similar to the previous one, but with larger leaves. - C. with yellow flowers; very bright yellow flowers, pubescent at the base; sweet-smelling. - C. Éclatant; large purple or purplish flowers on the outside, yellowish-white on the inside. - C. entrelacé; purple-red flowers outside, blooming in summer and autumn. These species can withstand the rigors of winter in the Paris climate; half-sun exposure. Cool, light soil; they multiply from bedding, rooted suckers and seeds. Chamerisiers. Chamerisier de Tartarie, Dwarf cherry. Height: 3 meters; small white flowers inside, pink outside; red berries. Can be propagated from suckers and seeds in any soil and at any exposure. Red-flowered and white-flowered varieties. - C. de Ledebour (Central Asia). Height: 1 to 2 meters; reddish-yellow flowers with red bracts; black berries. Cool, loose soil; multi-purpose for bedding and cuttings. - C. des Pyrénées; pinkish-white flowers, blooming in May. Light soil; sunny exposure; many cuttings and seeds. - C. à courts pédoncules (Japan). Yellowish-white geminate flowers inside, washed with purple outside. - C. Xylosteon, 2.30 to 2.60 m tall; yellowish-white flowers; hedges. - C. d'Ibérie; pink flowers; red fruits similar to small cherries. - C. des Alpes, very similar to the above and, like it, multiplying in every way. GRAPE LOPEZIA (Œnothérées). - Mexico. Annual; reddish-pink flowers bloom from May to frost; warm exposure; light soil; multiply from seed sown in pots on a warm bed in spring; transplant in situ. GRIMPING LOPHOSPERM (Scrophularinea). - Mexico. Climbing plant, 3 meters or more in length; long flowers, about Om O4, pink with white or yellowish spots, blooming from August to October. Sow indoors in March; transplant into pots left indoors; plant out at the end of May. - L. d'Henderson, variety with white speckled flowers, etc.; multiply from cuttings; from seed; rich soil; temperate greenhouse. LOTUS JACOBÆUS, Lotier de Saint-Jacques. - Biennial; height Om, 70 to 1 m, with dark-brown flowers in threes. Sow in spring in a greenhouse; flowers in August; light soil. LUNAIRE ANNUELLE, Monnaie du pape (Crucifers). - Biennial; height: 1 meter; clusters of red, purplish, white or variegated flowers in April-May. Propagates from seed in any soil where it sows itself. LUPINUS, Lupin (Papilionaceae). - This very numerous genus includes: 1° perennials, which must be sown in pots and transplanted in place; 2° annuals, which are best sown immediately in place; heather soil and even siliceous soil with good exposure is sufficient for these plants, among which we will limit ourselves to mentioning: 1° Among the annual Lupins: - L. Dumretti superbus; purple-purple, white and yellow flowers. - L. varius with white and blue flowers. - L. venustus with dark blue-purple standard and dark purplish wings. - L. sulfureus, long spikes of sulfur-yellow flowers. - L. subramosus, bright blue standard, blue wings, white carina with blue tint at top. - L. nanus with small blue flowers. - L. changeant, tall, 1m, 60; blue and yellow flowers, very sweet-smelling. - L. luteus with yellow flowers. - L. hirsutus with blue flowers. - L. Cruikshanksii. These annual Lupins are content with any soil, except limestone. 2° Among the Lupins with perennial roots: L. triste with brown flowers. - L. rivularis. Height: 1 meter; pale yellow flowers. - L. polyphyllus with long clusters of pretty blue flowers, white-flowered variety. - L. macrophyllus with purple-brown flowers. - L. Hartwegii with light blue flowers mixed with white. - L. H. flore pleno, white flowers, then purplish-white. - L. arboreus. Mexico. Height: 1 to 2 meters, pale yellow flowers; lives 4 to 6 years. Perennial; pink or white flowers, single or double, May-June, etc., etc. COMMON LYCIET (Solanaceae). - Bushy shrub; solitary or geminate pale violet flowers; brilliant red fruits. Ordinary soil. DENTICULATED LYCOPODE (Lycopodiaceae). - Very suitable for decorating artificial rocks, waterfalls and fountains; it forms a lawn; it lines greenhouse walls well. - L. ombreon, parasol-shaped stem with fine, graceful fronds. - L. arborescent. 1 meter high; fronds with a metallic sheen. - L. bluish; same uses as Lycopodium denticulate; metallic color. - L. stoloniferous; same uses. LYCORIS AUREA, Golden yellow lily (Amaryllidae). - China. Height: Om, 65; umbel of 6 or 10 pretty yellow flowers, blooming in July-August; heat causes their anthers to twitch very sensitively for several minutes. Light soil, renewed every year. Temperate greenhouse. LYPERIA VIOLACEA (Scrophularinea). - From the Cape, lilac flowers, about Om, 15 to Om, 20 long; light soil or heather. PINE MACLURE, Osage orange, Bois d'arc (Morées). - North America; greenish female flowers in globose catkins; male flowers in spiciform catkins, June and July. Height: 1 metre; yellow flowers with reddish rays at base, open in summer and autumn. Sow in spring or autumn. MAGNOLIA, Magnolia (Magnoliaceae). - North America and Asia. Some species tolerate the cold very well, in full soil, in the Paris climate. In general, they need light, deep, cool soil; pure heather is preferable. Multip, seed 4 and cuttings. Pink varieties are grafted onto common species. MAMILLARIA, Mamillaria (Cacti). - This genus comprises fat plants covered with conical nipples (hence the name), each terminated by bristles and spines. Cold greenhouse. - M. very thorny, white thorns and red thorns, larger than the former; violet flowers, gathered in a crown at the top of the plant. - M. de Shiè de, with whitish flowers and bright red berries. - M. bright; nipples with cottony areoles; purplish-red flowers; prolific stem. MIMOSA PUDICA, Sensitive (Mimosées). - Native to the West Indies; well known for its curious sensitivity; a breath, a smell, anything frightens it, making it withdraw into itself. Fears the cold and should be kept in warm soil or under a frame. MIRABILIS JALAPA, Belle-de-nuit (Nyctaginea). - From Peru.
