MUSA PARADISIACA, Banana, Adam's Fig (Musaceae). - Stems 3 to 4 meters long; trunk covered with fleshy purplish flowers; those at the base of the trunk become fruit, those at the top remain sterile. Pots, crates and, preferably, tarpaulin or casing in a warm greenhouse; mixture of light soil, heather soil and potting compost; maintain temperature 15 to 25 degrees above zero, if B. is to bear fruit. Minimum 4-meter elevation under greenhouse glazing. Multiply by eyelets. B. de la Chine rarely exceeds 60. MUSCARI ODORANT, Musk hyacinth (Liliaceae). - Bulbous plant with yellow-purple flowers, musky scent; late April. Multiply by seed and cloves in July. Replace every three or four years, in October. MYOSOTIS DES MARAIS, Remember me (Borraginae). - Hardy perennial; height Om, 35; celestial-blue flowers with yellow dots, April-August. Moist soil. Multip, from seed, cuttings or splinters. - M. des Alpes, with softer blue flowers than the previous variety, from April to June. Sow in June-July in nurseries; transplant in September. Makes pretty borders. - M. des Açores. Sows in August-September. Transplanted into pots in winter; set out in May. COMMON MYRTA (Myrtaceae). - This shrub, native to western Asia, northern Africa and southern Europe, can be propagated from seed or, more commonly, from cuttings, marcottes and offshoots in light, nourishing soil; water frequently in summer, rarely in winter; shelter from the first frosts; orangery. The common Myrtle, with white flowers, offers varieties such as the Roman Myrtle with small and large leaves; the M. with double flowers; the M. d'Andalousie with orange leaves. Other varieties include the small-leaved M. Temperate greenhouse - Cottony M. Temperate greenhouse; cuttings on warm bed. N NARCISSE DES POETES, Porion Claudinette (Amaryllidae) - Native. Height: Om, 32; white flowers edged with purple, fragrant; May. Light, open soil; multiply from seeds or cloves separated from onions, lifting in the second or third year and replanting in October. Frequent watering in hot weather. - Tazetta à bouquets, native; large, fragrant yellow flowers in May; many varieties and sub-varieties with single or double flowers. - N. Soleil d'or, sulfur-yellow with thick orange-yellow crown. - N. Grand Monarch. Large, whitish flowers with pale yellow crown. Blooms in January-February in pots, or in carafes full of water if exposed to light and brisk air, and protected from frost. - N. daffodil. Native; single or double flowers of beautiful yellow, very fragrant, in April. Plant in September. In the open ground; covered in winter. Advance by potting or decanting. - False Narcissus, Aiault. Native; yellow flowers; very common in meadows; good for borders. - N. incomparable, native. Perennial. Height. Om, 30-40; solitary yellowish-white flower with dark yellow crown. - N. multiflora. Le Tout-Blanc, very fragrant; later than other species and very successful in decanters. The onions of double-flowered Narcissus must be lifted every year; the onions of single-flowered Narcissus, which do not fear the cold, remain in the open ground with impunity. NARDOSMIA FRAGRANS, Winter Heliotrope (Compositae). - Height Om, 35; purplish-white flowers, with a scent of Heliotrope. Multip, from roots and splinters; open soil, mid-sun. NEGUNDO ACEROIDES, Maple negundo (Acerinae). - With Ash leaves, green dioecious hanging flowers. Multiply by seed and preferably by cuttings in fresh soil. - N. variegatum with pinkish-white, then yellowish foliage mixed with green. Same culture. NELUMBIUM, Nélombo (Nélombonées). - Native to India; large leaves from Om, 35 to Om, 80 in diameter; large flowers from Om, 20 to Om, 25, deliciously fragrant. Plant rhizomes, in April, in tubs of muddy soil with a water table of about Om, 15, under well-closed, south-facing frames; set out (still in tubs) in June; flowers around mid-August. Grown in hot greenhouses in northern France. REMARKABLE NEMOPHILE (Hydrophyllaceae). - Annual, with azure-blue axillary flowers; several varieties with variegated white flowers, etc. - N. maculée with 3-centimeter-wide flowers, with a dark purple or azure-blue wedge-shaped spot dividing each petal down the middle. Nemophiles are widely used in borders. Sow in Sept.-Oct. in nurseries for replanting in April; or sow in March-June on site to obtain, for the first sowing, flowers from May to June; for the second sowing, flowers from June to August. DISTILLATORY NEPENTHES (Nepenthea). - Remarkable for its ascidians, a kind of small vase with a lid, always filled with water; these vases are formed by the dilation of the tendrils holding the leaves of this plant; small, insignificant flowers. Orchid greenhouse; grown in pots of raw heather mixed with moss. NICOTIANA TABACUM, Ordinary tobacco (Solanaceae). - Annual. Height 1m,40, purpurine flowers; in July. Sow in substantial soil in situ or for transplanting. Can be potted for use in greenhouses when the cold weather approaches. NIGELLE DE DAMAS, Cheveux de Vénus (Renonculaceae). - Annual plant with white or blue flowers and ruffles, June-September. Sow in light soil; warm exposure. SPRING NIVEA, Snowdrop (Amaryllidae). - Native; solitary white flowers with a greenish spot on each division. SUMMER NIVEA (Amaryllidae). - Trompe ending in 5 or 6 snow-white flowers with a green spot on each division. Open soil, half-shade. Multiply by cloves planted in October. ARROCH LEAF NOLANIA (Nolanaceae). - Annual; twiggy, recumbent stem; large blue and yellow flowers in summer and autumn. Used for rock gardens. NUPHAR LUTEUM, Yellow water lily (Nymphaeaceae). - Submerged or floating leaves, fragrant yellow flowers. Multiplied by dividing the rhizome or by sowing seeds, when mature, in soil always covered with water. SELAGIN LEAF NYCTERINE (Scrophularinea). - Annual; high D Fig. 20 -- Nicotiana tabacum: from Om, 15; fragrant pink flowers all summer; good for borders and beds. Sow outdoors in March-April; transplant outdoors for planting in May. NYMPHÆA ALBA, White water lily (Nymphéacées). - Indigenous, with large, beautiful pure-white flowers floating gracefully on the surface of calm waters, blooming in June-August. Multipl, by rhizomes or seeds. N. bleu d'Égypte, with its superb, fragrant blue flowers, can be grown in the aquarium of a warm or temperate greenhouse. O OCIMUM BASILICUM, Common Basil (Labiatae). - Height Om, 32; white or purplish flowers. Several varieties. Multip, from seed or cuttings. Small Basil with white flowers blooming all summer long. - B. with large white flowers. - Ceylon B., Warm greenhouse. - Sweet-smelling, pinkish-white flowers with purplish stamens. Sow in March; warm greenhouse. ODONTOGLOSSE AGRÉABLE (Orchids). - Pure white flowers, orange labellum, with reddish-vine dots. Warm greenhouse; peat and moss. EYE. V. Dianthus. OENOTHERA, ONAGRE (ŒEnothérées). - This genus contains many species, of which we shall mention only: the large-flowered Œ. Donkey grass; large, yellow, fragrant flowers bloom in June-October; sow outdoors in August or September. - L'Œ. versicolore; annual; axillary flowers, red tinged with yellow, Om,03 wide. - L'OE. pompeuse, from Om,70 to 1 meter high; large, fragrant white flowers from July to the first frosts, etc., etc. Multiplying in spring. LINSEED LEAF OMPHALODS (Borraginées). - White flowers bloom in June-August. Sow in spring or autumn. For borders and clumps. ONAGER. V. ŒEnothère. ONONIS A FEUILLES RONDES, Round-leaved Bugrane (Papilionaceae). - Hardy; clusters of yellow flowers streaked with bright pink. Multiply by seed or root cuttings in any soil, any exposure; renew the plant every two or three years. ARABIA ONOPORDE (Compositae). - Biennial plant, 2m,20 to 2mn,60; very large purple flowers in large heads. Sow in spring for flowers the following year. OPHRIS, V. Orchis, ORANGER, CITRUS AURANTIACUM (Aurantiaceae). - Orange trees are propagated by sowing, cuttings and marcottes in pots lined with orange-tree soil, a very complicated form of compost that used to be widely used, but is now replaced by free-range soil with the addition of one-third leaf compost and oily manure or heather soil. The young plant remains under a frame for three years, and in the fourth year is exposed to the air in summer. Escutcheon or Pontoise graft. V. Fruit trees, pome fruits, chap. xn, for further details that need not be repeated here. ORCHIS (Orchids). - Native orchids will do well in gardens if you give them the soil and exposure they find in meadows or woods, where they originally come from: Orchis fusca; with large spikes of brownish and white flowers. - O. militaire; with helmet-shaped flowers in various shades of purple and white. - O. taché; leaves and stem spotted with red, flowers punctuated with white and purple. - O. simia or O. singe; so named because it more or less imitates a hanging monkey. - O. de Robert; spike of greenish flowers with light purple labellum, etc. (1). ORIGANUM MAJORANA, Marjoram (Labiatae). - Native to the Orient. Grown in borders. - O. Dictamus from Crete; purplish flowers in June-July. Multiply from seed and smothered cuttings; in light soil. ELM, Ulmus (Ulmaceae). - Native, first-growth tree. Must be grafted to Om, 16 de terre. Exposure similar to that which works well in the primitive state. (1) See also Cypripedium, etc. ORNITHOGALE PYRAMIDAL, Milk ear, Virgin's ear (Liliaceae). - Indigenous. Height: Om, 50; starry white flowers in spikes. Light, nourishing soil; take care to lift the onion every two or three years to separate the cloves, which should be replanted in October. - Eleven o'clock lady. Height from Om, 15 to Om, 18; beautiful white, starry, fragrant flowers, in May-June; they open around eleven o'clock and close again around three o'clock in the afternoon, for two weeks, when the sun shines. Ordinary open ground. ORNUS EUROPÆA, Flowering Ash (Oleineae). - 6 to 10 meters tall; flowers with colored petals; produces a kind of manna. Like the Manna Ash; native to the East and Italy. ORPIN. V. Sedum orpin, OSMONDE ROYALE (Ferns). - Indigenous; with large bipinnate leaves 1 to 2 meters high, and terminated by a compact cluster of yellowish globules. Place in the shade, at the foot of rock gardens bathed in water, where it produces a very nice effect. OXALIDE (Oxalidae). - This genus includes numerous species with very pretty white, yellow, pink, purple or red flowers, and tuberous food roots. They can be propagated by bulbils, by cuttings or by separating the stems; all need daylight or air, all like pure or mixed heather soil, and can be placed in a hot greenhouse, temperate greenhouse or cold frame; annual species are planted in clumps or borders, in the open air, in spring. The pink-flowered Oxalis, 15-20 cm high, produces small pink flowers in loose clusters all summer long. Sow outdoors in April-May. Baskets, borders, flowerbeds. - O. De Deppe, with ten or twelve dark-red flowers, makes beautiful borders in vegetable gardens, etc., etc. OXIPETALE BLEU (Asclepiadae). - Voluble plant with clusters of azure-blue star-shaped flowers in summer. Sow indoors in autumn and transplant in spring, either in the garden or in pots; soft, smoky soil, moderate watering. P PÆONIA, Peony (Ranunculaceae). - Has several stems, from Om, 35 to 2 meters high, bearing large, multi-colored flowers in April, May and June. Multiply by dividing roots, taking cuttings, grafting or sowing; free soil or orange-tree soil with the addition of a quarter of heather soil. - P. Moutan produces beautiful double or semi-double flowers with golden-yellow stamens in April. Many varieties. - Adonis peony. PANDANE ODORANT (Pandanaceae). - Gladiate leaves, 1 to 1.30 m long; very fragrant male flowers in panicles; ball-shaped female flowers. Warm greenhouse; pineapple soil. Multip, by seeds brought from the plant's country. PANICAUT AMETHYSTE (Umbelliferae). - Indigenous. Height: Om 65; numerous blue-amethyst flowers. Multip, from seed sown in a terrine as soon as mature, or in the garden in March. PAPAVER SOMNIFERUM, Garden poppy (Papaveraceae). - Annual; height from Om, 70 to 1m, 30: double varieties of all colors, except blue. Multip, from seed sown in place in any soil. If you sow in autumn, you'll have flowers in June-July; if you sow in February and March, they'll come in August. - Poppy, with its vivid ponceau flowers in June and July, offers numerous varieties with single or double flowers, white or pink, or scarlet-red; sow on the ground in April-May or at the end of September. - The yellow-flowered P. Camrique should be sown in light, moist soil in a shady spot and cannot be transplanted. - 22 Papaver involucré; bright red flowers with a beautiful effect; transplant the young plant in place. - P. de Tournefort. - P. à bractées. - P. safrané. PAQUERETTE. V. Bellis perennis. PASSE-FLEUR. V. Althœa rosea. PASSIFLORE GRENADILLE, Passion Flower (Passiflora). - A numerous genus of climbing plants, armed with tendrils, remarkable for the beauty and singularity of their flowers, which appear to be the instruments of the Passion, hence their name : Passiflora. - Passiflora edulis, 6 to 8 meters high; native to Mexico; its fruits are edible; it bears blue flowers; it is grown in a warm greenhouse and in the open ground, covered in winter, PAULOWNIA IMPERIAL (Scrophularinea). - Native to Japan, a tall, handsome tree about 12 meters high, with broad leaves and violet-blue flowers dotted with brown and yellow lines, with a violet fragrance. Multiplies by cutting root sections. Full soil. PAVOT. V. Papaver. PELARGONIUM (Geraniaceae). - This genus, which comprises nearly 600 species, almost all of which are native to the Cape, is wrongly confused with the Geranium genus, from which it differs in having flowers with unequal petals, whereas the Geranium has regular flowers. New varieties are obtained by sowing in spring in trays or terrines lined with light soil; as soon as the young plants are strong enough, they are transplanted into small pots, and later into large pots. If you're not looking for varieties, take cuttings from selected individuals from July to September. Water moderately; keep them clean; keep the greenhouse temperature 5 to 12°C above zero until April, when your Pelargoniums will flower; then give them some air: never keep them more than 1.40 m away from the glass. Bring Pelargoniums into the greenhouse at the beginning of October. These include: P. d'Endlicher with pink flowers. - P. zonale with flowers changing from brilliant scarlet to pink and pure white. - P. à feuilles tachantes. - P. triste. - P. à fleurs en tête or Geranium rosat. Large-flowered Pelargoniums. P. Alphonse Duval. P. Anacreon. P. ardens. P. belle Milanaise. P. Clara. P. de Candolle. P. Egérie. P. Galathéa. P. Gloire de Paris. P. Linnée. P. P. Vesuvius. Florence. P. Neptune. P. Thisbé. P. Vulcan. P. Theophrastus. P. Royal-Albert. P. Reine blanche, etc. Fancy pelargoniums. P. Adonis. P. Arabella. P. Bella. P. delicata. P. Edith. P. king of Roses. P. Rachel. P. Sarah. P. Venus, etc. Pelargoniums with scarlet flowers for open ground. P. Comtesse de Chambord. P. Beauty. P. Alice. P. Archbishop of Paris. P. bel demonio. P. Diogènc. P. Gloire des Roses. P. prince of Wales. P. Sobiesky. Pelargoniums with variegated leaves. P. Alma. P. bijou. P. brillant. P. purple cord. P. élégans. P. Fairy queen. P. Mary Ellen, P. M. Lenox. P. perfection. P. Reine d'or. P. Talma. PENSTEMON CAMPANULE (Scrophularinées). - With campanulate flowers, red on the outside, whitish on the inside: multiplies by seed, cuttings and splinters; outdoors in summer; in orangeries in winter. Several varieties: with carmine-red flowers, light blue, etc., etc. SNOWBED. V. Spring snowflower. PERILLA NANKINENSIS, Nanking perilla (Labiatae). - Om, 60 to Om, 80 tall; purple-black foliage with metallic sheen; rather insignificant violet-pink flowers from Sept. to Nov. Edges. Sow outdoors in March or April; grows like Basil. PERSICAIRE. V. Polygonum: PERVENCHE, V. Vinça. PÉTUNIA ODORANT (Solanaceae). - Height: Om, 70 to 1 meter; large, fragrant white flowers, summer-autumn. Multiply by seed, splinters and cuttings in loose, light soil. Sow indoors in March-April, or outdoors in April-May in a nursery. - P. with violet flowers, purple-purple flowers, fragrant towards evening. Beautiful varieties obtained by sowing. Trellises, railings, balconies, cradles. PHLOX (Polemoniaceae). - Phlox vary in color from violet to purple, pink to lilac and purpurin, depending on the species; they can reach heights of up to 1 meter; multiply from cuttings or by dividing clumps in mid-shade heathland; water generously in hot weather. The main species include: Drummond's P.; annual. - Oval-leaved P. - Spring P. - P. acuminata. - Setaceous P. - P. pyramidal, etc., etc. - P. with subulate leaves. Enthusiasts especially appreciate the Phlox: Alphonse Karr; purplish salmon. - Docteur Parnot; bright red. - Madame Antin; purplish red with purple center. - M. Delamarre; the most beautiful of all. - Comte de Lambertye; purplish-red, purple center. - Pius IX; salmon color. - Professor Kock; salmon-purple. - Souvenir de Rouen; salmon-purple. - Souvenir des Ternes; white background. - Madame Levrat; pink with purple center, etc. PHOENIX, Dattier (Palms). - This tree almost always withers in our greenhouses, where it needs a temperature of -22°. PIED D'ALOUETTE. V. Delphinium. PINUS ABIES, V. Abies sapin. The limits of this book do not allow us to go into greater detail on Conifers than is given in the article ABIES. PISTACHIER TEREBINTHUS, Pistachier térébinthe, P. sauvage (Anacardiaceae). - Twiggy shrub, leaves composed of seven to nine oval, oblong, obtuse leaflets, green and shiny, a little whitish underneath, small, paniculate flowers (June-July), replaced by pea-sized drupes. In warm climates, this shrub releases a resin known as turpentine through the cracks in its bark. At first liquid, then yellowish, then bluish, it thickens on contact with air. P. can be planted in the ground when five or six years old. - Cover in winter. Multiply from seed sown on a bed and under a frame in spring; transplant into pots. Orangery. Fig. 21 - Pistachio tree. PITTOSPORUM ONDULÉ (Pittospora). - Canary Islands. Height: 2 meters; white, jasmine-scented flowers bloom in spring. - P. with thick leaves, white flowers bloom in May. Multip, from marcots and cuttings. - Rolled P. Fruits resemble small lemons and contain coral-colored seeds. - P. de la Chine. Height: 3 meters; white, orange-blossom-scented flowers bloom all summer. Multiplication by seed, cuttings and marcots; orangery or outdoors. The various Pittospores can be grafted onto P. ondulé; they require light, free-form soil; pots or crates. PLAGIUS A GRANDES FLEURS (Composites). - Algeria. 22. Perennial. Height: 2 meters. Disk of yellowish florets, without rays. Propagates from seed or splinters. Orangery and outdoors. Water infrequently in winter. PIVOINE. V. Pœonia. PLANERA CRÉNELÉ, Siberian elm, Zelkoua (Ulmaceae). - Can be grafted onto common elm. - P. à feuilles d'Orme. PLATANE D'ORIENT (Platanaceae). - Height: 20 meters and more. Blooms in May. Any soil, but preferably light, deep and sheltered. Multipl. by seed, bedding and cuttings. - Maple-leaved P. - Corner-leaved P. - P. d'Occident or Virginia. Cool soil; seeds sown as soon as mature, and covered with soft, moist moss. JAPAN PLATYCARYA (Juglandées). - Its habit is reminiscent of Sumac; its male flowers are united in catkins like Willow flowers; its fruits resemble those of the Aune. BIG FLOWER PLATYCODON (Campanulaceae). - Large blue flower, Om,07, opens in July. Mixed heather soil; mid-sun; sow in spring; transplant in autumn. Varieties with double flowers, white, blue, etc. - Autumn planting. Very rustic. PLATYLORIUM ELEGANT (Papilionaceae). - Australia; large orange-yellow flowers with carmine spot at base of standard. - P. with lanceolate leaves. Height: 1 meter; yellow standard, attractive red carina. Orangery; heather soil. Seeds sown on bed and under frame; temperate greenhouse. PLECTRANTHUS WITH NETTLE LEAVES. - Height ? Om, 70; light blue flowers with purplish tint. Light, non-moist, free soil; multiple seeds. Sow in spring on warm bed and under frame. - P. nudiflore; temperate greenhouse. E PLEROMA DE BENTHAM (Melastomaceae). - Brazil; large Om 05 flowers, purple-purple with white nails. Multip, from cuttings; light soil; warm greenhouse. PLUMBAGO ZEYLANICA, Ceylon lacewort (Plumbaginea). - Height: Om, 50; white flowers in August-September. Very warm exposure; free soil; frequent watering in summer, infrequent in winter. Warm greenhouse. Multiply by seed, sow in bed. - Climbing P. - P. rose. Root propagation. - Cape P. Height: 3 meters. Tender blue flowers. Temperate greenhouse. - P. de lady Larpent (China). (Cobalt-blue flowers, then violet. Light, dry soil. Multip. of splinters; temperate greenhouse in winter; outdoors in May. PLUMIERA RUBRA, Red Frangipani (Apocynia). - Fragrant, light-red flowers; tanned and warm greenhouse. Multip. of cuttings. - P. with yellow flowers. Same culture. PODALYRIA ARGENTÉE (Papilionaceae). - Height: 1m, 60. milky-white flower with rust-colored calyx; multip. of seeds and cuttings; bright strap; orangery. - P. silky, with pink flowers. - P. with two lovely purplish-blue flowers. Temperate greenhouse; clean, light soil. PODOCARPUS EFFILÉ (Conifers). - From Cape Town. Mixed heather soil; orangery. - P, nuciferous. Same culture. PODOLEPIS WITH GOLDEN YELLOW FLOWERS (Compositae). - Height: Om, 30-33; flowers bloom in July-October. - P. with meaty flowers. Height: Om, 70; flowers vary from soft pink to pure white. For both these plants, sow in April on bed, requip on bed; plant out in May. TRILOBED LEAF PODOLOBIUM (Papilionaceae). - Australia. Height: 1m, 60. Yellow flowers bloom in June-July; heather soil; temperate greenhouse. Multiply with cuttings. PODOPHYLLUM WITH PELTED LEAVES (Berberiaceae). - North America. Hardy, perennial. White flowers with 9 divisions, 3 of which are smaller. Multiply by offshoots. - P. palmate. - P. of Emodus. POGOSTEMON, Patchouly (Labiatae). - Indo-China. Pale violet flowers; its leaves provide a well-known perfumed oil. Light soil; temperate greenhouse; multiply from cuttings. POINCIANA or Magnificent Poincillade (Caesalpinaceae). - India. Height: 3 meters and more; reddish-white flowers; yellow-flowered variety; warm greenhouse. - P. de Gillies. Height: 2 meters. Yellow flowers; very long stamens, purplish-purple, forming an aigrette. Light free soil. Multiply by cuttings and seed. Temperate greenhouse. POINSETTIA ÉCLATANT (Euphorbiaceae). - Mexico. Height: 2 meters. Greenish flowers with greenish-red or bright ponceau collars; variety with yellow bracts. Cut back branches after bracts have fallen off. - P. heterophylla. Height: 1 meter. Flowers with scarlet bracts. Warm greenhouse. Multip, from smothered cuttings. Temperate greenhouse. SWEET PEA. V. Chickweed. PLEMOINE. Greek valerian (Polemoniaceae). - Height: Om, 30 to Om, 40; clusters of blue flowers in June-July. Sow in May in nurseries, plant out the following spring. Renew the plant every year. LARGE-FLOWERED PRIMEMER (Primulaceae). - Indigenous and perennial; with uniflorous trunks, sulfur-yellow flowers turning green with drying; numerous varieties. - Bear's ear; flowers yellow, purple or variegated purple, red and white. Light, cool soil; north or east-facing exposure. Multiply by separating plants in autumn or by sowing, from December to March, in good heather soil. - P. officinale, Cuckoo flower; yellow, cup-shaped flowers. - P. élevée; with higher stems than the preceding one. - P. de Palinure; yellow flowers. - P. à feuilles de Cortuse; umbel of purple flowers, blooming in April-May. Sinensis, or P. de la Chine. - P. candélabre. 8 to 12 flowers, pink blade, yellow disk, swollen calyx, heather soil mixed with well-consumed compost; temperate greenhouse. S. erecta superba, with pinkish-copper flowers, with carmine or purplish highlights. Multiplied by cuttings, seeds and splinters. GARDEN WORTH (Ranunculaceae). - Perennial; root (claw) with finger-like divisions, joined to a trunk which bears 1 to 3 eyes at the top. Height: Om 16 to Om, 50; beautiful white flowers with 5 yellow or red petals and numerous pistils and stamens. There are many varieties with single, semi-double and double flowers in very bright and diverse shades. In general, Ranunculus needs light, fresh soil, even a little sandy, but substantial and not too stony; add a little leaf compost. Exposure to the east. Multiply from seeds which, on average, take about 40 days to sprout; the young plant needs to be protected from frost by a good bedding. The first year's plant should be lifted and placed in new soil. A strong stem; a full corolla devoid of any hint of stamens or pistils; a regular circumference of the whole plant, about Om.50 in diameter - these are the characteristics of the most remarkable varieties. Weeding; watering until deflowering - Monkshood. Silver bud. Native; pretty, very double flowers of pure white, blooming in May-June. Multiplying in the third year after leaf fall. - R. acre. Buttercup. Indigenous. Charming, domed, bright-yellow flowers in June; change location every two years. - R. creeping, Cocksfoot; double yellow flowers in the cultivated variety. Multiply by nets and runners in light free-rooting soil in part shade. Renoncules planted in autumn flower earlier, are larger and more durable than those planted in winter. Seeds can be stored for several years in paper bags in a dry place. RÉSÉDA ODORANT (Résédacées). - Perennial; greenish, very fragrant flowers. Any soil, not too damp. Sow outdoors from April to June. If you want beautiful specimens, cut the rising stem as soon as it begins to bear fruit; in a temperate greenhouse, you'll get a shrub that lives two or three years and flowers all winter long; it sometimes reaches 2.50 m. - R. with large flowers. Height: Om, 40; sow outdoors in April or in August, but need a frame to protect them from the winter cold, and put them in place in April. In England, fragrant Reseda can be seen 8 to 10 years old. Pontique. 2.50 to 3 or more meters high; large, purplish-purple flowers in May; remarkable white-flowered varieties, with white and yellow variegated leaves obtained from seed and perpetuated only by grafting. - R. with pink flowers, etc., etc. - R. de Catawba with white, pink, lilac or purple flowers with macules of various shades. In general, R. requires slightly sandy, moist soil; north or east-facing exposure. Propagate by grafting and marcotting; from seed in dull beds in spring, or in heather soil terrines always surrounded by water; transplant the seedling in the second year, and again two years later. Can be grown in pots. COMMON RICE, Palma christi (Euphorbiaceae). - Annual. Height: 1.50 to 2 meters; leaves with 7 digitations; clusters of male flowers at base, leaves at top, July-August. Light soil; southern exposure. Multiply from seed in spring, in situ; woody species can be stored in an orangery. ROBINIA PSEUDO-ACACIA, Robinier-Acacia blanc (Papilionaceae). - Native to Virginia, North America. Height: 16 to 24 meters; clusters of hanging white flowers, late May and June. Multiply from offshoots or shallowly sown seeds in March-April; light, cool soil. Its roots are tracing; protect nearby trees from their approach. OFFICINAL ROSEMARY (Labiatae). - Indigenous. Height: 1.50 m and over; pale blue flowers in February-May. Warm, sheltered exposure; light soil. Propagates by cuttings, marcots and splinters. White variegated varieties. Water and mow. COMMON RONCE, Rubus fruticosus. - Indigenous. Non-spiny varieties with white fruit, variegated leaves and double white flowers are cultivated. Terre franche; mid-sun; pruned in spring. TREMENDOUS ROSE. V. Althœa rosea. ROSE TREE (Rosaceae). - Poets have celebrated the Rose; the centuries have only confirmed its title of Queen of Flowers, which it deserves for its incomparable grace and marvelous beauty, its sweet fragrance and elegant habit. Rosebushes need good, clean, dry, sandy, slightly deep soil, fertilized with cow manure; plenty of air; it's best to move the plants every two years if the above conditions are not met. Choice of rosehips: Prefer vigorous two- to three-year-old rosehips, from which you will remove a large part of the stump, keeping only a good heel and the rootlets if there are any; smooth the surface of the wound with the serpette; make sure there is nothing left of the bruising caused by the action of the saw. Rosehip, long. R. canina quickly produces large heads. Plant in autumn, if the soil is not too damp; or in spring, in a nursery. Mode of reproduction: 1° by sowing; 2° by rooted ~& S offshoots; 3° by layering; 4° by cuttings; 5° by grafting. 1° Sowing is the only way to obtain beautiful double-flowered varieties. Take your seeds from the most double-flowered varieties, the most graceful forFig. 22. - Roses. 3 varieties, the most graceful in form and habit, and the richest in shade; sow immediately in pots or flowerbeds close to a wall and shelter in winter; if you choose spring, first soak your seeds in water for twenty-four hours. When the plants are about Om, 25 in height, in the second year, you transplant them into light, substantial soil Om, 15 deep, keeping a distance of about Om, 30 in all directions between each plant; it's hardly until the fourth year that they show their flowers. Seeds of Bengal, Tea, Hazelnut and Ile-Bourbon roses sown in spring sometimes produce flowers in the first year. Hybrid Roses (produced by mixing the seminal dust of different species purposely brought together in cultivation) take an average time to flower between the late and early species from which they come. 2° By offshoots: after separating the shoots produced by the roots from their mother, use them only to multiply free-standing individuals. 3° By "marcottes": you enclose one or more stems in a hollow furrow, where you hold them with a small branch forming a fork; the marcotte must have one or two eyes above ground; in spring, small beads with nourishing roots form around the buried eyes; only then wean the marcotte and plant it separately. This procedure is used for low-stemmed, bushy Roses; for headed Roses, use a pot filled with good soil, supported by a stand, with an opening about 2 or 3 centimetres wide at the side, through which you can insert the branch. In both cases, water and cover with straw. For certain lazy species, you can activate layering by making an incision, ligature or twist above the point where the roots are to emerge, three ways of blocking the passage of the descending sap and retaining it for the benefit of the layering. 4° By cuttings (see Cutting, Part I). Cuttings are rightly preferred for all softwood varieties: hazelnut, tea, Bengal, Bourbon and Portland roses, which are free-standing. When replanting, it's best to bury the graft a few centimeters below the surface, so that a bead forms at its base from which roots can grow; the grafted branch will then be free. 5° Grafting. Roses are grafted either in escutcheon or in cleft (V. Grafting. Part I). The escutcheon graft, preferable to the other, is used in May when the wood of the subject is strong, rich in sap and allows only its bark to come off. Growing-eye grafting is used when the eyes develop rapidly, producing flowering buds that can be used in autumn for dormant-eye grafting. Dormant-eye grafting is carried out from July to mid-September, then quickly resumes, but does not grow until the following spring; it therefore sleeps for six months; hence its name. Forced grafting takes place in January-February; ordinary split grafting in March-April; cover wounds with grafting wax. Do not empty the bud's eye, which is the shoot's germ; leave some call eyes above the bud, to attract the sap; however, if these eyes become too greedy, pinch them promptly and repeatedly without destroying them until the grafting is complete. Pruning Roses. - For delicate species, prune after flowering; for others, in the first few days of March, removing all diseased or old branches; prune back any weak branches to three or four eyes; some varieties want to be pruned much longer; experience alone is a guide here. Rejuvenating Roses. - If free-standing Roses rejuvenate naturally thanks to their annual shoots and the removal of branches that no longer produce flowers, it is worth explaining, in a few lines, the means indicated by the 19th century Maison rustique for prolonging the vigor and life of grafted Roses. "In England, the most skilful horticulturists have found a way of prolonging the life of Rosebushes grafted on Rosehips, by removing them every three years in mid-February, before vegetation resumes..... Damaged roots are removed with the greatest care, all healthy roots are shortened, and almost all hair is removed, since at this time of the Rose's existence, it is either non-existent or almost dead. The head is then subjected to severe pruning, which provokes very active growth; the soil around the trunks is renewed or fertilized with a well-consumed manure, and the Rose is immediately put back into place. The operation must be carried out quickly enough to ensure that the roots are not exposed to the air for longer than is strictly necessary. Forced cultivation of Roses. - (All potted Roses can be forced into a temperate or hot greenhouse, but usually only remontant Roses are forced to flower early and artificially, in the open ground under trays heated by manure heaters or a thermosiphon. Care must be taken to give the plants a good day, plenty of air and watering. Smoke and direct sunlight are harmful (see also Insectes nuisibles, Part I). Among the 3,000 varieties of Roses, we will indicate the most interesting of each tribe. 1st Tribe. - Banks Roses. There are two varieties, white and yellow; the latter is preferable and much used in fences, against walls where it produces a graceful effect, in spring with its flowers, all summer with its green foliage. It does not remont. Sheltered exposure. 2nd Tribe. - Cinnamon Roses. So named for the color of their bark. Generally thornless, they are preferred for beds in landscape gardens. They require little pruning. The main cultivated species include: Alpine Rose, with its many varieties. - R. cannelle, strictly speaking, and R. soufré, with yellow flowers, very double and difficult to open. None of these species are remontant. 3rd Tribe. - Pimprenelle roses. Their type is R. pimpinellifolia, which grows on high mountains and offers many varieties. R. pimprenelle want to be placed north, grown free-standing and in clumps, and if you 23. -- If you ask for a lot of flowers, don't prune them too much. The most beautiful burnet roses are : Persian Yellow, l'Ancienne jaune, and Perpétuelle Stanwell. 4° Tribu. - Hundred-leaf Roses. Their type is the R. Cent-feuilles, originally from northern Persia, but widespread everywhere for many centuries. The R. Cent-feuilles (Cabasse Rose) is rightly called the most beautiful rose in the world; it does not rebloom; hybrid varieties are obtained by crossing, which are as beautiful as the Cent-feuilles Rose, their mother, and which, being more generous than it, give flowers several times a year. 1st Sub-tribe. - Remontant hybrids. TULIPS (Liliaceae). - Tulips are propagated by cloves and seedlings, taking care to: 1° choose a good south-east or south-west exposure; clean, loose, substantial or slightly sandy soil, but not damp; 2° plant from November 10 to 25, digging as many MAS .3 bt. of pits as you wish to make beds; 3° lift every two years, as soon as the leaves have faded, to replant immediately in well-prepared soil; 4° weed well; 5° shelter from winter cold and rain. Caiuses always produce plants identical to those from which they originate; the seeds of a Tulip do not reproduce its variety. A beautiful Tulip should have a straight, well-proportioned stem; the flower should be 1/5 longer than it is wide, with a white base, harmoniously rounded divisions at the top, and at least three bright, strong colors. Tulip varieties number in the hundreds. Let's just mention: Gessner's or florists' tulip, bare stem, obtuse divisions with colored or uncolored bottoms, giving rise to two divisions: bizarre tulips and white-bottomed tulips or Flemish tulips; - Tole's tulip, reddish-yellow at the tips; -- Sun's eye tulip, red with a long black spot bordered with yellow on each division, etc., etc. SWEET COLTSFOOT. Warm greenhouse, peaty soil and moss. V. Orchis, ZYGOPHYLLUM, Fabagella commune. V. Fabagella. Vocabulary of key gardening and botanical terms (1). A ACCLIMATER. To accustom a plant to the temperature and influences of a new climate. ACEROUS (leaves). Said of leaves that are long and pointed like a needle: pine leaves. ACICULAR. Narrow, linear, roughly cylindrical leaves: Acacia. ACOTYLÉDONES. Refers to plants without lobes or cotyledons: Algae, Mushrooms, Lichens, Mosses, Ferns, etc. ACUMINATED. Finished in a long, thin point. ADHERENT. Said of any part of a plant that is more or less intimately connected with the surrounding parts. ADNÉ, Refers to any organ attached laterally along its entire length to another part: anther of the Ranunculus. ADOS. An elevation of the ground in the shape of the back of a chest, wider at the bottom than at the top; ados are used to protect (1) We will only give here the notions that are absolutely necessary for the understanding of the book. a crop from the bad weather of the off-season, to provide early crops with good exposure to the south. ADVENTIFS, accidental. Said of buds and roots born on points other than those where they are usually seen. AFFRANCHIR. A grafted tree is said to be freeing itself when, from the grafted spot, it sprouts roots that sink into the earth. AGREE. Flowers united on a common receptacle, with distinct anthers: Leaded, Valerian, etc., etc. AIGRETTE. A collection of hairs of various shapes that crown certain seeds and fruits. It is only a particular form of the calyx blade, according to Richard: Valerian, Dandelion, etc. AIGRIN, from sour. Name given to wild apple and pear trees. AIGUILLONS. Hard, sharp projections, entirely composed of cellular tissue, which appear in greater or lesser numbers on plants and adhere only to the surface of the plant, as distinct from spines, which are integral with the parts in which they appear. WING. Part of the papilionaceous corolla: Legumes. WING. Winged stem, that on which the leaf blade usually extends: Comfrey, etc.; winged fruits those whose pericarp enlarges into a membrane: Elm, Maple. AISSELLE. Inward angle below the attachment of a leaf to a branch or a branch to a stem. The leaf axils usually contain the buds and very often the flowers, which are then said to be axillary. ALBUMEN. Name given to the part of the kernel that accompanies the embryo: coffee, cereals, poppies, etc., etc. ALPINES (Plants), Name given not only to plants native to the Alps, but also to those growing on other high mountains. ALTERNATE. Said of the superposition with mutual succession of the same organs of a plant on a common axis. In this sense, leaves are alternate as opposed to opposite or whorled. Petals are Alternate to sepals in the greatest number of cases; stamens are Alternate to petals when they are in the same number as the latter. Oak, Cherry, etc. have alternate leaves. AMANDE. Fruit seeds, their essential part. AMENTACEOUS. V. Kitten. AMPLEXICAULE. Said of any part whose base surrounds the stem; leaf or petiole. ANDROCEE. Stamens or male organs. ANNUAL (Plant). That which, in the course of a year, germinates, flowers, bears its seed and dies. ANTHERE. Upper end of the stamen, containing the pollen. AOUTÉE (Young branch). The wood of which has hardened before winter. APETALE. Flower without petals, hence without corolla: Daphne. APHYLLE. Devoid of leaves: Dodder. TREE. Plant whose woody trunk exceeds 6 meters in height. SHRUB. Woody plant rising from 1 to 6 meters. SHRUB. Woody plant rising only from Om, 35 to 1 meter: Rosemary, etc. ARÈ́TE. Beard or extension of balls or feathers in Grasses. ARTICULATED. Having or simulating several knots. AUBIER. The outermost layers of wood in dicotyledonous trees and the subfibers encrusted with resinous material; a new sapwood is formed every year. B 429 BACCIFEROUS. Bearing berries. BACCIFORME. Before the shape of a berry. BAIE. Fleshy, indehiscent fruit whose seeds are scattered in a succulent pulp when ripe. BALIVEAU. Young, unpruned tree, growing straight up with all its branches. BALLS or Glumes, floral envelope of grasses. BASE. Lower part by which the petal is held to the receptacle, the leaf to the stem, etc. BASIFIX. Attached at the base. BASILAIRE (Style). Starting from the base of the ovary. BASSINER. To water lightly in the form of fine rain. BIFIDE. Split deeply in two. BIFURQUÉE (Branch or stem). Splitting in two. BILABIA. Calyx or corolla whose limb has two main divisions or lips, one above the other. BILOBE. Divided into two lobes up to the middle. BILOCULAR. Presenting two compartments. BINAGE. Action of hoeing. BINER. To give a second way to soil, with a spade or hoe, to prevent it from hardening, or to destroy weeds; depth from em, 06 to Om, 08. BIPENNÉ. V. Penned. BISANNUEL. A plant that takes two years to set seed and wither: Digitale. BITERNEOUS. Leaves borne on a petiole which divides into 2 petioles, which in turn subdivide into two others. BORGNE. Deprived of terminal bud. BORNER. To prick out a young plant; to surround the roots by bringing the soil closer together with the planter. BOURGEON. A kind of small tuber that encloses leaves and stems before they develop. BOUTON. Said particularly of flowers before they bloom. BOUTURE. A branch detached from a tree and planted so that it can take root. BRACTÉES, or floral leaves. Small, sessile leaves, often colored, close to the flowers and sometimes intermingling with them: Sage. BRINDLE. Last offshoot of a branch; fruiting branch, slender and short. BULB. Underground, rounded stem: onion, lily, etc. BUI.BIFER. Giving bulbils at stem joints or axils, instead of flowers. BULBILLE. Small bulb born in the leaf axils of certain plants, or in place of flowers. 4 3 1 CADUC. Falling very quickly, as soon as its functions are fulfilled. CAIEU, Small bud forming on the side of an old onion or bulb: Hyacinth. CALCARIFORM. Spur-shaped: corolla of Linears, calyx of Nasturtiums, etc. CALCE. V. Basic botanical notions. - Find in this vocabulary the qualifiers given to this part of the flower. CALICINAL. Taking after the calyx, or resembling it. CAMBIUM. The tissues in the process of formation, still gelatinous and soft. CAMPANIFORM, CAMPANULATED. Refers to bell-shaped corollas and calyxes. CAPILLAIRE: Fine as a hair. CAPITATED. Finished at the top. CAPOT. Diminutive of layer: Hot manure covered with Om, 15 to Om, 25 earth for planting melons, cucumbers, etc. CAPSULE. Dry fruit with one or more cavities, CARENE or NACELLE. Lower petals fused into a carina in the flowers of Peas, etc., etc. CAULINAIRE. Attached to or dependent on the stem. CHATON. Long, flexible spike, somewhat similar to a tail; incomplete flowers: Coudrier, Willow, etc. CHAUME. Grass stem. CHEVELU. Small hair-like roots. CILIÉ. Bordered by hairs, arranged much like eye lashes. CIRRHIFEROUS. Before tendrils, CLAVIFORME. Club-shaped. COADNÉES. V. Cone-shaped. COLLERETTE or INVOLUCRE. First envelope of certain flowers: Umbelliferae, Ranunculaceae, etc. COLLET. Ideal point marking the separation of the stem from the root. COMPOUND. Leaves whose parts separate without tearing at the end of their life: Acacia. -7 i . 44 E 27 1 432 VOCÁBULARY. CONJUGUED. Bound together. CONNECTED. Leaves placed opposite each other on the stem and welded at the base. CONNECTIVE. Fleshy part following the fillet and separating the two anther chambers. HULL. Capsule, with several chambers, separating from each other: Euphorbias. CORDIFORME. Heart-shaped: Lilac leaf. COROLLE. See the chapter on the flower and, in the Vocabulary, the qualifiers added to this word. CORYMBE. Species of umbel whose peduncles or rays start at different points, although the flowers all arrive at about the same height and form a kind of parasol: Cherry. COTYLEDONS. V. Notions of elementary botany. LYING. Lying down. V. Marcotte. COURSONS. Branches cut short. CRAN, To make a notch, is to remove transversely a small piece of bark and wood, above an eye or bud. CRENELLED. A leaf with broad, rounded teeth. CUNEIFORM. Wedge-shaped. CUPULE. Envelope of the acorn. DECURRENT. Leaf whose blade extends onto the petiole or stem: certain thistles. DEDOSS. To separate a large clump of perennial roots into several small clumps. DEHISCENCE. Action by which distinct vulvas, joined by a weld, separate regularly without tearing along this weld. HALF-FLOWER. Very small flower with a leaf blade extended into a tongue on the outer side. DICHOTOMES. Stems or branches bifurcated or subdivided from two to two. DIGITAL. Leaves with leaflets imitating an open hand: Potentilles. D DRAGEON. Young underground stem. DRAIN, to drain off excess water, by means of furrows, gullies, stones, etc., etc., etc. DRUPE. Fleshy fruit with a single seed enclosed in a bony pit: peach, etc. E 433 SPLIT. To separate the roots or shoots of a plant. ENSIFORME. Having the shape of a sword. EPIPHYLLE. Inflorescence of certain plants where the flowers are mistakenly thought to be born on the leaves: PetitHoux. EPIPHYTE. Plant that vegetates on other plants: Orchids, etc. ÉTENDARD. Name of the upper petal in the flowers of Peas, Lupines, etc. LEAVES. See the various adjectives applied to leaves. FIBEROUS. Root whose bundle starting from the collar consists of thin, elongated threads: Wheat. FLOSCULAR. Flower head composed of florets: Thistle. FOVILLA. Essential element of pollen. FRONDES. Leaf-like organs of ferns. FUSIFORM. Spindle-shaped root. G GIBBOUS. Calyxes or corollas with bulges or bumps: snapdragon, etc. GLABER. Hairless. GLADIED. V. Ensiform. GLANDES. Small vesicular bodies found on various parts of plants: Myrtle, Orange, etc., and usually contain an essential oil. 25 GLAUOUE. Bluish green. GYNECEA. The female organs of a plant. H HASTÉ, Said of spade-shaped leaves: field bindweed, sorrel, etc. HETEROPHYLL. Plant with differently shaped leaves: Water Crowfoot, etc. HYBRID. Plants produced by two species of the same genus or even by two species of different genera; hybrids rarely produce fertile seeds. HYPOCERIFORM. Corolla whose straight, elongated tube ends abruptly in a spreading blade: Periwinkle. IMBRIOUÉ. Arranged like the tiles of a roof: Sedum, Joubarbe. INDEHICENT. Not opening. INERMAL, Without thorns. INFLORESCENCE. Arrangement of flowers on a plant. Inflorescence in spike, cluster, capitulum, fascicle, umbel. See these words. INFUNDIBULIFORM. Funnel-shaped: Bindweed. INVOLUCRE. V. Collar. L LABELLE. Internal division of the Orchid flower. LABIÉ. Lip-shaped. LACINIÉ. Having fine, uneven indentations: grape leaf. LAGENIFORME, Bottle-shaped. LEGUMINOUS. A large, natural family, details of which can be found in the cultivation of genera. 435 MACROPHYLLOUS. With long leaves. MARCESCENT. Calyx and corolla that dry out without falling off. MARCOTTE. Branch held to the tree and laid down in the ground to make it produce roots. FURNITURE. Soft earth, dividing well. MICROPHYLLE. Small leaves. NECTAIRES. Small bodies distilling a sweet liquor. NUCELLE. Pulpy nipple through which the ovule begins to manifest itself in the ovary. EYELETONS. Projections from certain roots: Artichoke. OMBELLE. Arrangement of flowers in a parasol; when the peduncles or pedicels start from the same point and arrive at the same level, the partial umbel is called an umbellule. ONGLET. Lower part of the petal. ORBICULAR. circle-shaped. PALEA. Leaf with 5 or 7 divisions that somewhat resemble fingers and meet at a common center representing the palm of the hand. PANICULE. A kind of compound cluster whose lower branches, longer than the upper ones, give the whole inflorescence a pyramidal shape: Yucca. PAPILIONACEES. Flowers so called because of their supposed resemblance to butterflies. V. Leguminosae. PARITETAL. Placenta barely extending into the ovary cavity: poppy, PARPENNEE. Compound leaf not ending in an odd leaflet. PECTINATE. Leaves having their indentations placed in two parallel rows like the teeth of a comb. PEDATE or PEDALATED. Leaves whose petiole divides at the tip into two divergent parts. PELTED. Shield-shaped. PENICELLED. Brush-shaped. PENNATIFIDES. Leaves with indentations not split up to three-quarters: Chamomile, Romaine. PHYLLODE. Expanded petiole whose blade does not develop. PLACENTA. Organ on which ovules are inserted. PLUMEOUS. Lined with hairs arranged like the barbs of a feather. PLURILOCULAR, Fruit with several lodges. PROLIFEROUS. Any flower from the center of which another flower emerges: some roses and carnations. PROVINE. To multiply by provins or marcottes. PUBESCENT. Covered with a light down. PULP. Flesh of soft, succulent fruits: Grape, currant, etc. PYRIRORME. Pear-shaped. PYXIDE. Opening like a soap box: Chickweed, Henbane, etc. R RACHIS. Part of the petiole to which the leaflets of compound leaves are attached. RACINE. See the various qualifiers added to this word. RADICANT. Said of a plant whose branches sprout roots. RADIANT. Arranged in rays from a common center: daisy, etc. REGIME. Name given to the cluster of flowers or fruits of certain plants: Palm trees, Banana trees, etc. RENIFORM. Kidney-shaped. NETWORKED. Intertwined like the meshes of a net. RHIZOME. Root-like stem extending horizontally: Iris, etc. ROCAMBOLE. V. Bulblet. RONCINATE. Pennatifid leaf whose strips run from top to bottom: Dandelion. ROTACÉE, wheel-shaped flower. SAGITTÉE. Shaped like an arrowhead: Sagittarius. SAUVAGEON. Tree that has not been grafted. SCABRE. Stems or leaves dotted with spots that are rough to the touch. SETACEOUS. Leaf as loose as silk. SETIGEROUS. Bearing one or more silks. SINUATE. Leaf with rounded, very open indentations: Oak. SPADICE. Spike of incomplete flowers, first enveloped in a sort of cone called a Spathe, the males at the top, the females at the base: Calf's foot. SPATHE. V. Spadice. Membranous envelope peculiar to Narcissus, etc. SPATULATED. Elongated like a spatula, a kind of flat spoon used by pharmacists, etc. STIPULES. Small leaves at the base of the leaf. SUBULAR. Leaf linear at its base and ending like an awl. SURGEON. Offshoots of a shrub. TABLIER. V. Labellum. TERNEA. Three leaves forming a sort of collar around the stem or twig. THYRSE. Panicle whose middle stalks are longer than the ends. TOMENTOUS. Loaded with tightly packed hairs: Poppy. TRICUSPIDATE. Fillet or other organ with three points. TRIFID. Split into three fairly deeply. TRIFOLIOLATE. With three leaflets borne on a common petiole: Clover. TUBEROUS. Root very swollen towards the middle: Dahlia. TUBULAR. Cylindrical flower ending in a more or less open, often divided blade: Jasmine, Lilac, etc. TURBINED. Top-shaped. TLRION. The bud arising immediately on the rhizomes: Asparagus. U UNIFLORE. Having only one flower. URCÉOLÉ. Bell-shaped. VESICULAR. Bladder-like calyx inflated with air. VITTÉES. Refers to the oil-filled strips found on the petals of some St. John's wort plants and on the fruits of some Umbelliferae. VIVACE. Herbaceous plant that lasts several years. VOLUBILE. Stem that curls to one side in all individuals of the same species. BOTANY is the study of plants, which it teaches us to know, distinguish and classify. Plant Physics or Organic Botany is the part of the science that considers plants as organized beings, with a kind of life, indicated by the inner structure and action of each of their organs. Examine a plant with the naked eye, or with the aid of a magnifying glass, and you'll see that it's made up of cells of variable shape and cylindrical vessels or ducts, sometimes alone, sometimes joined together; hence two main forms in the elementary parts of the plant: cellular tissue and vascular tissue. Cellular tissue is made up of contiguous cells that can be compared to the light foam of shaken soap water; depending on the obstacles they encounter, these cells change shape and communicate with each other through very small pores. To get a more or less accurate idea of vascular tissue, we need to imagine cells that are more or less (1) We will only give here the notions that are absolutely necessary for the understanding of the book. aligned and whose partitions have partly disappeared, thus establishing a direct, more or less continuous communication. If they are constricted from distance to distance, and cut by diaphragms (or partitions) pierced with holes, they are called rosary vessels; if they are cut by transverse slits, they are called false tracheae; the name tracheae is reserved for vessels formed by a membranous cylinder, in the interior of which is wound a spiral wire quite similar to the copper wire which forms the elastic of certain suspenders. Finally, the vessels themselves are short, non-porous tubes containing a juice particular to each plant. Some plants germinate in a day: watercress; others take years: Hazel, Dogwood, etc. Three external agents contribute to germination: water, heat and air. If there is perisperm, it softens and is absorbed by the embryo, which breaks its bonds and develops; if there is no perisperm, germination takes place much more quickly: Beans, Peas, etc. The radicular part elongates from top to bottom, towards the center of the earth; the young stem rises from bottom to top, towards the sky. The point where the root and stem meet is called the collar or vital node. The root serves to fix the plant in the earth and provide it with a large part of its nourishment; it never turns green from the action of air and light. Sometimes they float in water: Water Lentils; they attach themselves to the trunk or root of other trees: Ivy, Orchids, Orobanches; adventitious roots are the types of roots that detach themselves from the various points of the stem of some trees and shrubs, etc.: Mangrove, Maize, Magnolia. These supernumerary roots only develop in diameter when their tips reach the ground. The ability of the stems and even the leaves of certain plants to emit roots is widely used for cuttings and layering (see the articles on these two operations). If you cut a branch of willow or poplar and plunge it into the ground, you will see, after waiting a few days, its lower end produce rootlets. Considered as a whole, the root offers: 1° the body or middle part, varying in shape and consistency, more or less swollen: Carrot; 2° the collar or vital node, the demarcation point between stem and root, from which the annual stem bud emerges in perennial roots; 3° the rootlets or hair, more or less loose fibers, highly suitable for absorbing ambient fluids (V. for the different root species, the word root in the Vocabulary). The stem is that part of the plant which, growing in the opposite direction to the root, rises towards the sky to enjoy the sun and air, and to support leaves, flowers and fruit when the plant has them; it should not be confused either with the stump or rhizome, a kind of underground stem of certain perennial plants which runs in the soil and grows leaves and flowers from its front end as the rear end withers: Lily of the Valley, Iris Flambe. - nor with bulbs or onions, of rounded or conical shape, wrapped in scales or tunics, from which emerges a flowering branch, a true peduncle improperly called a stem: Porreau, Lis blanc; - nor finally with the tuber, short, swollen, usually rather irregular, without scales or tunics, a kind of outgrowth that arises from the roots of certain plants, offering on its surface buds called eyes: Pomme de terre. We'll only go into a little detail here on the trone and the stipe, two species of stem that we need to know well in order to understand one of the very distinctive characteristics between Dicotyledons and Monocotyledons. The trunk is conical and elongated, i.e. thicker at the base than at the top: 25. Oak, Ash, Fir, etc.; it is naked at the bottom, ending at the top with branches, twigs and thousands; it grows in length and thickness, increasing the layers of its circumference; it belongs to the dicotyledonous trees. The stipe is a kind of cylindrical column, as thick at the top as at the base, often even thicker in the middle than at either end, rarely branching and crowned with a bouquet of leaves and flowers: Palm trees, Dracœna, Yucca, etc. Its bark, when it has one, is quite indistinguishable from the stem; it increases in thickness by multiplying the threads of its circumference; its increase in height is achieved by the development of the bud that terminates it at the top. Cut crosswise, the trunk of dicotyledonous trees offers you, as has been so aptly described (1), a series of interlocking cases increasing in size from the center to the circumference: 1° At the center, a canal or medullary casing containing the pith; 2° Layers of tightly entwined fibers and vessels, all the harder and darker the closer they are to the center; these layers are called swan layers, and their softer part is called sapwood; the harder part is the wood itself; 3° The bark itself, made up of two layers, the inner or liber, a collection of thin sheets; the other outer, consisting of the epidermis, a thin, almost diaphanous layer pierced by a multitude of openings and covering all parts of the plant; the herbaceous envelope, a layer of cellular tissue, usually green, situated beneath the epidermis, and cor(1) Richard, Nouveaux éléments de botanique. the liber to which they are applied. Medullary rays extend from the pith to the circumference of the trunk, becoming wider and more active the closer they are to the bark; towards the center, they contain less starch and liquid juices. If you cut across the stipe of a Palm tree, all the parts examined above, very distinct in dicots, now seem to merge; the woody bundles blend into the pith; the bark is barely visible. Plants grow in two directions: as their diameter increases, so does their height. In dicotyledonous trees, diameter increases through the annual transformation of bast into sapwood, sapwood into wood, and successive renewal of bast. Growth in height is achieved by the young shoots of each year; the trunk is formed, as it were, of very long cones superimposed on one another; this kind of development is particularly noticeable in Pines and Firs. The stipe of monocotyledons grows very little in thickness, since instead of concentric layers, it forms superimposed rings; a Palm tree 40 meters high is often only a foot in diameter. Buds are organs that contain within them the rudiments of stems, branches, leaves and fruiting bodies; they always develop in the leaf axils or at the tips of twigs; scales covered with a viscous, resinous coating and a very fine down protect them from the rigors of the atmosphere. The flowering or fruiting bud contains one or more flowers without leaves; the leafing bud contains only leaves; the mixed bud contains both flowers and leaves. You'll recognize the first by its conical, swollen shape: apple trees, pear trees, cherry trees; the second, by its tapering, pointed, elongated shape: Bois-gentil; the third shares the shape of the others. Leaves have rightly been compared to the lungs of animals; they draw from the air the fluids necessary for plant growth; they combine air with sap and produce liquors analogous to our blood. Before their full development, they are enclosed in the buds. You'll find them variously arranged in relation to each other, but always in the same way in all plants of the same species, often of the same genus, sometimes even of an entire natural family. This arrangement, known as prefoliation, is a very good way of coordinating genera. - They can be folded lengthwise, half over half, i.e. their left lateral part is applied to the right so that their edges correspond perfectly on each side: Syringa; - they can be folded up and down several times over themselves: Aconit; - they can be folded over their length, so as to imitate the folds of a fan: Vine, Blackcurrant; - they can be rolled over themselves in the form of a spiral, as in certain Fig trees, Apricot, etc.; - their edges can be rolled up and down several times over themselves: Aconit; - they can be folded over their length, so as to imitate the folds of a fan: Vine, Blackcurrant; - they can be rolled over themselves in the form of a spiral, as in certain Fig trees, Apricot, etc.; - they can be folded over their length, so as to imitate the folds of a fan: Vine, Blackcurrant.their edges may be rolled inwards or outwards: Poplar, Pear; - finally, the leaves may be rolled in a crozier shape: Ferns. The leaf has two faces, one upper, the other lower; a base or point of attachment, an apex opposite the base; a contour or edge. Sometimes the leaf has no tail, in which case it's called a sessile leaf: poppy; sometimes it has one, known in botany as the petiole, hence the name petiolate leaf: lime, horse chestnut, etc. If the blade is aborted, the leaf is called phyllode: Acacia simple leaf of New Holland. On the underside of the leaf, the petiole is divided into projecting extensions called veins, almost always simple in monocotyledons, highly branched in dicotyledons. See Vocabulary for more details. Compound or articulated leaves, i.e. those whose leaflets are attached by articulation to the common petiole, show remarkable movement and irritability. Leguminosae, and Acacia in particular, rise as the sun rises over the horizon and fall as it disappears; the Sensitive is impressed by the slightest tremor, by the shadow of a cloud, by a gas, by a perfume, by a slight noise. Hedysarum gvrans has stipules that bend and twist on themselves, independently of each other. Dionea muscipula has two lobes at the tip of its leaves, lined with long, stiff yellowish cilia and trimmed with red glands: when an insect lands on them, the two lobes suddenly straighten up, move closer together and only reopen once they have smothered their enemy. Flower. - Fruit. Having briefly reviewed the parts that fix the plant to the ground, and those that supply it with the aqueous or aeriform fluids necessary for its nourishment and development, let's move on to the organs that renew and perpetuate the species. Plants remain in the place where they were born, where they must die, but they most often bear on the same individual the double apparatus of fertilization; hermaphroditism is as common among them as it is rare among animals. The flower is the culmination of vegetation on the stem or branch. It consists essentially of four whorls: calyx, corolla, stamens and carpels (ovary topped by style and stigma). Reduced to its last degree of simplicity, it can be formed by a single male or female sexual organ, i.e. a stamen or a pistil (willows); hence the name male flower or female flower, or hermaphrodite, the flower that combines the two sexual organs, male and female. If we take a complete flower, here's what we'll see when we examine it from center to circumference: the pistil or female sexual organ is always in the central part of the flower. It consists of the ovary, style and stigma. Further out are the male sexual organs, or stamens, usually more numerous than the pistils, each composed of a thread and anther. Outside the stamens are the two floral envelopes: the inner one, the corolla, called monopetal if it's made up of a single piece, or polypetal if it's made up of several pieces; - the outer one, the calyx, called monosetal or polysetal depending on whether it's made up of one or several pieces. The floral leaves and bracts do not belong to the flower itself. Sometimes the flower attaches itself immediately to the branches or twigs by its base: sessile flower; sometimes it uses an accessory part, a simple tail, in botany, peduncle: Carnation, pedunculate flower; a branched tail, pedicel, Vine, Lilac, pedicellate flower. Let's look at each part of the flower. Usually, there is only one pistil in a flower, as in the Lily, the Poppy, etc.; sometimes there are several, as in Ranunculus, the Rose, etc. The pistil or pistils are often attached to a particular extension of the receptacle called a gynophore, as in Strawberry and Raspberry. The ovary always occupies the lower part of the pistil; it may be simple or compound; when cut longitudinally or crosswise, it has one or more cavities called lodges, containing the rudiments of the seeds or ovules. It is in the interior of the ovary that the ovules are fertilized, develop and become seeds; this organ is therefore rightly compared to the uterus of animals. Most often, the ovary is free at the bottom of the flower, i.e. it does not adhere to the calyx (lime, lily); sometimes it is more or less fused to the base of the calyx, and only the top is free; in the first case, the ovary is said to be free or superior, in the second, adherent or inferior; two positions that provide valuable characters for grouping genera into natural families. V. Vocabulary. Whenever the ovary is inferior, the calyx is monosepal. The style is the more or less filiform extension of the apex of the ovary which supports the stigma; sometimes it is missing entirely (poppy, tulip), in which case the stigma is sessile. V. Vocabulary. The stigma is the apex of the style or of the ovary when the style is missing; it receives the pollen or fertilizing substance in its elongated utricles, united by a mucilaginous material; there are always as many stigmas as there are distinct styles or obvious divisions in the style. In the stamens, or male sexual organ, we note the upper part or anther, an essential part, a kind of small membranous sac; the pollen, a substance usually formed of small vesicular grains necessary for fertilization; the net, a thread-like appendage often absent from the stamens. The number of stamens varies greatly in different plants, and this is where Linnaeus got the idea for his classification system. He called flowers with a single stamen monandrous (Red Valerian); diandrous, flowers with two stamens (Lilac), etc., etc... Let's just note that the stamens can be all equal (lily, tulip); they can be unequal, i.e. some larger, others smaller in the same flower. Geraniums, for example, have ten stamens, five large and five smaller, arranged alternately. When a flower contains four stamens, two of which are always shorter, these stamens are called didynames (Linen, Calf's Club). When a flower contains six stamens, four of which are always larger than the other two, they are called tetradynamous (Radish). The stamens usually alternate with the corolla divisions when their number is equal to the corolla divisions (Borage); at other times, they are opposite the petals (Grapevine). The stamens of a flower are called definite when there are at most a dozen of them; indefinite when there are more. They are free or distinct (in Lilies); fused by the anthers (in Compositae); fused by the threads and then joined together in one, two, three or more distinct groups, each of which is called an adelphia; monadelphia, if there is only one group (Mallow), diadelphia, if there are two (Bean). In squash, the stamens are joined by both anthers and threads. The corolla is the inner, usually coloured, part of the perianth or double floral envelope; it immediately surrounds the organs of generation; it often contains a highly volatile oil which imparts a characteristic odour to the plant. Although the corolla is a real eye-catcher, thanks to the dazzling beauty with which it is often clothed and the grace of its cut-outs, in botanical terms it is of far less value than the simple pistil and stamen, which are sometimes barely visible. The corolla inserted around the ovary is called perigynous (Campanula); epigynous, when it is at the top of the ovary (Queen Margaret); hypogynous, if it is inserted under the ovary (Carnation). When these different parts are joined together, the FLOWER is said to be FRUIT. corolla is monopetal or gamopetal (Bindweed); polypetal, if these parts are separated (Rose). The first falls in one piece; the second lets its petals fall separately. In any petal, you will consider: 1° the tab or narrowed, more or less elongated lower part, by which it is attached; 2° the blade or enlarged part which surmounts the tab. A regular corolla is one in which all the incisions and divisions are equal, or whose parts appear to be arranged regularly around a common axis (yellow wallflower). An irregular corolla, on the other hand, is one whose incisions are unequal or whose parts do not appear to be arranged symmetrically around a fictitious common axis (Nasturtium, Snapdragon). For corolla modifications, see Vocabulary, under Corolla qualifiers. The single-petaled corolla has three distinct parts: 1° the tube, the lower part, usually cylindrical and tubuliform, more or less elongated; 2° the limbus, the upper part of the tube, more or less flared, sometimes spread out and even reflected; 3° the throat, the separation between the tube and the limbus. The calyx is the outermost envelope of the double perianth, or of the perianth itself when it is simple. The calyx usually has the consistency and herbaceous color of the bark it continues; sometimes it is colored (Fuchsia). See Vocabulary for the word calyx. Nectaries are the clusters of glands inside the flower that secrete the honey-like liquid so sought-after by bees. Inflorescence is the general arrangement of flowers on the stem and other supporting organs. If the flowers arise singly from different points on the stem, at varying distances from one another, they are said to be solitary (Tulip, Hundred-Leaf Rose). Located at the top of the stem (Tulip), they are called terminal; lateral, if they develop on the sides of the stem or branches; axillary, when they are born in the leaf axils (Periwinkle); geminate, if they are found two by two emerging from the same point on the stem (Viola biflore); ternate, when they emerge three by three from the same point on the stem (Teucrium flavum); fasciculate or bundled, if they emerge more than three by three together from the same point (Common cherry). Once fertilization has taken place, the floral envelopes wither and are destroyed, the stamens fall, and the stigma and style abandon the ovary, which concentrates all the flower's vital force within itself; then fructification takes place; the fruit is simply the fertilized and enlarged ovary. It then takes on the name of pericarp (envelope of the fruit); the ovule becomes the seed. You'll always notice three parts in the pericarp: 1° the epicarp, the thin outer membrane that covers the pericarp and determines its shape; 2° the endocarp, the inner membrane that lines the seminiferous cavity; 3° the sarcocarp, the fleshy part situated between the two preceding membranes, If the fruit comes from a single pistil, it's called single (Peach, Cherry); multiple, if it comes from several distinct pistils enclosed in the same flower (Strawberry, Clematis); compound, if it results from a more or less considerable number of pistils joined and often fused together, but belonging to distinct flowers (Blackberry, Pineapple, Pine cone). The thin, low-sugar pericarp produces dry fruit; the thick, succulent pericarp produces fleshy fruit (Apricots, Peaches, Melons). If fruits open with valves, they are said to be dehiscent; if they remain closed, they are called indehiscent. Depending on the number of seeds they contain, fruits are divided into oligosperms, or fruits containing few seeds, and polysperms, or fruits containing many seeds. The seed is to the plant what the egg is to the oviparous animal; it contains an organized body which, given the right conditions, will develop into a being perfectly similar to the one from which it originated. The seed has two parts to consider: 1° the episperm or integument itself; 2° the kernel contained within the episperm. The point at which the seed attaches to the pericarp is called the umbilicus or hilum, and you'll recognize it by a scar of varying size left by the seed on the surface of the seed coat. The center of the hilum always represents the base of the seed, whose apex is indicated by the point diametrically opposite the hilum. It is very important to consider the position and especially the direction of the seeds relative to the axis of the pericarp, when these seeds are in a given number; for, from this, we derive excellent characters for the coordination of families. When a seed is compressed, that of its two faces which faces the axis of the pericarp is called the face proper, the other, turned towards the walls of the pericarp, is called the back; the junction of the face and the back represents the edge of the seed. - When the hilum is on one of the points of the seed's edge, the seed is said to be compressed; on the contrary, it is said to be depressed if the hilum is on its front or back. The kernel is the whole part of a ripe, perfect seed contained in the episperm cavity; its essential character is that it contains an embryo, i.e. a body capable of reproducing a new plant of the same species. Sometimes the embryo forms the entire kernel (bean, lentil); sometimes an accessory body, the endosperm, is attached to the embryo (wheat, castor-oil). The two organs are so different in structure that a glance is enough to distinguish them. The endosperm, a more or less hard, soft or fleshy mass of cellular tissue, withers and diminishes in volume rather than acquiring it; it serves as food for the young embryo. Its presence or absence is a very good generic character, especially in Monocotyledones. The embryo is essentially made up of four parts: 1° the root body; 2° the cotyledonary body; 3° the gemmule; 4° the tigella. It is the root body or radicle which, through germination, gives rise to the root. Having already discussed monocotyledonous and dicotyledonous plants, we won't return to this subject. The gemmule, a small simple or compound body, is born between the cotyledons, or in the cavity of the cotyledon itself, if there is only one; it always forms the first bud, gemma, hence the name gemmule, sometimes wrongly replaced by plumule. The primordial leaves emerge from this bud. The tigella is not always clearly visible, because it often merges with the cotyledonary body and the radicle; sometimes, during germination, it lifts the cotyledons out of the ground, which in this position are called epigeous. SECTION TWO Methods. Three main systems have been used to study and classify plants, either in a systematic order, or according to a natural method. Tournefort's method (see table no. 1). - Tournefort established the 22 classes shown in the table below, and divided them into sections based on: 1° the shape, consistency and structure of the fruit, and even its economic uses; 2° the union or separation of the male or female organs; 3° the shape of the corollas; 4° the arrangement of the leaves. Q Linné's system. - This botanist divided plants into 24 classes (see table no. 2), and based his classification on the presence or absence of sexual organs. The first 10 classes are made up of plants whose flowers have from one to ten stamens; the 11th class includes flowers with twelve stamens; - the 12th class includes plants whose stamens, numbering twenty, are inserted on the calyx; - the 13th class includes plants whose flowers have more than twenty stamens attached to the receptacle. For these first thirteen classes, he bases orders on the number of pistils, and calls them monogyny (flower with a single pistil); digyny (flower with two pistils); polygyny (flower with more than two pistils). In the 14th class, he establishes two orders: gymnospermia, characterized by four naked seeds at the bottom of the calyx; angiospermia, characterized by a polysperm capsule. Tetradynamia, or the 15th class, is divided into siliqueuse, when the fruit is significantly longer than it is wide, and siliculeuse, when its length barely exceeds its width. He subdivides the 16th, 17th and 18th classes according to the number of stamens. Syngenesis or the 19th class contains six divisions: 1° equal polygamy: all florets and half-florets fertile and hermaphroditic; 2° superfluous polygamy: all florets and half-florets fertile, but not all hermaphroditic; 3° frustrated polygamy: center florets hermaphroditic and fertile, half-florets sterile; 4° necessary polygamy: sterile florets in the center, fertile florets around the circumference; 5° separate polygamy: each floret has its own calyx; 6° monogamy: this last order, like the previous five, no longer includes compound flowers, but only those which, without being part of the composites proper, nevertheless have stamens fused together by their anthers. 1 I Cats456 ~~~ METHODS. * The 20th, 21st and 22nd classes form their orders according to the number of stamens and pistils. Polygamy, or the 23rd class, has the following subdivisions: monoecy, when hermaphroditic and unisexual flowers are found on the same plant; dioecy, when hermaphroditic and unisexual flowers are found on different plants; trioecy, when male, female and hermaphroditic flowers are found on separate individuals. Finally, the 24th class, or cryptogamy, includes plants that have no embryos or cotyledons, and whose sexual organs cannot be seen because of their small size, location or shape: mosses, liverworts, ferns, mushrooms and algaes.
